A system whose verifiers died together is a broken loop and goes to Chapter 11. A private generator that fails any check goes to Chapter 12, whatever the country around it. Triage reads the present state of the loop; the verdict adds its direction; the six fields then have to justify both, and a case whose fields contradict its triage is misfiled, which is the one error the form catches on its own.

The order of reading is closed loops, partial loops, broken loops, market self-sclerosis, and the order is an argument. Closed loops teach the naming of live verifiers; partial loops, home to four of the six stress tests, say which leg holds and which is failing; broken loops show failure overdetermined. Market self-sclerosis comes last because read earlier it would be a hedge and read last it is a test: by then the reader has learned the six fields on states and watches them fill with private names, a verifier paid by the generator it judges at the rating agencies as at Gosplan's statistical office, exit killed by a property covenant as by an exit visa. State failure (P3) is shown before market failure (P2) because a dead verifier must be seen in a state before its private twin can be recognized, and the chapter that closes the atlas shows that the form does not know who owns the generator.

Six cases were chosen to kill the theory, and the bar for each is fixed here, before any case is read, so that the tally in Chapter 13 is checked against a text the cases could not have shaped. A bar set after the case is read is a verdict written by the generator.

India survives only if the two distinctions that resolve it, the noisy verifier and the veto point, were defined before India and not for it. The puzzle is a democracy that stagnated for four decades; the resolution is that a general election is a noisy verifier of licensing policy and that the License Raj was vetocracy, ex ante blocking with no ex post feedback. Vetocracy was defined in Chapter 4 on Californian material; the noisy verifier was defined in Chapter 3 eight chapters before the case, and its illustration was Indian, so the stricter test applies to it: a concept defined on one case must predict in others, and the tally in Chapter 13 checks whether it does. Had either first appeared inside the India case, the rescue would be an epicycle.

The Gulf survives only if the imported verifier does predictive work beyond the Gulf. Performance is real and the domestic verifiers look absent. Chapter 6's hypothesis is that global markets for capital, talent and reputation verify from outside. It is an insight if the same extension explains Greece, where Eurostat was the verifier that finally fired, Korea, where the export market was Amsden's discipline, and the accession states of the European Union, where conditionality did the verifying that no domestic institution yet could; it is an epicycle if it explains the Gulf alone. Oil is conceded as a residual either way.

Japan in the 1990s survives only if bindingness is shown to be a separate leg of the loop. Price, export and election/press were alive; banks declined to recognize losses, regulators forbore, and the bankruptcy verifier stood decorative (Caballero, Hoshi, and Kashyap, 2008). If a country with live verifiers can refuse consequences and rot, the third leg is independent and the case strengthens the theory. If the refusal was a verifier failure in disguise, the loop has two legs.

The Nordic trust objection survives only through within-Nordic variation on reform timelines. The test is whether Denmark, Sweden, and Norway, sharing the trust, diverged when their purchaser-provider splits, DRG funding and choice guarantees diverged, and whether Swedish waiting lists and Arbetsförmedlingen rot where a verifier is missing despite the trust. If loops decide direction inside the family, trust is a multiplier and is conceded as one; if trust alone predicts, the residual clause of Chapter 6 was too generous.

China from 1980 to 2010 survives only if benchmark plus export are accepted as sufficient verifiers with the press dead, and only if the deceleration after 2012 follows their decay. The promotion tournament that ranked provinces on growth (Li and Zhou, 2005) is a benchmark the ranked generator reports on itself, so Goodhart decay is predicted by the design, and its measured onset after 2008 (Chen, Chen, Hsieh, and Song, 2019) must precede the slowdown. The dead press is why the decay went uncorrected. A China that decelerated with its benchmark intact would refute the book.

The United States survives only if veto density, not verifier absence, predicts the sclerosis. Its verifiers are the strongest on paper anywhere: price, exit, election/press, benchmark across fifty states, bankruptcy. The claim is that ex ante blocking (CEQA, licensing, judicial review used to stop rather than to evaluate) has substituted for ex post verification, which is verification theater (Chapter 4). If sectors decayed in proportion to veto density while their verifiers stayed live, the distinction carries weight; if decay tracks verifier death instead, the distinction is decorative and the book is Olson with a vocabulary.

The form is still blank. Each case answers the three questions.

## Chapter 9. Closed Loops

The form is filled first for the systems that will look best on it, and the compliment the atlas pays them is the only one the book allows: their live verifiers are named, one by one, with the label each carries, and the soft spot each loop has is written into the fourth field rather than left out. Eight cases follow, from the country that verifies its own legislature twice to a city that lets a price settle what a planning commission settles elsewhere. None of them is closed by culture or by luck; each is closed by a component that can be pointed to, and each is given the one place where the component is starting to fail.

### Switzerland

**Verdict.** Closed loop, stable, with a slow ex ante creep at the edge: the country runs two verifiers on its own government that almost no other polity runs at all, and both fire after the output exists.

**Operated and structured.** The Swiss federal state operates little and structures nearly everything. It runs the army, the customs service, a railway holding, and a statistical office; the cantons run schools, police, hospitals, and tax collection; the municipalities run water, refuse, and the land registry. What the federal level structures is the machine that judges all three tiers: a constitution that submits every federal statute to a possible popular vote and every constitutional change to a mandatory one, and a fiscal order in which 26 cantons set their own tax rates in public view and a resident or a firm can move across a cantonal line at the cost of a commute. The system's own logic is that the people who pay for a law are the only verifier of it that cannot be lobbied in aggregate, and that a government small enough to leave keeps itself honest without having to be virtuous. Parliament, on that logic, drafts; the voter and the moving taxpayer judge; and the judgment arrives after the fact, cheaply, on four Sundays a year. Geography and scale are multipliers the lens does not claim (a canton the size of a county makes exit nearly free); the mechanism is the mechanism at any size.

**Live verifiers.** Referendum, in its ex post form: since 1848 the federation has held 677 votes, close to ten a year since 1991, and the optional referendum, which any 50,000 citizens can trigger within 100 days of a statute's passage, has let 58 percent of the challenged laws stand, whereas the popular initiative, which asks voters to generate constitutional text themselves, has passed 11 percent of the time (Le News, 2025; admin.ch). The spread between those two figures is the difference between a verifier and a generator: the electorate is good at judging what parliament made and reluctant to make law itself. The record of the last three years reads like an audit log. The electorate approved a thirteenth pension payment in March 2024 with 58.25 percent, rejected the occupational-pension reform in September 2024 with 32.87 percent in favor, rejected motorway expansion in November 2024, approved the E-ID Act in September 2025 by 50.39 percent, approved individual taxation in March 2026 with 54.26 percent, and rejected the population-cap initiative in June 2026 by 54.8 to 45.2 on 58 percent turnout (Wikipedia, 2024 to 2026; SWI swissinfo, 2026). Benchmark, in its fiscal form: the combined corporate rate runs from 11.85 percent in Zug to 20.54 in Bern, with Zurich at 19.61, and the top personal rate from 21.98 percent in Schwyz to 41.63 in Geneva (KPMG, 2025); every canton can read its neighbors' rates and its own net migration of firms and taxpayers, which is exit converted into a published number. Election/press is live and ordinary; the referendum makes it less necessary, because a government that can be overruled on a statute does not need to be removed for passing one. Bankruptcy fires even on public bodies: when the municipality of Leukerbad defaulted in 1998, the Federal Supreme Court held in 2003 that the canton of Valais was not liable for its debts (Feld and others, 2017), which made the soft constraint (Kornai's term) on every other municipality visibly harder. Price and export are the normal verifiers of a small open economy and need no special naming. The market-side captured verifier sits in the same country: for eight decades the banking secrecy statute of 1934 (Goldblum, 2026) disabled the foreign tax authority that was the only verifier of what Swiss banks sold to foreign clients, and neither the referendum nor the press fired, because the cost fell on taxpayers who did not vote in Switzerland; the verdict, when it came, was imported, in the form of the 2009 UBS settlement with the United States Department of Justice for USD 780 million (Department of Justice, 2009) and the automatic exchange of account information from 2018 (Goldblum, 2026). A verifier disabled by law and unmourned by the domestic loop is the same object as a rating agency paid by the issuer (Chapter 12).

**Bindingness.** The referendum binds absolutely: a statute that loses the vote is dead, and parliament's only recourse is to draft again, which is iteration, not override. The fiscal benchmark binds through the cantonal budget: a canton that loses firms to Zug loses revenue the federation only partly replaces, and the federal debt brake, adopted by referendum in 2001 with roughly 85 percent in favor (Avenir Suisse), binds federal spending to a rule that the federal parliament cannot suspend without the voters. The soft constraints are two. Fiscal equalization since 2008 (Federal Finance Administration, 2020) moves revenue from high-yield to low-yield cantons, which is a soft constraint on the benchmark's loser side, and the OECD minimum tax has begun to drift the low cantonal rates upward (KPMG, 2025), compressing the range over which the benchmark can select. The larger soft spot is procedural and named here because every closed-loop case has one: the pre-parliamentary consultation, the Vernehmlassung, in which the cantons, the parties, and the umbrella associations are invited to comment on a bill before the Federal Council submits it (Swiss Federal Assembly, 2017). The consultation exists because of the referendum: an association that can collect 50,000 signatures is consulted before generation so that it will not fire the verifier after it. That is rational for the generator and corrosive for the loop, since it converts an ex post verifier that lets iteration continue into an ex ante veto point that shapes what is generated at all, and a veto point, unlike a verifier, leaves no record of the laws it prevented. The initiative supplies the second ex ante channel: an initiative that passes writes into the constitution a block that operates before any project exists (the 2012 cap on second homes at 20 percent per municipality is the standard instance; Wikipedia, 2012), and the threat of one disciplines drafting the same way the consultation does. The distinction the case turns on is therefore explicit: the Swiss referendum is a verifier because it evaluates a completed statute and lets parliament try again; the consultation and the initiative are veto points to the extent that they block generation on a forecast, and their growth is the direction in which this loop would open.

**The component.** The referendum is ex post. Parliament generates a finished statute; the verifier evaluates the thing itself, not a document about it; the verdict binds; and iteration continues, since a rejected law returns in another draft and the motorway question will be asked again. That is the canonical sentence of Chapter 4 ("the Swiss referendum is ex post, CEQA is ex ante") carried by the country that supplies it, and it is why Switzerland is verified rather than merely well regulated: the oversight here produces a verdict, and the verdict selects. The cantonal benchmark completes the pair by attaching the market's own mechanism to government: 26 generators, a published rate, and a customer who can leave, which is competition guarding exit so that the verifier stays honest (Chapter 2), transplanted from firms to jurisdictions. The two together make the case an instance of the asymmetric law in its enabling half: live verifiers on the state itself do not guarantee good law (the same electorate withheld the federal franchise from women until 1971 and let the banks sell secrecy for eight decades; Wikipedia), but the Swiss loop has stayed closed for as long as both have run live, and the direction in which it would open is the one this case names, verifier turning into veto. (P1, P7)

**Prediction.** If the cantonal benchmark were destroyed by federal harmonization of tax rates (an initiative to that end was rejected in 2010; Wikipedia, 2010), or compressed to a spread under three points by the minimum tax and equalization together, expect intercantonal relocation of firms to fall toward zero and cantonal spending per head to converge upward within ten years, and expect the referendum alone to be unable to hold the line, because a verifier that fires once a federal statute exists cannot select among 26 cantonal budgets. If instead the consultation stage hardens into a formal precondition of drafting, expect the share of federal statutes challenged by referendum to fall over two legislative periods with no rise in the acceptance rate, which is the signature of a verifier being pre-empted rather than satisfied.

### Singapore

**Verdict.** Closed loop in every sector where the state operates or invests, stable, with one verifier decorative by design: the election/press pair does not verify the state's conduct of itself, and the system runs instead on export, benchmark, tender, and exit.

**Operated and structured.** The state operates housing, land, ports, the airline, the sovereign portfolios, the road-pricing system, and the pension parameter, and it structures the rest. The flats of the Housing and Development Board (HDB) housed 77.2 percent of resident households in 2025 (SingStat, 2025), which is the largest operational intervention in the closed-loop atlas. The Board operates the layer that can be verified cheaply: land assembly under compulsory acquisition powers dating from 1966 (Singapore Statutes Online), town planning, the allocation queue, and the pricing of new flats. It does not operate construction. Every HDB building contract is procured by open tender on the government's GeBIZ portal, and a bidder must be registered with the Building and Construction Authority in the workhead and financial grade the tender notice specifies (HDB, 2026); the builder is a private generator that wins on price, is paid on delivery, and loses the next job by failing this one. Temasek holds the state's commercial companies at arm's length and reports a net portfolio value, SGD 518 billion at 31 March 2026, with one-year, ten-year, and twenty-year total shareholder returns of 10.5, 7.1, and 6.8 percent (Temasek Review, 2026), against SGD 434 billion and 11.8, 5, and 7 percent a year earlier, a figure the fund has since restated at SGD 469 billion by moving its unlisted holdings from book value to its own marks (Temasek, 2025; Temasek, 2026). The system's own logic is that a city-state with no hinterland lives on what the world will pay for, so the state's task is to keep every domestic generator exposed to that verdict and to keep politics from softening it. On that logic one-party continuity is not the suppression of a verifier but the protection of the others from electoral softening, and the wage the state pays its ministers, pegged to a private-sector benchmark (Public Service Division), is the same logic applied to its own personnel. Scale and geography are multipliers (a port on the strait needs no industrial policy to find customers).

**Live verifiers.** Tender, in the HDB construction market: the Board is the client and never the builder, so the cost of a housing unit is set by competition among generators the Board does not own, and the Board's own output (units delivered, waiting time, price of a new flat) is a count. Price, in the resale market next door: a new HDB flat is sold below market, and the resale price of the same flat type in the same estate is a public number that verifies the subsidy, the location, and the build, with no ministry able to set it. Exit, in the household's choice among a new flat, a resale flat, and the private market, and in the grocery sector the state pointedly does not operate: the largest chain is a union-linked cooperative competing against listed private rivals (Wikipedia), and the hypothetical state grocer of Chapter 1 does not exist here because its price would be the state's own. Export, on the Temasek companies: the airline, the port operator, the shipyards, and the bank sell to customers who can buy elsewhere, and the losses are booked where they occur. The record contains visible losses, which is the point: Temasek reported a negative one-year return for the year to March 2023 and wrote off its USD 275 million stake in FTX with a cut to the compensation of the team responsible (CNBC, 2023); the container line was sold to a French carrier in 2016 (Ship Technology, 2016); the semiconductor foundry was sold in 2009 (EE Times, 2009); and the offshore and marine yard paid USD 422 million in 2017 to settle bribery charges brought by the United States, Brazil, and Singapore together (France 24, 2017), an imported verifier firing on a state-linked generator in public. Benchmark, on the state's own reporting: the published TSR series is the number the state chooses to be judged by, and it is comparable to any fund in the world. The captured and decorative verifier is election/press. The press operates under a licensing act that places management shares in approved hands (Wikipedia); the governing party has won every general election since 1959 and took 65.6 percent of the vote and 87 of 97 seats in May 2025 (Wikipedia, 2025). The election fires, the press reports its firing, and neither passes a verdict the state has not already priced in. This is stated as a classification, not as a charge: a decorative verifier is one whose bad verdict is not a possible event, and for the output "the conduct of the Singapore state by itself," no other verifier on the list applies. Congestion pricing and forced saving are signal interventions rather than verifiers, and both ride verifiers that were already running: the Area Licensing Scheme of June 1975 cut morning-peak entries to the center from 32,500 to 7,700, Electronic Road Pricing replaced it on 1 April 1998, and ERP 2.0 had on-board units in 930,000 vehicles, 93 percent of the fleet, by February 2026, with installation mandatory from 1 January 2027 and distance-based charging under study (Wikipedia, ALS; Malay Mail, 2026; LTA, 2026); CPF sets a contribution of 37 percent of wages, 17 from the employer and 20 from the employee, for workers under 55 from 1 January 2026 (CPF Board, 2026), a parameter on a savings market that still prices the savings.

**Bindingness.** The tender binds: a builder that overruns is not refinanced, and the Board's construction cost is what the last auction said it was. Export binds on the Temasek companies as long as the state lets them lose, and the record above shows that it has. The benchmark on Temasek binds less than it looks, because 50 percent of the portfolio is unlisted (Temasek Review, 2026), so half of the reported value is marked by the generator being judged, and the rise from the restated SGD 469 billion to 518 billion matches the reported return of 10.5 percent, but only on the fund's own marks; a benchmark the generator can mark is a benchmark that decays by the mechanism of Chapter 15, and the lens predicts that this is where a Singapore failure would first be hidden. The soft spot named for this case is the decorative election/press pair. Its consequence is not that HDB will build badly or the port will lose ships; those outputs have their own verifiers, and this is why the sector-granularity claim (Chapter 3) survives the case. Its consequence is that the one output the pair alone verifies, the state's use of its own discretion, has no verifier and no consequence, so that a bad verdict on it would arrive last, from outside, and at once. Veto points, for their part, are nearly absent: the compulsory acquisition power means no project is blocked ex ante on a forecast, and the same absence extends to the political veto on the state itself, which is the honest cost of the design.

**The component.** HDB is the developer-client and never the builder. The state sits on the generator's side of the loop only where its output is a count that a resident can check against a market next door, and it buys the layer whose productivity it could not verify, through a tender it runs as purchaser and cannot lobby. That is the operator rule as Chapter 3 derived it from the axis: a state may operate where verification is cheap and consequences still bind, and elsewhere it must structure loops, and Singapore's fidelity to the rule is visible in what it does not run. The same state that houses more than three quarters of its households does not sell them groceries, does not build its own flats, and does not shield its airline from foreign carriers; it operates at the bottom of the axis, tenders in the middle, and, at the top, where the output is its own judgment, has simply declined to install a verifier. (P4)

**Prediction.** If HDB internalized construction, replacing the tender with an in-house public works builder, expect unit cost to rise and completion times to lengthen within one build cycle of about five years, with the resale premium over new-flat prices widening as the subsidy absorbs the overrun. If instead the press verifier were restored while the tender stayed, expect no measurable change in HDB delivery within the same window, because HDB's verifiers were never the press, and expect the first effect to appear in the reconciliation of Temasek's unlisted valuations, where the decorative verifier is the only one missing.

### The Nordics

**Verdict.** Closed loop across Denmark, Norway, and Sweden, stable in the aggregate and decaying in the two Swedish sectors where the consequence has gone soft. The bar from Chapter 8: the case survives the trust objection only if within-Nordic variation on reform timelines shows the loop, not the culture, deciding direction.

**Operated and structured.** The three states operate hospitals, schools, and employment services on a scale no other closed-loop case matches, and they structure the operation with a verifier borrowed from the market: the purchaser is split from the provider, the money follows the patient or the pupil, and the patient may leave for a private provider at public expense when the public one fails a published deadline. Denmark pays its hospitals partly through DkDRG, a casemix system of roughly 1,000 groups with an annual tariff, used for inter-regional settlement and hospital-level funding (Sundhedsdatastyrelsen, 2026), the instrument Fetter and Thompson designed at Yale in the 1970s and Medicare adopted in 1983 (Chapter 14). Norway operates its oil through a fund whose capital it does not spend: the Government Pension Fund Global (GPFG) was worth NOK 22,683 billion at 30 June 2026 after a record krone return in the first half of the year (NBIM, 2026), and a spending rule adopted in 2001 limits the budget to the fund's expected real return, 4 percent then and 3 since 2017 (Norwegian Ministry of Finance). The system's own logic is Le Grand's other invisible hand: a universal, tax-funded service is the only one every voter will defend, and its quality can be held without rationing by letting the user choose the provider and letting the budget follow the choice. The state guarantees the entitlement and the timing; the provider, public or private, is verified by the choice and paid by the case. Trust is conceded in one clause, as a multiplier that lowers verification cost (a Danish tax return believed on submission is a cheaper benchmark than one presumed false).

**Live verifiers.** Exit, in the patient-choice guarantees, which is Hirschman's verifier attached to a consequence: Denmark's extended free hospital choice lets a patient who is not treated within 30 days go to a private hospital at the region's expense, a limit cut from two months in 2007 (OECD, 2020); Sweden's statutory vårdgaranti promises same-day primary-care contact, assessment within three days, a first specialist visit within 90 days, and treatment within 90 days (Socialstyrelsen; SKR). Benchmark, in three registers: the published regional waiting-time series in Sweden, the DRG tariffs that let a Danish region compare the cost of a hip in every hospital it pays, and the Norwegian fund's list of every holding in more than 7,200 companies, published twice a year with voting records and data back to 1998, which scored 100 out of 100 on the Global Pension Transparency Benchmark in October 2025 (NBIM; Top1000funds, 2025). Export, on the tradable sectors that pay for the rest: Danish pharmaceuticals and shipping, Swedish engineering, Norwegian petroleum sold at a price no Nordic ministry sets. Election/press, live and effective, with the exceptional property that the press reads the benchmarks: the waiting-time dashboard, the fund's half-year report, and the DRG accounts are news. Price, on the Norwegian fund itself, which holds listed equities marked daily by markets it cannot influence, so that its verifier is the incorruptible instance of Chapter 2 rather than a governance score. Tender, in Sweden's decision to contract job matching to private providers under fixed-plus-results payment from 2022, whose verdict is recorded below. The market-side symmetry sits inside the same hospitals: a private provider paid by DRG has the same incentive to code a case upward as a Chinese province had to report growth, and DRG creep by providers under activity funding is documented wherever the tariff is paid on the provider's own code, Norway included (Anthun and others, 2017), which is Goodhart decay running through a private generator against a public benchmark; Sweden's independent schools, paid per pupil and grading their own pupils, show the same decay in the form of grade inflation relative to national tests (Wennström, 2020).

**Bindingness.** The Danish exit binds because the consequence is automatic and lands on the budget of the region that failed: the patient leaves, the private hospital bills, and the region pays. The Norwegian fund rule binds because it is a number the press can check against the budget every October, and because the fund's assets are marked by the world rather than by the ministry. The Swedish soft spots are two, and both are consequences that went soft while the verifier kept firing. The vårdgaranti publishes its own failure: no region meets it, the share waiting more than 90 days for a first specialist contact runs from about 4 percent in Stockholm to 38 percent in Västerbotten and for treatment from about 1 percent in Stockholm to 48 percent in Örebro, and Stockholm's own share has drifted from about 4 to 5.5 percent by May 2026 (Vårdföretagarna, 2026, an industry association's reading of the regions' data; Socialstyrelsen says only that fewer were waiting in 2025). The verifier is live; the consequence is administered by the generator, since the region that missed the deadline is the body that must arrange care elsewhere, and a region with no private capacity within reach has nowhere to send anyone, so the deadline is a published verdict with a soft constraint (Kornai) attached. Arbetsförmedlingen is the second. The 2019 January Agreement contracted job matching to private providers under the Rusta och matcha model, national from 2022, and IFAU's effect evaluation of September 2020 to December 2022 found that participants assigned to private providers left unemployment no faster than those in the agency's own services, at higher cost, with results-based fees doing slightly better than flat ones (IFAU, 2024). That is the tender verifier firing correctly on a choice mechanism that lacked the exit behind it: an unemployed person assigned to a provider is not a customer who can leave, so the provider was paid for a case, not selected by one, and the verdict is that the loop was drawn with a verifier and no consequence at the point where the consequence would have done the work. The within-Nordic variation is the answer to the stress test. The same trust that supposedly carries the region of Stockholm carries Västerbotten, and the spread between them is nine-fold under one statute; the Swedish crisis of 1991 to 1993, with a banking collapse, a currency float in November 1992, and a deficit above 10 percent of GDP (Riksbank; Jonung, 2009), arrived in the highest-trust society then measured, and the performance since dates from the verifiers installed afterward, an expenditure ceiling and surplus target under the 1997 budget law, an independent Riksbank from 1999 (Bergström, 1999), and a notional defined-contribution pension legislated between 1994 and 1998, with an automatic balancing brake added in 2001 (Settergren, 2001), not from a change in how Swedes regard one another. Denmark's flexicurity settlement of 1994 (Wikipedia) made exit from a job cheap for the employer and the loss cheap for the worker, and paid for the second with a benefit conditioned on activation, which is a consequence on both sides. Norway's rule came in 2001, five years after the fund's first deposit in 1996 (Centre for Public Impact), and Norway's oil, like Venezuela's, was a multiplier on whatever loop it met. Three countries with one culture reformed at three dates, and in Sweden, the one with a crisis to date the change by, performance followed the verifiers rather than the culture; that is what the bar asked for. Veto points are few: the Nordic planning regimes block little ex ante, and the guarantees evaluate completed waiting rather than licensing a hospital before it opens.

**The component.** The money follows the patient across the public-private line, and where it does, the deadline binds; where the region both fails the deadline and administers the remedy, the deadline is a published number and nothing more. That is the asymmetric law with the necessary-condition logic of Chapter 6 exposed inside one culture: the live verifier (the published wait) enables but does not guarantee, and the difference between Stockholm and Västerbotten, between Denmark's automatic exit and Sweden's administered one, is the bindingness leg alone. Trust could raise every region's performance by a constant; it cannot produce a nine-fold spread under one law, and the reform timelines run the other way from the objection, since the trust was there before the verifiers and the performance came after them. (P7)

**Prediction.** Norway abolished its free-treatment-choice scheme, under which patients could take public funding to approved private providers, from January 2023 (NRK, 2022), destroying the private-exit half of its guarantee while Denmark kept its 30-day rule; if the lens is right, expect Norwegian specialist waiting times to lengthen relative to the Danish series within five years of the abolition, by 2028, and expect the Danish series to hold. If Sweden attached the Danish consequence to its own guarantee, an automatic referral at the region's expense that the failing region does not administer, expect the regional spread in the share waiting over 90 days to compress from nine-fold toward two-fold within five years; if the guarantee remains a deadline the regions police themselves, expect the spread to persist and Stockholm's share to keep drifting up.

### Germany

**Verdict.** Closed and stable where the state structures, decaying where it operates: the loop that runs German manufacturing through world prices and an uninstructable central bank has held for seven decades, while the two places the state took the generator's seat, its regional banks and its long-distance railway, run under soft constraints (Kornai) that its own founding doctrine was written to forbid.

**Operated and structured.** Ordoliberalism's best version is this: the state does not run the economy, it writes the constitution of the economy and then holds itself to it, because a competitive order is a public good that no participant will supply and that only the state can guarantee. Eucken's constituent principles (a functioning price system, monetary stability, open markets, private property, freedom of contract, liability, constancy of policy) and Röpke's insistence that the market needs a frame it cannot make for itself amount to an architect's brief: build the loop, then stay out of it (Eucken, 1952; Röpke, 1958). The distinction between Ordnungspolitik and Prozesspolitik, rules for the game against interventions in the play, is the nearest ancestor of the signal rule, and Haftung, the principle that whoever decides bears the loss, is bindingness written as a constitutional norm. What the Federal Republic structured is therefore nearly everything: firms, banks, wage bargains, and, since the currency reform of June 1948 lifted price controls (Bundesbank), the prices at which they meet. What it operates directly is a short list: the railway (Deutsche Bahn, wholly federally owned since the 1994 reform turned the Bundesbahn into a company (Deutsche Bahn, 2024)), the motorways, the universities, and, through the Länder rather than the federation, a tier of public banks (the Sparkassen and their wholesale arms, the Landesbanken) whose liabilities carried state guarantees. Ordoliberalism lacked the one thing the lens supplies: it wrote the rules and did not ask who verifies, cheaply and continuously, that the rules are kept (Chapter 7). Germany's answer to that question was institutional rather than doctrinal, and it carries the case.

**Live verifiers.** Price is live and was made hard to capture by a single act: the Bundesbank Act of 26 July 1957 created a central bank independent of government instruction (Bundesbank, 2025), which placed the steward of the price verifier outside the reach of the generators it verified. A central bank verifies fiscal and wage policy without a veto. The Bundesbank could not block a budget or a wage settlement; it priced them afterward. A finance minister who borrowed too much met a bond market that knew the Bundesbank would not monetize the debt; a wage round that settled above productivity met a central bank that would not print to accommodate it, so the excess arrived as unemployment in the bargaining sectors within a year or two rather than as inflation spread across everyone. The bargainers learned to negotiate against a verdict they could not instruct, and the discipline reached the ministries by the same road. On the one occasion a government overrode the verdict outright, the one-to-one conversion of East German marks in 1990 against the Bundesbank's public advice, the consequence arrived anyway: the bank tightened through 1991 and 1992 and the tightening broke the European exchange-rate mechanism (NBER, 2021; LSE, 2003). Since 1999 the verifier sits in Frankfurt under another name; the European Central Bank inherited the Bundesbank's statute but not its focus, since a verdict on the euro area as a whole is a noisier verifier of a German wage round than a verdict on the mark was (Chapter 3). Export is the second live verifier and the one that grades the Mittelstand: a machine-tool maker in Baden-Württemberg is verified by a buyer in Ohio or Guangdong who can choose Japanese instead, and the grade cannot be lobbied in Berlin (Amsden, 1989; Studwell, 2013). Exit is live in product markets and, for firms, in the world capital market. Election/press is live and uncaptured. Benchmark is live but muted: sixteen Länder publish comparable figures, and the fiscal equalization that transfers between them blunts the consequence of ranking last. Bankruptcy is live for the Mittelstand, which is what Haftung means in practice, and was decorative for the Landesbanken. Tender is live in one place that matters below. Referendum is absent at the federal level by design after 1933; Germany verifies through institutions, not plebiscites. The residuals (the postwar reset Olson described, the apprenticeship system, a location at the center of the continent) multiplied a loop that was closed; they did not close it (Chapter 6).

**Bindingness.** The consequences bind where the ordoliberal rule is applied to others and soften where the state is the generator, and both soft spots in Germany's closed loop have that shape. The first is the Landesbanken. State-guaranteed wholesale banks with no retail franchise and no verified business model borrowed cheaply on the guarantee and bought what the guarantee let them buy: SachsenLB's off-balance conduits (Ormond Quay) failed in August 2007 and the bank passed to LBBW in 2008 under a EUR 2.75 billion guarantee from Saxony; WestLB took a EUR 5 billion guarantee in February 2008, moved EUR 85 billion of assets into a public wind-down vehicle in November 2009, and ceased to exist as Portigon on 30 June 2012 (Hellwig, 2018; European Commission, 2009). Hellwig's tally of the taxpayer's bill runs to roughly EUR 18 billion for WestLB, EUR 16 billion for HSH Nordbank, EUR 10 billion for BayernLB, EUR 5 billion for LBBW, and at least EUR 1.5 billion for SachsenLB (Hellwig, 2018). This is Kornai's soft constraint in a capitalist federation: the Länder could not credibly refuse to cover losses, the banks' creditors knew it, and the bankruptcy verifier was decorative from the first loan. The symmetry on the market side is exact, because two private banks bought the same paper through the same conduits and got the same rescue: HRE cost about EUR 14 billion and IKB about EUR 9.6 billion, taking the total above EUR 70 billion (Hellwig, 2018). Private ownership did not harden the constraint; the expectation of rescue softened it under both ownerships, which is the book's claim in one balance sheet. The second soft spot is Deutsche Bahn. Train punctuality is a cheap, low-axis output (Chapter 3), so the verifier fires continuously: long-distance punctuality (arrivals less than six minutes late) was 62.5 percent in 2024 and 60.1 percent in 2025 (LOK Report, 2026). The verdict is public, exact, and reproduced by every passenger on every platform; what it lacks is a consequence. No budget falls, no manager loses a post, no competitor takes the slot, and the federal owner responds by voting money. Regional trains ran at 88.7 percent in the same year (LOK Report, 2026), and the gap is not only that regional runs are shorter: regional services are bought by the Länder through competitive tenders in which DB Regio must bid against private operators, an arrangement dating from the regionalization of 1996 (Deutsche Bahn, 2024), so the tender verifier attaches a consequence that the long-distance monopoly never faces. The same company, verified by the same clock, performs differently exactly where its output is tendered. The debt brake belongs here too. It was a rule, an ex ante ceiling on borrowing, not a verifier, and when it bound the outgoing Bundestag rewrote it in a week: on 18 March 2025 a two-thirds majority exempted defense spending above 1 percent of GDP, created a EUR 500 billion special fund for infrastructure and climate over twelve years, and gave the Länder a collective 0.35 percent of GDP in structural borrowing room, with the Bundesrat concurring on 21 March by 53 of 69 votes (Noerr, 2025; CNBC, 2025). A rule that binds only until it binds is a soft constraint with a constitutional citation. The verdict on the resulting debt will arrive through bond prices, as it always has; the brake's authors had forgotten that this was the verifier all along.

**The component.** The Bundesbank Act of 1957 placed the steward of the price verifier outside the instruction of every generator it verified, and the ordoliberal state then intervened in the economy it had structured almost entirely through that one channel, an interest rate the loop already read. Wage bargainers, finance ministers, and Landesbank boards all negotiated against a number none of them could set, which is the thinnest instrument any state has governed with, and the two sectors where Germany reached past it to operate directly are the two where its own principle of liability was suspended (P5).

**Prediction.** If exit is restored on the long-distance network, meaning open-access operators running full timetables on the main corridors with slot allocation the incumbent cannot veto, expect long-distance punctuality to climb above 75 percent within five years of the first competitor's full timetable; if the published figure continues to carry no consequence, expect it to stay below 70 percent through 2030 regardless of how much of the EUR 500 billion fund is spent on track.

### New Zealand

**Verdict.** Closed and stable since a reset in 1984 rebuilt the fiscal and monetary loops from the ground, with one verifier whose mandate the generator's principal has rewritten twice in five years, which is the soft spot and the reason the trajectory is stable rather than strengthening.

**Operated and structured.** In July 1984 a snap election, a run on the currency, and a Treasury that had spent years drafting its critique of the Muldoon economy produced Olson's reset without a war (Te Ara). The wage and price freeze, the import licensing, the agricultural subsidies, the Think Big energy projects, and the fixed exchange rate went in a package between 1984 and 1993 that a Labour government began and a National government finished (Reserve Bank of New Zealand, 1996). The reset cleared the coalitions; it did not build verifiers (Chapter 5), and what distinguishes New Zealand from the other liberalizations of that decade is that it then built three. The steel-man of the design is this: a small, open, unicameral democracy cannot afford to run on discretion, because every discretionary instrument becomes a lobby's instrument within one electoral cycle, so the reformers converted discretion into published contracts whose breach anyone could read. The state would say in advance what it intended, in numbers, and be held to the numbers by a governor, a Treasury, and a press that could not be told to look away. What the state operates was cut back to what a contract could describe: the State-Owned Enterprises Act of 1986 turned trading departments into companies with boards, commercial objectives, and published accounts, and several were then sold (Reserve Bank of New Zealand, 1996); the Public Finance Act of 1989 moved the budget to accrual accounting and appropriated money for outputs rather than for departments (Treasury, 2015). What the state structures is the rest, through the instruments described next.

**Live verifiers.** Price is live and its steward is the verifier the world copied. The Reserve Bank of New Zealand Act 1989 made price stability the bank's statutory objective and created the Policy Targets Agreement, a signed contract between the finance minister and the governor stating the target as a number; the first, in March 1990, required inflation of 0 to 2 percent by December 1992, and the band became 1 to 3 percent in 2002 with an explicit 2 percent midpoint from 2012 (Reserve Bank of New Zealand; LSE). Inflation targeting was invented here, and the invention was not the number but the attachment: the governor signed personally and could be removed for failing the target (McDermott and Williams, 2018). The Act was a verifier, not a veto. The governor blocked nothing; a budget passed and a wage settled and then met a rate. The government kept a power to override the target, but only by a public instrument laid before Parliament (McDermott and Williams, 2018), so capturing the verifier was possible and expensive: it cost a headline, which routed the capture through the election/press verifier. The second instrument is the Fiscal Responsibility Act 1994, which contained no numerical rule at all. It required each government to state its fiscal intentions against principles (prudent debt, an operating balance over time) and to publish a Budget Policy Statement, a Fiscal Strategy Report, and a pre-election economic and fiscal update, and in 2004 it was folded into the Public Finance Act as Part 2 with a four-yearly long-term fiscal statement added (Treasury, 2015). A rule is a veto on a number; the Fiscal Responsibility Act was a verifier of a promise, and the pre-election update is its sharpest edge, since it forces the Treasury to publish the books weeks before the electorate votes on the people who kept them. Election/press is therefore live and doubly loaded, because two statutes deliberately route their consequences through it. Export is live for a country that sells milk, meat, and timber into world prices it cannot move. Exit is live at the scale of a nation whose citizens hold a standing right to live and work in Australia, so a bad decade is verified by departure figures the government publishes itself (Wikipedia). Bankruptcy is live, and the SOE reform made it reachable for state companies by giving them balance sheets that could fail. Benchmark is thin: there are no states or cantons to compare, and the substitute is comparison with Australia, which the press performs weekly. Tender is live inside the output-appropriation system. Referendum exists but is in general non-binding, so it verifies opinion rather than statute (New Zealand Legislation). The residuals (a small population, distance, the trust a five-million-person society sustains) are multipliers, and the proof that they did not close the loop is the same country before 1984.

**Bindingness.** The consequences bind on the governor, the Treasury, and the state companies, and the soft spot is that the verifier's own mandate turned out to be soft. In 2018 the Act was amended to add "maximum sustainable employment" as a co-objective and to replace the governor's personal agreement with a Monetary Policy Committee Remit from 1 April 2019; the Reserve Bank of New Zealand (Economic Objective) Amendment Act 2023, in force 20 December 2023, removed the employment objective and restored the single price-stability mandate, employment surviving as a matter the bank must "have regard to" (Reserve Bank of New Zealand; Treasury, 2024). Two rewrites in five years, one per change of government. The damage is not that either mandate is wrong; it is that a verifier whose target is rewritten by the party in power is a verifier whose independence is a matter of the next election, and a bargainer who knows the target can move negotiates against the next government rather than against the number. A dual mandate also degrades the verifier on its own terms, since employment sits higher on the axis than the price level (Chapter 3): it is harder to attribute, slower to measure, and easier to argue about, so the second objective handed the generator's principal a lever on the verdict that the first did not. The precise verdict on the soft spot is that the 1989 design attached a personal consequence to a single cheap metric, that 2018 removed both halves, the signature and the single metric, and that 2023 restored the metric but not the signature. The same softening appears on the market side in the same country. When the finance-company sector failed between 2006 and 2010, the Crown's retail deposit guarantee of October 2008 turned the bankruptcy verifier off for private lenders, and the collapse of South Canterbury Finance in 2010 landed on the taxpayer's account at roughly NZD 1.6 billion (Office of the Auditor-General, 2011); Air New Zealand, privatized in 1989, was recapitalized by the state in 2001 after its Ansett acquisition failed (NZ Herald, 2001). A private generator whose creditors expect the Crown to pay is under Kornai's soft constraint as surely as a Landesbank, and the state that had hardened its own budget could not, when the moment came, refuse to soften someone else's.

**The component.** The Policy Targets Agreement put a signature on the verifier: one governor, one published number, and a personal consequence for missing it, which is the thinnest instrument a state can intervene with, an interest rate, attached to the strongest bindingness a state can offer, a named person's job. The reset of 1984 made the design possible by clearing the coalitions that would have vetoed it, and the design then did what a reset cannot, which is to install a verifier on the cleared ground (P5).

**Prediction.** If the single price-stability mandate survives the next two changes of government without amendment, expect two-year-ahead inflation expectations to stay inside the 1 to 3 percent band through the next full tightening cycle; if a dual mandate is reinstated, expect expectations to breach the band within that cycle and the subsequent tightening to run deeper than one under the single mandate would have, with roughly five years to the verdict.

### Estonia

**Verdict.** Closed and closing further: the state that collapsed the cost of verifying its own administration from a queue to a query is still extending the collapse, and its two limits are a residual it cannot change and a pathology it has not yet met.

**Operated and structured.** The steel-man is a thesis about cost. A small state that regained independence in 1991 with no money and no bureaucracy worth inheriting could not verify its administration the way rich states do, with inspectors, audits, and paper, so it built the verification into the plumbing: if every exchange of data between institutions runs through one logged layer, the record of who did what to whom exists as a byproduct of doing it, and the citizen can be handed the log. Estonia operates what any state operates, schools, courts, police, registers, and structures the rest of a small open economy with as few instruments as it could find: a flat income tax introduced in 1994 (Law Library of Congress), no tax on retained corporate profit (Tax Foundation), a currency board and then the euro from January 2011 (European Commission), and a balanced-budget habit that kept public debt among the lowest in the European Union (ERR News). The flat tax is a thin signal in the sense the signal rule means: one rate every earner can compute is one no lobby can carve, and the thinness is itself a verifier, since a deduction that does not exist cannot be captured. What the state built that other states did not is X-Road, the open-source data-exchange layer in use since 2001 (X-Road.global), through which more than 3,000 services, some 52,000 organizations as indirect users, and about 2.2 billion transactions a year now pass, and which has been deployed in more than twenty countries (e-Estonia, 2026). The government's figure that 99 percent of public services are online is a marketing claim rather than a statistical one and should be read as such (e-Estonia, 2026); the mechanism does not depend on it.

**Live verifiers.** Election/press is the verifier the digital state is built on, and it runs in an unusual direction, from the single citizen upward: what the citizen reads in her own log reaches a consequence through a complaint, the prosecutor, and the press. The Data Tracker (andmejälgija), delivered through the eesti.ee portal over X-Road, shows a citizen which institution accessed which of their personal data and when; adoption is register by register, and the state information authority RIA has proposed making it mandatory for every public database (RIA, 2026; ERR News, 2025). A verifier is an evaluation external to the generator, cheap enough to run continuously, and hard to capture (Chapter 1). The tracker satisfies all three for the output hardest to verify in any administrative state, the conduct of officials toward records: it is external (the reader is the person the record concerns), continuous (the log is written at the moment of access), and hard to capture (an official cannot edit the citizen's copy of the log). It is ex post, and the distinction from a veto is the point: the alternative design, a committee approving each data exchange in advance, would have carried the cost of oversight and none of the feedback (Chapter 4). Radical statistical and fiscal transparency feeds the same verifier: open data on the budget and the registers let the press and the opposition check the government against its own statements, and the imported instances, Eurostat's fiscal surveillance since accession in 2004 and the euro's entry criteria, supplied a benchmark Estonia was too small to build alone (Chapter 6). In its ordinary form election/press is live as well, and the press is free. Price is live. Export is live for an economy that sells to Finland, Sweden, and Germany at world prices. Exit is the loudest verifier in the country: Helsinki is some eighty kilometers across the water (Wikipedia), and a decade in which wages or services fall behind is verified by the ferry manifest before any election reads it. Bankruptcy is live and applies to firms, including the e-residency companies, of which more than 39,000 have been founded by some 135,000 e-residents from 185 countries since late 2014, yielding a record EUR 125 million of direct state revenue in 2025 (e-Residency, 2026). Tender is live in procurement. Referendum is rare and was used for accession in 2003 (Wikipedia).

**Bindingness.** The consequences bind on officials, because the tracker turns an access into a fact a citizen can bring to a prosecutor, and on the treasury, because the imported fiscal verifier attaches a market consequence to a small state's debt. The soft spots are two, and the second matters more. The first is the residual: small scale is not a verifier, and Estonia's coherence owes something to an administrative elite that fits in a room, which the lens must acknowledge and cannot explain. The second is a limit inside the design. Digital legibility is verification, and a cheap verifier can select for what it measures and destroy what it was meant to protect; this is Scott's warning, carried in full in Chapter 16, and Estonia is the case where it applies to a state that did nothing wrong. "Share of services online" measures the interface, not the service: a benefit claimed in three clicks is verified as processed, not as adequate, and a school whose records are flawless on X-Road is not thereby a good school, because school quality sits high on the axis where no log reaches (Chapter 3). The e-residency figure verifies that the state collected EUR 125 million; it does not verify that the companies behind it produce anything, and a metric of that kind is exactly what Goodhart decay consumes once someone is rewarded for it. The pathology has not arrived, and the tracker is the reason it may not: a verifier that watches the officials rather than the citizens is the one cheap metric that cannot be gamed by the people it reports on. The market symmetry sits inside the same borders. Between 2007 and 2015 the Estonian branch of Danske Bank passed roughly EUR 200 billion of non-resident flows that its own compliance function did not verify (Euronews, 2018), in the most legible administrative state in Europe, because a private bank's ledger was not on X-Road and its verifier was a department the generator paid. Legibility stops at the perimeter of the loop that installed it; the state's records were transparent, the bank's were not, and the failure landed on the side the state did not verify.

**The component.** X-Road collapsed the cost of verifying administrative conduct from an audit to a log, and the Data Tracker then handed the log to the one reader an official cannot instruct, the citizen whose record it is. Bureaucracy grows where output cannot be verified, and Estonia is the case of a state that lit the dark before complexity could grow in it; the honest limit is that a log verifies conduct, not outcomes, and the line between the two is the axis (P6).

**Prediction.** If RIA's proposal makes the Data Tracker mandatory for every public database, expect within three years of full coverage a measurable fall in record accesses with no case file attached and at least one prosecution a year originating in a citizen's own tracker complaint; if coverage stalls at the registers that volunteered, expect the next Estonian data-misuse case to originate in an untracked register within the same three years.

### The Netherlands

**Verdict.** Closed and stable in the two sectors the country is known for, water and healthcare, and opening in one, land use, where a court's verdict of 2019 was answered with a veto point instead of a verifier, and the housing shortage is the running bill.

**Operated and structured.** The Dutch state operates where the output is dry land and structures where the output is health, and the split is the operator rule drawn on a map. The 21 water authorities (waterschappen) are functional governments with one mandate: keep the water out and keep it clean, on a defined territory, funded by their own tax on the residents and owners they protect, governed by boards elected every four years, most recently on 15 March 2023 alongside the provincial elections (Kiesraad, 2023; Government.nl). They build and maintain dikes, pumps, sluices, and treatment plants, and nothing else; the oldest, Rijnland, dates its charter to 1255 (Wikipedia), and the count has fallen from about 3,500 boards in the mid-nineteenth century to 21 by merger (Wikipedia), the mandate unchanged. The steel-man of the design is that a polity whose existence depends on a single physical output should give that output its own government, taxed and elected by the parties with the most to lose, and keep it out of the general budget, where a dike competes with a hospital for money in a year when neither has failed yet. Seven centuries of continuous operation below sea level is the argument in its own favor, and no general-purpose legislature has an equivalent record on any physical output. Healthcare is the opposite arrangement. Since the Health Insurance Act (Zorgverzekeringswet) took effect on 1 January 2006, every resident must buy a basic policy from a competing private insurer, insurers must accept every applicant at a community-rated premium, enrollment reopens every year, and a risk-equalization fund pays insurers more for sicker enrollees so that the profitable strategy is managing care rather than selecting patients; the design descends from the 1987 Dekker committee and from Enthoven's managed competition (NCBI Bookshelf; Health Affairs, 2008). The state writes the basic package, sets the deductible, collects the income-related contribution, and runs the equalization formula; it owns no insurer and, in the main, no hospital. Insurers and providers generate; the state structures.

**Live verifiers.** In water, the verdict comes from the flood, which is none of the eight but the outcome that two of them, benchmark and election/press, are built to read, and it is the cheapest and most binding outcome in this atlas. Nobody has to be paid to notice that a polder is wet; no board can lobby the North Sea; the verdict arrives in hours and cannot be appealed. The board's design turns that verdict into a benchmark and an election/press verifier before it is allowed to fire in earnest: dikes are inspected against statutory safety norms on a fixed cycle of at most twelve years (Compendium voor de Leefomgeving, 2024) and the results are published by segment, so a failing section is a named object with a named board responsible for it, and the board's electorate lives behind it. The 1953 flood is the case where that outcome arrived at full force, killing more than 1,800 people (Zeeuws Archief), and the consequence was the Delta Works, a program whose success is verifiable to the meter and which has been verified every storm season since. Exit is absent by design: one cannot leave a water board, which is why the board is elected and taxes only those it protects, the interest-pay-say principle (belang-betaling-zeggenschap; Unie van Waterschappen, 2019) that ties the voter, the payer, and the beneficiary into one person. In healthcare, the live verifiers are price, exit, benchmark, and bankruptcy. The nominal premium is public and compared on every comparison site; roughly one in fifteen policyholders switches insurer in a typical open-enrollment window (NL Times, 2025), enough exit to discipline the premium. Insurers negotiate prices with hospitals in the freely negotiable segment (Commonwealth Fund, 2025) and contract selectively, so the hospital faces a purchaser with an alternative. Published quality benchmarks, scheduled by the National Health Care Institute and drawn in surgery from the registries of the Dutch Institute for Clinical Auditing (Zorginstituut Nederland, 2026; Annals of Surgery, 2020), are the medium-cost verifier the axis predicts for surgical quality. And bankruptcy is live on the provider side: two hospitals, MC Slotervaart in Amsterdam and the IJsselmeer hospitals in Lelystad, went bankrupt in October 2018 and were not rescued as going concerns (NL Times, 2018). Election/press verifies the whole and is noisy at the sector level.

**Bindingness.** Consequences bind in water because the board that fails an inspection must raise its own tax to fix the segment and face its own voters afterward; there is no ministry to transfer the cost to, and the ministry could not cancel the flood if there were. In healthcare, the constraint is hard for hospitals, harder than in most public systems, and a soft constraint (Kornai) in one predictable place: the state's premium subsidy (zorgtoeslag; Commonwealth Fund, 2025) absorbs part of any premium rise for lower-income households, which mutes the price verifier at the bottom of the distribution, and the four largest insurer groups hold most of the market (Commonwealth Fund, 2025), so exit selects among a small set. The soft spot that matters is neither of these. It is nitrogen. On 29 May 2019 the Administrative Jurisdiction Division of the Council of State ruled that the national nitrogen program (Programma Aanpak Stikstof, PAS) was incompatible with the Habitats Directive and could not be used to grant permits, stalling thousands of projects; on 2 November 2022 it struck the construction exemption that had been enacted to route around the ruling; on 18 December 2024 it tightened the rules for internal offsetting; on 11 March 2026 it annulled the A27/A12 road decision at Amelisweerd, and a hearing on dairy and Schiphol permits is set for 27 October 2026 (Raad van State, 2019 to 2026). The ruling itself was verification in the exact sense of Chapter 4: an independent court evaluated a completed program, ex post, against a binding standard, and found that the program permitted deposition today against reductions promised for tomorrow. What followed was veto substitution. Instead of installing a verifier the loop could read (an instrument of the kind Chapter 14 describes for carbon), the state left every project to prove, individually and in advance, that its nitrogen contribution was nil or offset, and the freeze became a permit regime. The generators had asked for the PAS: the dairy sector and the construction sector both lobbied for a program that let them build on a promise, and a distributional coalition (Olson) writing its own verdict is a market failure of the same shape as a ministry writing its own. The bill is the housing shortage, a deficit counted in the hundreds of thousands of units (ABF Research via NOS, 2025), in a country whose builders, planners, and buyers all have live price, exit, and bankruptcy verifiers pointed at them and cannot generate because a veto sits in front of the first spade. The verifier survived; the loop opened at the consequence, because the consequence of a bad nitrogen verdict is not a smaller project or a dearer one but no project at all, which is not a verdict on output but the suspension of output. Geography is a residual the lens does not claim, and the board and the freeze operate on the same soil.

**The component.** The water board is a state operator with a single verifiable output, and its seven-century record is the operator rule working at the floor of the verification-cost axis: the verifier is the world, the verdict is binary and public, the board taxes the people who read it and is elected by them, and the same state, when it moved to a sector where quality is medium-cost to verify, did not operate but built a managed-competition loop with price, exit, benchmark, and bankruptcy attached, and where it faced an output nobody could cheaply verify (deposition avoided by a project not yet built) it substituted a veto for a verifier and stopped generating (P4).

**Prediction.** If the per-project nitrogen assessment is replaced by an instrument the loop can read ex post, such as a deposition price of the kind Chapter 14 describes for carbon, verified against measured deposition, expect housing completions to return to their pre-2019 pace within three years of the change; if the assessment stays in place, expect the shortage to widen through 2030 and the ministry to answer it with operational targets for units that the veto prevents it from delivering.

### Houston

**Verdict.** Closed and stable, a municipal loop in which the price of housing verifies the land-use regime continuously and the regime lets the price bind, with one blind spot, flood risk, that the price does not count because a federal instrument was built to keep it from counting.

**Operated and structured.** The city operates roads, drainage, water, and permitting, and structures nearly everything else by declining to zone. Houston's voters rejected zoning by referendum in 1948, 1962, and 1993 (Mercatus, 2020; Works in Progress), so no map assigns uses to parcels; a warehouse may sit beside a townhouse row beside a church, and what sits where is decided by whoever pays for the land. The city does regulate, through setbacks, parking minimums, and above all minimum lot sizes, and its two decisive reforms were reductions in those. In 1998 it cut the minimum single-family lot inside Loop 610 from 5,000 square feet to 3,500 as of right, and to as little as 1,400 under conditions, a reduction of up to 72 percent; in 2013 it extended the reform citywide and lowered the threshold at which a neighborhood may opt out from 60 to 55 percent of owners; between 1999 and 2016 the city recorded 25,269 new residential parcels under 5,000 square feet, most of them two or three townhouses replacing one detached house (Mercatus, 2020; HUD Cityscape; Market Urbanism, 2024). The steel-man is Hayek's argument applied to dirt: the highest-valued use of a parcel is knowledge of time and place that no planning commission holds, and a city that lets buyers and builders discover it, then lets the next buyer overrule them, will house more people at lower cost than one that forecasts the use and forbids the rest. The second half of the steel-man is that private ordering fills the gap: subdivisions write deed restrictions, owners who want a single-family street can buy one, and nobody has to impose the preference on the whole city to have it on their block.

**Live verifiers.** Price is the verifier and it runs every day. Redfin's median sale price over the three months to June 2026 was USD 357,805 in Houston, up 2.2 percent on the year, and USD 1,699,075 in San Francisco, up 15.2 percent (Redfin, 2026). The two cities sit under the same constitution, the same federal environmental statute, the same federal flood-insurance program, the same tax code, and the same interest rate; what differs is state and municipal law governing one sector, land use, and the difference in that sector's price is a factor of nearly five. This is the sector-granularity delta of Chapter 3 read at municipal scale, and it is the within-geography method of Acemoglu and Robinson's Nogales applied to a sector rather than a regime. Exit is live and cheap: a household priced out of one Houston neighborhood moves to the next, a builder refused by one lot buys another, and the city has no discretionary review at which a neighbor's objection converts into delay. Benchmark is the verifier the case is built on: Houston against San Francisco, and Houston against Dallas and Austin, which zone and are dearer (Redfin, 2026), is a comparison anyone can run from a listings site. Election/press is noisy on land use, as everywhere; the referendum was not: the electorate verified the zoning question directly, ex post on a drafted ordinance, three times, and each time the verdict was no. The permitting side of the contrast is asserted more than measured. San Francisco's median of 627 days to a full multifamily building permit (San Francisco Chronicle, 2023) is the other half of the pair, recorded in Chapter 4; a February 2026 analysis of 1.4 million permits puts San Francisco's issuance median at 209 days against 62 in New York, measuring a later stage (Prevesta, 2026). Houston's speed is a reputation. The city launched a 30-business-day residential permit pilot on 3 July 2025 without publishing a baseline (City of Houston, 2025), so the one number it could have offered, the current time, was not offered: a small verification failure inside the fast city.

**Bindingness.** Consequences bind because the price is allowed to move and the builder is allowed to respond to it. A townhouse that does not sell at cost is a loss for its builder, not for the city; a lot that is worth more as three units than as one is redeveloped, and the owner who sold it read that verdict off the offer. The soft spot is flood exposure, and it should be named without the consolation that zoning would have fixed it. In August 2017 Hurricane Harvey dropped on the order of 50 inches of rain on parts of the metropolitan area (Kinder Institute, 2017) and flooded tens of thousands of homes (Kinder Institute, 2017), many outside the mapped floodplain and some inside the design pool of the Addicks and Barker reservoirs (Houston Chronicle, 2018). The lens reads the event through Chapter 2's caveat: price verifies that someone will pay, and not an inch further; it does not verify that the costs to anyone outside the transaction were counted, and it cannot count a cost that has been deliberately taken off the invoice. The National Flood Insurance Program, a federal instrument identical in Houston and San Francisco, priced flood risk below its actuarial cost for decades (Government Accountability Office, 2023), so the one dimension of a Houston house that the price should have punished, its elevation relative to the bayou, was the one dimension the price could not see; the 2021 repricing under the program's revised rating, Risk Rating 2.0 (FEMA, 2021) is the belated restoration of that signal. Zoned cities flood too, and Houston's unpriced floodplain is a federal signal failure, not a municipal one, but the limit of no-zoning is real: the regime verifies what the buyer can perceive, and a buyer cannot perceive a detention pond that was never dug upstream. The second soft spot is the market-side one. Deed restrictions are private covenants, written by subdividers, enforced by owners and, unusually, by the city attorney under state law (City of Houston Legal Department), and on the land they cover they do everything a zoning code does, including fixing a minimum lot the 1998 and 2013 ordinances would otherwise have lowered; the 55 percent neighborhood opt-out is the public version of the same instrument. A covenant that prevents a parcel from being redeveloped to its highest-valued use is exit killed by contract, the same shape as the Canadian grocers' property covenants that kept rival stores off the plaza next door (Chapter 12), and the same shape at a smaller scale as CEQA: a private ex ante block on generation that no price can overrule. Houston's loop is closed in the aggregate because the covenants cover parts of the city and not the whole, so the uncovered parcels carry the adjustment. Geography, a flat coastal plain with no ocean on three sides, is a residual the lens does not claim; it lowers the cost of building out, not the cost of building up, and the townhouse count measures the second.

**The component.** The zoning referendums were ex post verdicts on drafted ordinances and the minimum-lot reforms of 1998 and 2013 were reductions in the one ex ante rule the city did impose, so that a house in Houston is verified by the offer on it and not by the forecast of a commission; the price system does the work Chapter 2 assigns it, distributed, continuous, and incorruptible by any neighbor, and the city's contribution was to leave exit intact so that the verifier stayed honest, which is the proposition that markets are verification machines tested at the scale of a parcel (P1).

**Prediction.** If Houston restores a citywide 5,000 square foot minimum lot, by ordinance or by covenants spreading to most of the inner loop, expect the creation of small-lot parcels to fall by more than half within two years and the Houston median to detach upward from the Texas metropolitan average within five; if the regime holds and the federal flood premium continues to converge on actuarial cost, expect the price of floodplain lots to fall relative to elevated ones within the same five years, which would be the first time the Houston price counted the bayou.

## Chapter 10. Partial Loops

Houston's last prediction ended on a price that had never once counted a bayou, which is what a live verifier looks like from the inside: precise about what it reads and blind to what it does not. The eleven systems in this chapter are built of such verifiers, and each is missing one leg or has one leg failing, and the interest of the middle is that the failing leg can be named. Four of the six stress tests live here, and each restates its bar from Chapter 8 before the verdict is allowed to stand.

### The United States

**Verdict.** Partial loop, decaying: the verifiers are live in more columns than in any other case in this atlas, and the output they would judge is increasingly blocked before it reaches them. The bar from Chapter 8: the case survives only if veto density, not verifier absence, predicts the sclerosis, since a country with the strongest formal verifiers ever written down and an accelerating sclerosis would otherwise refute the asymmetric law on its home ground.

**Operated and structured.** The government of 1788 was designed to operate almost nothing and to structure everything, and the design document says so in this book's vocabulary. Madison's premise is that declarations fail, that the "exterior provisions" of a paper separation of powers are "found to be inadequate," so the remedy must be interior: "Ambition must be made to counteract ambition." Each department is a generator whose output is judged by a rival with a standing appetite for finding fault, and the electorate is named as "the primary control" with "auxiliary precautions" conceded because it is too slow and too aggregated to select on any single output, which is the noisy verifier of Chapter 3 named two centuries early. Federalism in the same paper is the benchmark design Chapter 5 sets out: fifty generators of the same output type under published accounts, with citizens and capital free to move between them. The steel-man of everything added since is in the same paper. Madison's second consideration is "to guard one part of the society against the injustice of the other part," and every veto point accreted over two centuries can claim descent from that sentence: a review any neighbor can trigger, a license any board can withhold, an injunction any plaintiff can seek are, on their own account, the multiplication of sentinels in a society too large and various for any one of them to be trusted.

What the American state operates today is narrow and mostly verifiable: defense, courts, a pension and a health payer that write checks against published rules, and at the municipal level schools, roads, water, and permits. What it structures is nearly everything else: a continental market in which it sets no price, a banking system it insures rather than runs, a health sector private in ownership through most of its length. The drift is therefore not from structuring toward operating; the American state did not become Gosplan. It is a drift inside the structuring role, from verifiers toward veto points, from judging what was built to reviewing what might be. CEQA and NEPA, both enacted in 1970, each require that a document forecasting a project's effects be produced and defended before the project exists, and each is enforced by whoever chooses to sue (Wikipedia; Planning and Conservation League). Occupational licensing covers roughly a fifth to a quarter of American workers, 21.6 percent on the narrow BLS measure for 2025 and 29 percent in Kleiner and Krueger's survey, up from about 4.5 percent after the Second World War (BLS, 2025; Kleiner and Krueger, 2013; IZA World of Labor): an ex ante credential, examined once, in place of the ex post verdicts (exit, tort, the second visit) that already exist for nearly every licensed trade. The judiciary, which Chapter 4 showed can be either, has been drawn toward the veto side: the preliminary injunction that halts a project until its paperwork is adequate issues from the same bench that judges a completed act, at the end of the axis where the object must be imagined rather than examined. The result is legible in the one verifier the veto cannot block: under the same constitution, federal statutes, and interest rate, a Houston house sells for about a fifth of a San Francisco one (the Houston case, Chapter 9), and of the differences between the two cities the lens claims only veto density; geography and demand are residuals.

**Live verifiers.** Price is live and deeper than anywhere else: a continental market with no administered goods prices, and the deepest capital markets in the world pricing every listed generator daily; scale and geography are multipliers on that verifier the lens does not claim to explain.
