Exit is live between jurisdictions and between firms; the moving van from California to Texas is exit delivering its verdict, and the Houston price is that verdict written down. Benchmark is live by design: fifty states with published budgets, tax rates, test scores, and permit times, the laboratories Brandeis named (New State Ice Co. v. Liebmann, 1932) and Madison specified. Election/press is live and binding: governments lose and leave, and a press the state does not own reports what the state would suppress; the capture mode Levinson and Pildes described in 2006 (Harvard Law Review, 2006), parties spanning the branches so that the branches stop checking one another, has degraded the interior verifier Madison built without touching the exterior one. Bankruptcy is live for firms and for municipalities, and decorative for the largest banks, since 2008 established that the rescue would come. Referendum exists in roughly half the states (Ballotpedia), but its American form is mostly the initiative, which generates law rather than verifying it; its ex post instance is the Houston zoning vote. Export verifies little, since the tradeable share of output is small; tender is live in procurement and buried under a rule thicket of its own. Two verifiers are named captured, both private, because the case would be dishonest without them. The rating agencies before 2008 were verifiers paid by the generators they judged; Moody's settled for nearly USD 864 million in January 2017 with admissions that it had departed from its own methodologies and failed to manage the issuer-pays conflict, S&P for USD 1.375 billion in 2015, and the issuer-pays model remains dominant (DOJ, 2017; JURIST, 2015; SEC, 2024). And American health care has removed the patient as verifier by contract: out-of-pocket spending was 11 percent of USD 5.3 trillion in 2024 (CMS, 2024 data), three quarters of reviewed hospitals posted price files no patient could read (PatientRightsAdvocate, 2025), and the administrative share was 34.2 percent of spending in 2017 on the broadest estimate against 17 percent in Canada, with narrower definitions giving 8 to 15 percent (Himmelstein, Campbell, and Woolhandler, 2020). Those are the two private loops that opened beside the world's deepest price verifier, and Chapter 12 carries both; the market side decays by the same two mechanisms as the state side, a captured verifier and a stack of intermediaries that blocks the verdict before it can be passed.

**Bindingness.** Consequences bind at the bottom of the axis and soften as it climbs, which is the ordinary profile of a partial loop. A firm that loses its customers is liquidated and nobody rescues it; a city that loses its residents loses its tax base and, in Detroit's case in 2013 (NPR, 2013), its solvency; a government that loses an election leaves. The soft constraints (Kornai's term) sit where the output cannot be verified and the generator is large. The largest banks learned in 2008 that their bankruptcy verifier was decorative, and the compliance stack that grew afterward is the veto substituted for the verdict (Chapter 4; Chapter 15). The health payer stack imposes bankruptcy on the patient and cancels it for the institution. The federal budget has no bankruptcy verifier, and its bond market is a price verifier with the largest reserve in the world behind it, which defers the verdict rather than cancelling it (Chapter 6). And the veto point itself is the softest constraint in the system, because it bears no consequence in either direction: a good project blocked costs the blocker nothing, a bad project blocked earns no credit anyone can audit, and the plaintiff who never cared about the environment is never charged with the building that does not exist. The system has begun to say so about itself, which is the evidence that the diagnosis is not imported. The Fiscal Responsibility Act of June 2023 capped environmental impact statements at 150 pages, 300 for the most complex, with two-year deadlines (Bipartisan Policy Center, 2023). On 29 May 2025 the Supreme Court held 8 to 0 in Seven County Infrastructure Coalition v. Eagle County that courts owe agencies substantial deference on NEPA documents and that an agency need not analyze the effects of separate projects outside its control (Supreme Court of the United States, 2025), a court declining the veto role and reserving itself for the act. On 30 June 2025 California exempted infill housing on sites of up to 20 acres from CEQA outright and confined the review of a project that misses an exemption on one criterion to that criterion alone (Holland & Knight, 2025; Allen Matkins, 2025). None of the three improved the review. Each removed output from its reach, which is what a legislature or a court does when it has concluded that a process yields cost without verdict, and Chapter 5's corollary applies to the first: a page limit on a document whose length was the symptom is simplification without a verifier, and the pages will migrate into appendices unless something verifies the project that gets built.

**The component.** The founders built verifiers and the descendants built veto points on top of them, and the sclerosis tracks the second, not the first. Nothing in Federalist 51 asks whether a department's work is good, only whether it exceeds its place, and the auxiliary precautions it specified, bicameralism, the presidential negative, and the judicial review that followed, are blocks on generation as much as evaluations of it; the descendants took the blocking half of Madison's design and extended it, by statute, credential, and injunction, to every output a neighbor could describe as harmful, while the verifying half, price, exit, benchmark, bankruptcy, election/press, was left standing and in most columns deepened. That is why the American profile is neither Japan's (verifiers live, consequences cancelled) nor India's (verifiers noisy, the Raj a pure veto): it is verifiers live and binding, and a generation increasingly prevented from producing the output they would judge. The distinction of Chapter 4 carries the weight the bar demanded, and P6 explains why the accretion is not reversible by simplification alone: each veto point is a review whose output is a document, a document's adequacy has no verifier, so the review grows in the dark and regrows there when cut. The case survives its bar on one checkable condition: where veto density falls, output must respond, because if it does not, something other than the veto was the predictor (P6).

**Prediction.** If the veto points removed in 2025 stay removed (the CEQA infill exemption in California, substantial deference on NEPA documents federally), so that price and exit pass verdicts on output that now reaches them, expect permit timelines and multifamily starts in California's large cities to diverge measurably from the national trend within five years, with the San Francisco multifamily permit median falling well below the 627 days the Chronicle measured (San Francisco Chronicle, 2023); if the review migrates into other ex ante instruments (design review, discretionary review, litigation under other statutes) and the timeline holds, the veto reading of this case is refuted at the exact margin the reform touched, and the sclerosis needs a predictor the lens has not supplied.

### France

**Verdict.** Partial loop, stable: closed and performing wherever the output is measured in megawatts and minutes, open and repeatedly failing wherever the output is a strategy, with the boundary running through a single ministry culture rather than between ministries and markets.

**Operated and structured.** France operates more than any other rich democracy in this atlas, and it operates the same way in every sector: a corps of state engineers plans, a state company executes, and the treasury stands behind the balance sheet. The steel-man is the fleet. Engineers selected by the hardest examinations in the country, insulated from the quarterly verdict, can plan on a horizon no private financier will fund, and the proof is a grid that produced 547.5 terawatt-hours in 2025, 373.0 of them nuclear, 95.2 percent of it low-carbon, with record net exports of 92.3 terawatt-hours (RTE, 2026), built by a state that decided in 1974, under the Messmer plan, to standardize one licensed design and repeat it some fifty times in two decades (Wikipedia, History of France's civil nuclear program). The same corps, under the same ministries, with the same treasury behind it, operated Plan Calcul, Concorde, and the national champions of the 1970s and 1980s, and lost every one of them. The state is therefore not the variable; it did not change between the reactor hall and the computer plant. What changed is what the output was and who could pass a verdict on it. A reactor built in series is verified against the last reactor: its cost, its schedule, its availability factor, each a number the next unit either beats or does not. A high-speed train is verified by a clock: the Autorité de régulation des transports counts trains five minutes or more late, and 25 percent of domestic high-speed services were late in 2025, up 0.9 points on 2024 (ART, 2026), a statistic every passenger on the platform reproduces without the regulator's help. A national computer industry is verified by nothing until the world market for computers has voted, and Plan Calcul, approved in July 1966 after General Electric's takeover of Bull and American export denials, produced CII in December 1966, a European consortium that shipped in 1974, and an abandonment in 1975, with CII folded into Honeywell-Bull the following year after more than USD 100 million had been spent between 1967 and 1971 (Gaston-Breton, undated; Wikipedia, Plan Calcul). Concorde entered commercial service on 21 January 1976, fourteen aircraft flew, the development cost was estimated at around USD 1.6 billion at the time, and the type retired in 2003 (AFP via phys.org, 2019); the only two airlines that ever bought it were owned by the two states that built it (Heritage Concorde), so the export verifier, the one that counted, returned zero orders and was answered with the states' own purchase. Flamanville 3 is the modern instance and the honest one, because the output was a reactor, which the same state had verified fifty times before. Construction began in 2007 on a plan of EUR 3.3 billion and 54 months; EDF's estimate in December 2022 was EUR 13.2 billion, the Cour des comptes' full-cost estimate in January 2025 EUR 23.7 billion, and the reactor reached full power on 17 December 2025 (ANS Nuclear Newswire, 2025; Cour des comptes, 2025). The fleet's verifiers had been the series and the export market for reactors, which Framatome had once served in South Africa, South Korea, and China (World Nuclear Association); by 2007 the last French unit had been connected eight years earlier (Bloomberg, 2024), the world market for new reactors had contracted to a handful of orders, and the EPR was a first-of-a-kind with no last unit to beat and no buyer it could lose. The verifier that had made the fleet cheap no longer existed, and the same corps that had built to a benchmark built to a document, at seven times the plan.

**Live verifiers.** Price is live on the fleet's output and partly administered on its sale: European wholesale electricity is priced hourly across interconnectors, and every exported terawatt-hour is sold at a price no French ministry sets, while the domestic regulated-access regime that obliged EDF to sell a share of nuclear output to rivals at a fixed price (ARENH, ended on 31 December 2025; Enerdata) was a signal intervention laid over the verifier rather than a cancellation of it. Export is live and binding on the fleet, and it fired on the train as well: the TGV's technology was sold abroad, to Korea and Spain (Wikipedia, KTX-I and Renfe Class 100), which is the verdict Concorde never received. Benchmark is live wherever the state built in series: unit against unit for the reactors, fleet against fleet on availability factor, and the ART's published lateness count for the train. Election/press is live, and its most useful French instrument is the Cour des comptes, whose reports are public and whose verdicts on Flamanville and on the EDF buyout are the evidence used here. Tender is live inside the operator: EDF procures civil works and components from private contractors, Bouygues for Flamanville's civil works (Bouygues Construction), which is the structure of Singapore's Housing and Development Board (HDB) applied to a reactor hall. Exit is nearly absent for the passenger and has just appeared: the first foreign operator on the Paris to Lyon line, Trenitalia, entered in December 2021 (Railway Gazette, 2021), the only exit verifier the TGV has faced in four decades. Bankruptcy is decorative for every state company in the case, and that is the column in which the French loop is open. The veto point in this case is the one Chapter 4 calls legitimate, the licensing of a reactor before it is fueled because the ex post verdict is a meltdown, and its cost is honest only where it stays at the core: how much of Flamanville's overrun is that licensing and how much is procedural accretion around it is a question the Cour des comptes could not separate, which is the edge widening as Chapter 4 says it does.

**Bindingness.** The consequence binds on the physical output and softens on the balance sheet. A reactor that trips is counted; an exported terawatt-hour is paid for at the world's price; a late train is late on the regulator's sheet and the passenger's watch, though, as the Germany case (Chapter 9) shows, a lateness count binds only where a competitor or a tender can take the slot, and on the TGV that began in December 2021. The soft constraint (Kornai's term) begins where the number ends. Plan Calcul's losses were covered for nine years before the market's verdict was allowed to stand, Concorde's for twenty-seven, and the champions of the 1990s were refinanced on the same expectation, Crédit Lyonnais most expensively (Institutional Investor). The purest recent instance is the renationalization of EDF: the state already held 83.7 percent, completed a takeover bid and squeeze-out on 8 June 2023 at a cost of EUR 9.7 billion, and was told afterward by the Cour des comptes that the price was relatively high and the purchase unnecessary for control (EDF, 2023; Cour des comptes via energynews.pro). The remaining 16 percent was the last price verifier on EDF's equity, a daily verdict on the company's exposure to Flamanville and to the fleet's corrosion outages, and the state paid EUR 9.7 billion to stop reading it in the year the Flamanville cost was being fixed. The market side has the same shape and the opposite consequence. Westinghouse, whose licensed design the French fleet was built from, was bankrupted in March 2017 by cost overruns on its own first-of-a-kind reactors in Georgia and South Carolina, Vogtle and V.C. Summer (Utility Dive, 2017); the output type was identical, the overrun was of the same order, and the private generator was removed by the verifier the French state had cancelled for EDF. Ownership decided who paid for the overrun, not whether it occurred, because at that height on the axis nobody verifies a first-of-a-kind reactor until it is built.

**The component.** Same state, same corps of engineers, opposite outcomes, sorted by verification cost. The operator rule (P4) says a state may operate where verification is cheap and consequences still bind, and France ran the rule's experiment without intending to: megawatts exported and minutes late are cheap to verify, external to the ministry, and continuous, and the first of them bound, so the fleet is among the best-performing state operations in the world; a computer industry's future and an airliner's market are near-infinite ex ante, the only verifier that existed was the world market, and the state overrode it until the money ran out. A nation-level theory has to call France one thing, dirigiste or not, and cannot explain why the same kind of polytechnicien succeeded in one building and failed in the next; the axis predicts, inside one ministry culture, exactly where the state's hand holds and where it slips, which is the sector-granularity delta of Chapter 3 read off a single country. Flamanville 3 is the rule's confirmation, not its exception: the reactor moved up the axis when its series and its export market disappeared, and the state's performance moved with it (P4).

**Prediction.** If the EPR2 program, six units announced in 2022 (World Nuclear Association), restores the serial benchmark, with each unit's cost and schedule published against the previous unit's and civil works tendered outside EDF, expect the third unit to come in below the first on both measures by the time it is connected, on the current EPR2 schedule the late 2030s (Nuclear Engineering International, 2025); if each unit is licensed, financed, and audited as a one-off under a sole state owner that absorbs its overruns as it absorbed Flamanville's, expect no unit to cost less than its predecessor, and the Cour des comptes to say so within two years of the second unit's first concrete.

### Japan

**Verdict.** Partial loop: closed from the 1950s to the late 1980s, opened at the consequence leg from 1990, decaying for a decade, stabilizing after 2002 (Financial Services Agency), and since 2024 attempting to close again through the price of money. The bar from Chapter 8: this case survives only if bindingness is shown to be a separate leg, meaning a country whose verifiers were live refused the consequence and rotted anyway; if the lost decade can be explained by a dead or captured verifier instead, the third leg is a corollary of the second and the loop has two components, not three.

**Operated and structured.** The postwar state operated little and structured almost everything. The Ministry of International Trade and Industry allocated foreign exchange, licensed technology imports, and steered credit through the main-bank system, while leaving the firms in private hands and the verdict on their output to buyers in Detroit and Düsseldorf. The steel-man is the developmental state's own logic, and it is strong: a late industrializer cannot let a thin, scarcity-distorted domestic price system choose which industries to build, so a competent ministry picks the sectors and lets world markets pick the firms, and support that is conditional on foreign sales is not protection but a wager the ministry can lose (Amsden, 1989; Studwell, 2013). The wager was verified continuously. A favored generator had to sell abroad to keep its credit; the sectors that could not (the ministry's own ledger of retreats runs from aluminum smelting to the failed consolidation of the car industry; Pacific Affairs, 1983; Henderson, 1993) were merged, shrunk, or dropped. Export was the incorruptible verifier because no ministry could lobby a foreign customer, and the miracle was the loop closing on it. What the state structured for finance was different in kind. The Ministry of Finance ran the convoy system: banks moved at the speed of the slowest, deposit rates were administered, entry was closed, and the ministry's promise, never written down, was that no bank would be allowed to fail. That promise is the whole case, because a promise that no generator will fail is a soft constraint (Kornai) stated as policy.

**Live verifiers.** Nearly the full set was on paper and most of it fired. Price: live in goods, administered in credit until the liberalization of the 1980s (Reserve Bank of Australia, 2000). Export: live and incorruptible throughout, the verifier that built the country and never stopped working; Toyota was not spared by the lost decade, it was measured by the same buyers as before. Election/press: live; the Liberal Democratic Party lost power in 1993 for the first time since 1955 (Britannica), and the press reported the bad loans in detail for a decade; the electorate, informed, re-elected forbearance. Exit: live for consumers, disabled for capital, since cross-shareholdings and the main-bank relationship meant a lender could not walk away from a borrower whose equity it held and whose board it staffed. Benchmark: weak, because prefectures spend central money and are compared on little. Referendum: absent. Bankruptcy: present on paper (corporate reorganization statutes, courts, examiners) and switched off in practice for banks and their large borrowers. Bankruptcy is therefore the decorative verifier of the case: a court can be asked to rule on insolvency only if someone files, and a bank that refuses to classify a loan as impaired never files. The distinction between verifier and veto point matters here in the negative: Japan's stagnation was not vetocracy. Nothing blocked generation ex ante; construction firms kept building, developers kept borrowing, factories kept running. The verdict on all that generation was simply withheld.

**Bindingness.** The consequence leg failed alone, and it failed in the private sector first. After land and equity prices collapsed from 1990 (Wikipedia, Japanese asset price bubble), banks holding loans against collateral now worth a fraction of book faced a choice between recognizing losses, which would breach capital ratios and invite the ministry's attention, and rolling the loans over at concessional rates so that the borrower could keep paying interest and the loan could stay classified as performing. They chose the second, and the Ministry of Finance, whose examiners knew, chose to let them; regulatory forbearance is the state's name for agreeing not to read a verdict already in hand. Caballero, Hoshi, and Kashyap measured the result by identifying zombies as firms receiving credit below a benchmark built from prime rates: the share of listed firms so classified hovered between 5 and 15 percent until 1993 and then rose sharply, exceeding 25 percent in every year after 1994; in manufacturing the index rose from an average of 3.11 percent for 1981 to 1993 to 9.58 percent for 1996 to 2002, and in construction from 4.47 to 20.35 percent (Caballero, Hoshi, and Kashyap, 2008). Their central finding is not the waste inside the dead firms but congestion: in industries where zombies were prevalent, healthy firms invested less and created fewer jobs, so the withheld verdict taxed the generators that would have passed it. The mechanism is Kornai's soft constraint without socialism. The banks expected the ministry to cover them, the ministry expected the Diet, the Diet expected the taxpayer, and every expectation was correct. Kornai, Maskin, and Roland extended the concept to too-big-to-fail banking in capitalist economies (2003), and Japan is the canonical instance: a chain of private lenders and private borrowers, each acting rationally on the expectation that the verdict would be cancelled, and a state whose only contribution was to confirm the expectation. The refusal to verify losses was a market failure committed by private managers protecting private careers, and the convoy was its complement. The same shape recurred in the United States after 2008, and the Bank for International Settlements later found that across fourteen advanced economies the zombie share rose from about 2 percent in the late 1980s to some 12 percent by 2016 (BIS, 2018), which is what a soft constraint at the price layer looks like when rates fall to zero everywhere: when money is free, any borrower can service any debt, and the interest rate stops selecting. The soft spot closed only when the bankruptcy verifier was switched back on by an agency the banks could not lobby: the Financial Services Agency, separated from the ministry in 1998 (Wikipedia, Financial Services Agency), and the Takenaka program of 2002 (Financial Services Agency, 2002), which forced loss recognition against the banks' own valuations and ended the decade. Aging and a shrinking labor force are residuals the lens does not claim; they lowered the ceiling, they did not withhold the verdict.

**The component.** The consequence leg failed by itself, with the verifiers alive. Price reported the losses; export kept ranking the exporters; the press printed the loan books; the electorate had its chance in 1993 and after. None of that produced a single binding consequence for a bank or a large borrower for most of a decade, because the verdict of every live verifier was routed to a bankruptcy verifier that had been turned off, and a verdict that changes no allocation is not a consequence. This is why Japan strengthens rather than survives the theory. Kornai's leg is not a corollary of verification; a system can verify perfectly and refuse to act, and when it does, it rots on the same schedule and with the same symptoms (congestion, misallocation, a productivity gap that widens with each year of forbearance) as a system that never verified at all. The asymmetric law reads Japan exactly: live verifiers enabled the miracle and could not prevent the decay, because a dead consequence is as fatal as a dead verifier (P7).

**Prediction.** The Bank of Japan ended negative rates and yield-curve control on 19 March 2024, its first increase in seventeen years (CNBC, 2024), raised the policy rate in steps to 1.0 percent on 16 June 2026, the highest since 1995 (CNBC, 2026), and held there on 31 July 2026 while warning that core inflation would overshoot 2 percent from September (FocusEconomics, 2026). Read through the loop, this is an attempted restoration of price as a verifier of solvency: at 1 percent an interest coverage test means something again, and a borrower who cannot meet it generates a verdict that someone must either act on or cancel. The prediction follows the required form. If the Bank holds the policy rate at or above 1 percent through 2029 and the Financial Services Agency does not reinstate forbearance on classification, expect corporate bankruptcies to rise and the zombie share, on the Caballero, Hoshi, and Kashyap criterion, to fall back inside its 1981 to 1993 band within five years, with no lasting fall in aggregate employment as the released workers are absorbed by the healthy firms the zombies had crowded; if instead the Bank retreats toward zero at the first wave of failures, expect the share to stay above its 1994 level and the productivity gap to persist, which would be the convoy restated as monetary policy.

### China

**Verdict.** Partial loop: closed on two verifiers from roughly 1980 to 2008, decaying from 2008 as the first of them was captured, and opening deliberately since 2012 as the remaining ones were silenced. The bar from Chapter 8: this case survives only if benchmark plus export are accepted as sufficient verifiers with the press dead, and only if the post-2012 deceleration follows their decay rather than preceding it or arriving from somewhere else.

**Operated and structured.** The party's own logic is the best steel-man in the atlas. A continental country with no functioning price system in 1978 could verify nothing through markets, so it built a verifier out of the one instrument it possessed, personnel control: ranking provincial leaders against each other on growth and promoting the winners converts a hierarchy into a tournament, and opening the coast to foreign buyers imports a second verifier the party cannot lobby, so that the absence of elections is not a missing verifier but a substitution of cheaper ones, and for thirty years the output was hard to argue with. The state operated the commanding heights and still does: the state-owned enterprise sector at 23 to 28 percent of GDP by the World Bank's two methods (World Bank, 2019), the banks, urban land (state-owned, leased out by local governments as their principal revenue), and the statistical bureau. It structured the rest. Li and Zhou, using provincial party secretaries and governors from 1979 to 1995, found that the probability of promotion rose, and of termination fell, with provincial GDP growth both in absolute terms and relative to the predecessor (Li and Zhou, 2005): a benchmark verifier in its purest political form, with a binding consequence attached to the career of the person ranked. The export platform (special economic zones, then accession to the World Trade Organization in December 2001; WTO) supplied the incorruptible verifier: a Guangdong factory's output was judged by a buyer in Ohio who could not be promoted, demoted, or reported to. Township and village enterprises and later private firms generated most of the growth under that double verdict (Lardy, 2014). On the verifier-versus-veto distinction, China is the inverse of California: nothing blocks generation ex ante, the road is built before the review is written, and the problem was never veto density but the absence of any ex post verdict the generator could not author.

**Live verifiers.** Benchmark: live from 1980 to about 2008, captured after, and the capture was structural rather than accidental, because the metric on which provinces were ranked was compiled by the provinces. Export: live and incorruptible for the whole period; it is the only verifier in the case that the state never touched, because it could not. Price: partly live after the dual-track reforms of the 1980s (Wikipedia, dual-track price system) for goods, administered for credit and land, and walled off from world capital by controls. Exit: disabled for capital by those controls, partial for labor through the hukou system, live for consumers. Election/press: dead throughout; village elections select on nothing the center measures (East Asia Forum, 2014), no election at any level selects on output, and the press is a party organ by statute. Bankruptcy: decorative for state enterprises and local governments; the Enterprise Bankruptcy Law of 2006, in force from June 2007 (NPC Observer), exists, and a local government financing vehicle has never been allowed to default in the ordinary way. Tender: present on paper in procurement, captured in practice. Referendum: absent. The tournament itself was a real but partial verifier, never a meritocracy: Shih, Adolph, and Liu found that factional ties, not growth, predicted rank on the Central Committee (2012), and Jia, Kudamatsu, and Seim found that growth mattered only for leaders with patrons (2015); the correct reading is that the benchmark fired but with a captured component from the start. What the state does with a benchmark it can reach was demonstrated on an imported one: the World Bank discontinued its Doing Business report in 2021 after an external review found undue pressure from senior management to raise China's ranking in the 2018 edition (World Bank, 2021). This is the weakest survival among the six stress tests, and the reason should be stated here rather than deferred to Chapter 13: election/press was dead for the entire period, so the theory's account of the rise rests on two verifiers, and one of those two was reported by the generator it ranked, which means part of the rise it explains was a measurement the generator wrote.

**Bindingness.** Where the consequence bound, it bound hard: a provincial secretary whose growth lagged his neighbor's was passed over, and a firm that could not sell abroad lost its orders and its credit. Where it was soft, it was soft in the way Kornai described from Hungary, transposed to a country that never abandoned the vocabulary of planning. State enterprises borrow from state banks under an expectation of refinancing that has never been disappointed. Local governments, forbidden to borrow directly, borrowed through financing vehicles against land they lease, and the hidden stock of that debt is estimated at RMB 60 to 70 trillion, with the IMF's augmented measure putting public debt near 130 percent of GDP at the end of 2025 and the broad public-sector deficit around 13 percent of GDP (BOFIT, 2025; IMF, 2026); the program announced in November 2024, allowing RMB 2 trillion a year of special bonds through 2026 to refinance the most expensive hidden debt, is the bailout Kornai's firms expected, arriving on schedule (BOFIT, 2025). The market side shared it: private property developers borrowed under the same implicit guarantee and the same pre-sale model, and their collapse from 2021 (Wikipedia, Chinese property sector crisis) shows the softness was a property of the loop, not of ownership. The deeper problem sits at the junction of the two legs. The tournament's consequence was binding but its verdict was gameable, and a binding consequence attached to a falsifiable verdict does not select for output; it selects for falsification, with the same intensity that the consequence would otherwise have selected for growth. The rating agencies of Chapter 12 are the market instance of the same shape: a verifier whose input is supplied by the party it judges.

**The component.** The benchmark was a verifier the generator could falsify, so its decay was predicted by the theory rather than discovered by it. Chen, Chen, Hsieh, and Song showed the mechanics. The National Bureau of Statistics had been correcting the sum of provincial figures downward by about 5 percent of GDP on average since the mid-2000s, which is the verifier working: the center knew the provinces inflated and adjusted. After 2008 local data deteriorated further and the adjustment did not keep pace; reconstructing output from value-added tax receipts and other series less exposed to manipulation, they estimate that national growth from 2010 to 2016 was 1.8 percentage points a year lower than reported and that the investment and savings rate in 2016 was 7 percentage points lower (Chen, Chen, Hsieh, and Song, 2019). The finding is contested at the decimal and should be read for direction: satellite nightlights led Clark, Pinkovskiy, and Sala-i-Martin to conclude that official growth for 2004 to 2015 was if anything understated (2017), and a Federal Reserve indicator built from industrial production, retail sales, property, and partner-reported exports found post-2020 growth near 5 percent not overstated (Federal Reserve, 2025). The case does not need the decimal. It needs the date at which the center lost the ability to correct its own verifier, and both the forensic paper and the subsequent conduct of the state put that date at 2008, when the stimulus made growth the target of every official at once and Goodhart's regularity did what it does once pressure is placed on it for control purposes (Goodhart, 1975).

Chapter 15 will argue that verifiers die in a fixed order: bindingness softens first, independence is captured second, cost is inflated last and the verifier is quietly retired. China ran the sequence in order and on the record. The soft constraint on state enterprises and financing vehicles was there from the beginning; the benchmark's independence went in 2008; retirement began in 2023. The youth unemployment rate for ages 16 to 24 reached a record 21.3 percent in June 2023, the bureau suspended publication in August, and it resumed in January 2024 with a redefined series excluding students that read 14.9 percent (Time, 2023; Atlantic Council, 2024); the new series then set its own record of 18.9 percent in August 2025 (SCMP, 2025). Hundreds of other series have stopped, among them land sales, some foreign direct investment data, cremations, and a business-confidence index (China Digital Times, 2025), and in December 2025 the housing ministry suspended the private-sector home-sales data compiled by CRIC and China Index Academy (China Factor, 2025), which was the last benchmark on property that the generator did not compile. The retirements are the tail of a longer silencing. After 2012 the party recentralized the ranking of its officials onto loyalty and discipline, and the anti-corruption campaign that replaced the tournament as the career-shaping mechanism is not a verifier but a monitor, operated by the generator's own principal and reading no output anyone outside can check; the courts were told by their own chief justice in January 2017 that judicial independence is an erroneous Western concept (SCMP, 2017); the largest private generators were disciplined by regulatory fiat, beginning with the cancelled Ant Group listing of November 2020 (Lawfare, 2020); and the series that contradicted the line were the ones suspended (Time, 2023; China Digital Times, 2025). Export remained, and export, as Studwell's own cases show, verifies catch-up rather than invention, so the one incorruptible verifier left is the one least able to judge the output an economy at the frontier must produce. The deceleration follows the decay in time and in composition: growth fell from double digits toward the 5 percent target in the same years the benchmark was captured and the remaining verifiers silenced (Bloomberg, 2024), with demography, scale, and the exhaustion of easy catch-up acknowledged as residuals. The margin of survival is thin and should be stated as such. The theory accepts a closed loop on two verifiers, one of them self-reported, and dates the fall by the death of the self-reported one; a reader who thinks a self-reported benchmark should never have counted as live has a point, and the answer is that it counted for exactly as long as the center could and did correct it, and that its design guaranteed the correction would fail once the consequence attached to it grew large enough. That guarantee is the component: a verifier the generator can write is a verifier with a scheduled death, and the schedule was set by Goodhart before the tournament began (P7).

**Prediction.** If the benchmark verifier is rebuilt on data the provinces cannot touch, meaning a statistics bureau whose adjustments to provincial figures are published, the suspended series restored, and the property data compiled outside the ministry, expect official growth, partner-reported trade, and tax-implied output to reconverge within three years, and the count of retired series to fall; if instead statistics keep disappearing and the last incorruptible verifier is softened by state credit to exporters so that a foreign buyer's refusal no longer costs the seller anything, expect the augmented public debt ratio to keep rising past 130 percent of GDP and the number of suspended series to grow through 2030 while reported growth holds within a point of the target every year, which is the signature of a verdict written by the generator and the pattern the Soviet case of Chapter 11 shows in its last decade.

### South Korea and Taiwan

**Verdict.** Partial loop, closing after 1997 and stable since: an export verifier that was live from the mid-1960s, attached to a domestic consequence that bound on sales and stayed soft on debt until foreign creditors delivered the verdict the state had exempted.

**Operated and structured.** The Korean state under Park Chung-hee operated the banks and structured everything else. Nationalized in 1961 (Library of Congress country study), the commercial banks became the channel through which the state allocated credit to the chaebol, and directed credit was the subsidy: loans at negative real rates to firms that entered the industries the plan named (steel, shipbuilding, chemicals, automobiles). The state did not run those industries; it chose the generators and paid them to enter. Taiwan operated more at the top of the chain (state-owned steel, petrochemicals, and most upstream heavy production into the 1980s) and structured the rest, leaving the downstream to small and medium firms that sold abroad. The steel-man is the developmental state's own. An infant industry cannot meet world prices at birth, so the state must, in Amsden's phrase, get the prices wrong for a period; the danger in that logic is that infancy never ends, and Korea and Taiwan met it by letting the world market rather than the ministry declare when a firm had grown up. Latin America ran the same generator with the same subsidy and skipped the declaration. Import substitution in Argentina and Brazil combined tariffs, licensed entry, cheap credit, and a domestic market reserved for the protected firm; Brazil's market reserve for computers, in force until 1992 (Christian Science Monitor, 1991), is the type specimen, a protected producer selling to customers forbidden to buy anything else, so that the verdict on its output came from people who had no exit. The Korean firm was paid to produce and then required to sell to strangers who owed it nothing.

**Live verifiers.** Export, incorruptible by construction: the ministry in Seoul could set the loan rate but not the price a Hyundai fetched in Canada, and no lobby in Korea moved the world price of steel. Benchmark, in Studwell's account of Park's monthly export promotion meetings, where firm-level export figures were reviewed in person: an internal ranking that borrowed its metric from the external verdict, so the benchmark could not be gamed without also gaming customers abroad, which is the one design that resists Goodhart decay. Price, for the downstream Taiwanese firms, which bought inputs at administered prices and sold at world ones. Election/press, dead in Korea until 1987 and in Taiwan until martial law ended in the same year, and irrelevant to the industrial loop either way. Captured: the domestic bankruptcy verifier, because the banks that would have called the loans were owned by the state that had directed them, and a bank that has been told to lend cannot judge whether it should have. This is the imported verifier doing work outside the Gulf. The developmental state did not build a domestic verifier for industrial output because none was available at that point on the axis, and it borrowed the one verifier no domestic actor could reach; Chapter 13 asks whether the extension is an epicycle invented for oil states, and Korea is the instance it needs, with no oil in it. In Latin America the verifiers were absent rather than captured. The export verdict was never solicited, the domestic customer could not exit, and entry licensing was an ex ante veto on who might produce, which separates a verifier from a veto point in one sentence: Argentina's tariff wall verified nothing about the car behind it; it only prevented another car from arriving.

**Bindingness.** The consequence bound in one direction and not the other. Firms that missed export targets lost their credit lines, were merged into rivals, or were allowed to fail: Amsden's reciprocity, subsidy in exchange for performance standards, with the standards denominated in a currency the state did not print. Studwell's contrast is Malaysia's Proton, protected from 1983 and never forced to sell abroad, and the same contrast holds for the protected Argentine and Brazilian producer, whose subsidy was unconditional because nothing outside the country was ever asked to rate its output. But the consequence that bound on export performance did not bind on leverage. A chaebol that exported well could borrow without limit from banks that could not refuse, and the soft constraint (Kornai) lived in the balance sheet: the state had made itself the guarantor of every large loan, so debt-to-equity ratios above 400 percent by 1997 (Chang, 2003) were rational for the borrower and unreadable by the lender. The verdict that finally bound was delivered from outside. The collapse of Hanbo Steel in January 1997, the withdrawal of short-term dollar lending that autumn, and the IMF program of December 1997 (Yale Journal of Financial Crises) were the export verifier in its capital-account form, ruling on the one variable the domestic loop had exempted, and the Daewoo bankruptcy of 1999 (Chang, 2003), with debts the state declined to cover, was the first time the consequence reached the top of the roster. Taiwan passed the same year without a crisis because its exporters had no state bank behind them and had borrowed accordingly; their constraint had been hard the whole time, which is why they stayed small. The market-side symmetry sits inside the case: the chaebol's captive banks and Japan's zombie lenders (the Japan case, Chapter 10) softened by the identical mechanism, a lender who cannot afford to recognize the loss, and both were disciplined only when an outside creditor did the recognizing.

**The component.** Subsidy with an incorruptible external verdict attached: the state paid the generator to enter and let the world market decide whether it stayed, so directed credit, the instrument that wrecked Latin America, closed a loop in Korea because a verifier the state could not lobby sat at the output. That is P1 applied through the export verifier rather than the domestic price, and P7 in its exact form: the same subsidy predicts failure with the verifier absent and enables success with it live, while guaranteeing nothing about leverage, where the verifier was not looking. Cold War market access, which made the verdict available at all, Confucian schooling, and the land reforms imposed under occupation are residuals the lens does not claim (P1, P7).

**Prediction.** If Korea's post-1997 bankruptcy verifier is softened again for the largest groups, through state-bank forbearance of the kind extended to the shipbuilders after 2015 (IndustryWeek, 2017), expect group leverage to re-accumulate and a second forced restructuring of a top-ten group within a decade; if the verifier holds, expect the next large failure to be resolved by creditors and courts rather than by the presidential office within the same period.

### The United Kingdom

**Verdict.** Partial loop, oscillating rather than trending: the state installed a verifier over its own hospitals in 1991, weakened it in 1999, rebuilt it by 2006, extended it in 2012, and dismantled it in 2022, while its privatized water sector ran a private open loop under a regulator the generator could out-wait.

**Operated and structured.** The National Health Service is the operator rule at scale. The state owns the hospitals, employs the clinicians, and pays from general taxation, and its output sits in the middle of the axis (surgical quality verifiable by mortality statistics at scale, waiting times verifiable by anyone with a calendar), so the question is not whether the state may operate but what verifies the operator. The internal market's own logic, which Le Grand gave its theory, is that a public payer who does not also own the hospital can compare hospitals and move money between them, so a single-payer system can read the verdict a market would deliver without handing the service to profit. Under his four models of delivery (trust, command and control, voice, choice with competition), choice is the most robust because it works whether providers are knights or knaves and turns pawns into queens, but only with real alternatives, spare capacity, usable information, and protection against cream-skimming. The purchaser-provider split of April 1991 (NHS and Community Care Act 1990) made health authorities and fundholding general practitioners the purchasers and turned hospitals into trusts that sold to them (NHS England; Wikipedia, NHS internal market). Labour abolished fundholding in 1999 and kept the split (Health Act 1999); a national tariff (Payment by Results) from 2003 (Farrar and colleagues, 2009), foundation trusts from 2004 (King's Fund), and patient choice of hospital from 2006 rebuilt it with money that followed the patient; the Health and Social Care Act 2012 replaced primary care trusts with clinical commissioning groups on 1 April 2013 and opened services to any qualified provider; the Health and Care Act 2022 abolished the groups, gave integrated care systems statutory form on 1 July 2022, and ended the obligation to tender clinical services (NHS England; LGA, 2022). Water was privatized in 1989 (House of Commons Library) as regional monopolies under a price-setting regulator, which is structuring without any of the conditions Le Grand names: no alternative supplier, no exit, and information held by the generator.

**Live verifiers.** Exit, in the form of patient choice, live from 2006 wherever the conditions held, which is to say in cities and in elective surgery and hardly at all elsewhere. The results are the best evidence in the case: Gaynor, Moreno-Serra, and Propper (2013) found mortality fell faster after 2006 where hospitals faced more competition, Cooper and colleagues (2011) found the same for heart-attack deaths (Economic Journal, 2011), and Pollock and others disputed the method; the finding survives as a direction, not a magnitude. Benchmark: mortality statistics, waiting-time targets, and league tables, the strongest and most Goodhart-prone verifier in the system; the waiting-list gaming that Chapter 15 catalogs is this benchmark decaying under pressure. Election/press, live and noisy: a general election verifies the NHS as a whole and never one trust, and the press verifies scandals rather than distributions, which is how Mid Staffordshire ran for years before the Francis inquiry (Health Foundation, 2013). Tender, and here the verifier became a veto: the 2012 Act's procurement duty made commissioners buy through a process open to legal challenge, so money went to procurement lawyers and ex ante process displaced ex post comparison, and the 2022 Act was right to remove the duty and wrong to treat the split it sat on as the same thing. In water the roster is shorter. Price, set by Ofwat in five-year reviews rather than by any customer; exit, impossible; bankruptcy, present in statute as special administration and never yet applied to a large company; the regulator, captured in the soft sense that the generator holds the data on its own pipes and the regulator holds the pen.

**Bindingness.** In the NHS the consequence never fully bound because purchaser and provider had the same owner. A trust that lost patients lost tariff income, then received a deficit bailout from the same department, so money followed the patient in year one and followed the deficit in year three; the soft constraint (Kornai) is structural, since the state cannot let its own hospital close for want of custom and everyone in the loop knows it. What bound was the benchmark, when a minister chose to bind it: the four-hour and eighteen-week targets of the 2000s (Bevan and Hood, 2006) moved waits because chief executives lost their jobs against them, and then suffered Goodhart decay as the metric was gamed. In water the consequence is softer still, and the record is current. Thames Water's shareholders refused new equity in March 2024 (Fortune, 2024); KKR withdrew as preferred bidder on 3 June 2025, collapsing a GBP 3 billion raise, and existing creditors supplied a GBP 3 billion super-senior facility instead (Octus, 2025); the senior-creditor vehicle London & Valley Water proposed writing off a quarter of GBP 16 billion of Class A debt and all of the junior tiers while seeking forbearance on roughly GBP 1 billion of fines (CreditSights, 2026), which is a request, in writing, for a soft constraint; Ofwat's board split on approval in April 2026 (Bloomberg, 2026); on 21 July 2026 the offer added a government golden share and a ten-year dividend ban, and the new Burnham government said special administration "remains a viable option" (Water Magazine, 2026), with liquidity running only to the fourth quarter of 2026 (Good Law Project, 2026). As of late August 2026 nothing has been decided, and the regulator itself has been sentenced: on 21 July 2025 the government accepted the Cunliffe commission and announced that Ofwat would be replaced by a single water regulator, Ofwat continuing in the interim (HLWS865, 2025; Euronews, 2025). A regulator abolished for failing is a captured verifier autopsied. The generator that captured it is a private company, and the shape is identical to the NHS: a verifier the generator felt as a cost and worked to cancel, by lobbying in one case and by legislation in the other.

**The component.** The purchaser-provider split installed a verifier that the state, as generator, kept dismantling and rebuilding, and the oscillation is the evidence. A generator that felt no cost from its verifier would leave it alone, and one with no use for it would abolish it once; three decades of installing, weakening, rebuilding, extending, and dismantling the same instrument show a state that can see the verifier working (waits move, mortality moves where choice is real) and cannot bear the verdict for long, because every bad verdict lands on a hospital it owns. The operator rule is not violated by the NHS, whose output is verifiable enough to operate; it is violated by the operator's control of its own verifier, which is the one condition the rule forbids (P4).

**Prediction.** If tariff and patient choice are replaced by block budgets under the integrated care systems, expect the gap in elective waiting times between the best and worst systems to widen within five years with no published mechanism selecting on it, and expect a fourth purchaser-provider reconstruction under a new name within fifteen years of the 2022 Act; if Thames Water instead enters special administration with creditor losses actually imposed, expect the sector's cost of capital to rise within a year, which is the price of a bankruptcy verifier made live for the first time since 1989.

### The European Union

**Verdict.** Partial loop, closing at the border and opening at the center: the Union exports verification to candidates with a consequence that binds and softens consequences for members whose verdicts it cannot afford to let bind.

**Operated and structured.** The Union operates almost nothing: no army, no hospitals, a budget near 1 percent of the bloc's income (European Parliament) spent mostly on farms and regions. It structures. The single market is a mutual-recognition rule (Cassis de Dijon, 1979) backed by a Commission that can sue a member for keeping a product out; state-aid control is an ex post verdict on subsidies that no national government can render on itself; accession is a checklist with a prize at the end; the euro is a currency without a treasury, whose members promised at Maastricht in 1992 and in the Stability and Growth Pact of 1997 to hold deficits under 3 percent of output and debt under 60, with a procedure and a fine at the end of it. The Union's own logic is that twenty-seven governments agreeing once to a common rule produce more freedom of exchange than twenty-seven separate regulators, and that a candidate state will accept verification it would never impose on itself when the reward is membership. Both halves are true, and the second is the most successful imported verifier on record: Schimmelfennig and Sedelmeier's external incentives model holds that conditionality works when the reward is credible, sizeable, and speedy, the conditions determinate, and the adoption cost low, which described central Europe between the Copenhagen criteria of 1993 and the enlargement of 2004 (JEPP, 2019).

**Live verifiers.** Export, internally: the single market is an export verifier for every member's firms, incorruptible for the reason Korea's was, since no ministry sets the price at which a Polish tractor sells in Lyon. Exit, for capital and labor across borders and for firms choosing where to incorporate. Benchmark: Eurostat, which verified Greece in 2010 after the national office had not (the Greece case, Chapter 11), and the Commission's annual country reports, which verify and do not bind. Benchmark again, in state-aid control, where every member's subsidies are read against one common rulebook: a member that subsidizes a firm answers afterward to a Commission that can order the money repaid, which is verification, unlike the ex ante block that product and conduct directives often become. Election/press, noisy at Union level, where a European election verifies nothing in particular, and live at national level, where it pulls the other way, since a national electorate rewards a government for defying Brussels. Decorative: the fiscal rules, whose verdict on Germany and France in 2003 was set aside by the Council of finance ministers, which is to say by the generators being verified, when the excessive-deficit procedure was held in abeyance in November 2003 (ECB, 2003). Accession conditionality is the imported verifier seen from the lender's side, and its record after import is the model's own finding: leverage collapses on the day of membership, and the post-2004 reversals in Hungary and Poland followed (JEPP, 2019). Brussels is accused of veto accumulation, and the accusation is precise only once verifier and veto are separated. Unanimity in the Council on tax and foreign policy, the thicket of directives, and the comitology beneath them are ex ante blocks on generation, a rule-thicket intervention at continental scale; the Court of Justice's infringement rulings, state-aid recovery orders, and Eurostat's revisions are ex post verdicts. The Union has too much of the first and, on the fiscal side, too little consequence attached to the second.

**Bindingness.** For candidates the consequence binds absolutely: negotiating chapters close or they do not, and nothing the candidate can vote on changes that. For members the consequence is soft (Kornai), and the euro is the proof. Greece borrowed for a decade at spreads that priced no default (Bank of Greece, 2011) because lenders read correctly that a euro-area sovereign would not be allowed to fail, and when the verdict came it took the form the soft constraint predicts: three programs disbursing EUR 288.7 billion, of which EUR 203.8 billion from the EFSF and ESM and EUR 32.1 billion from the IMF, ending on 20 August 2018 (ESM, 2018). The market-side symmetry is inside the figure. The French and German banks that lent to Athens were generators whose own verifier, the price of Greek risk, they had disabled by assuming the rescue, and the programs made them right; a private lender who prices in the bailout is a soft constraint priced by the market, and the 2010 spreads were as decorative a verifier as the 2003 Council. The 2024 reform of the Pact, adopted by the Council on 29 April 2024 and in force the next day, replaced the old structural-balance arithmetic with a single operational indicator, net primary expenditure, four-to-five-year fiscal-structural plans extendable to seven with reforms and investment, and two safeguards for debt reduction and deficit resilience, with the 3 and 60 percent reference values retained (Council, 2024; ECB, 2025). It is a better verifier attached to the same consequence: the sanction remains a Council vote by the sanctioned, and no large member has ever paid a fine. Rule-of-law conditionality on Union funds, applied against Hungary since 2022 (Council of the European Union, 2022), is the first attempt to carry accession-style bindingness past accession, and its early record is a member trading vetoes for released funds, which is bargaining rather than verification. Scale, which lets the Union import verifiers by offering a market no candidate can refuse, is a residual the lens does not claim.

**The component.** The Union exports verification to candidates and softens consequences for members: one institution runs the most binding imported verifier in the atlas at its border and a decorative one inside, and the difference is not the quality of the verdict, since Eurostat and the Commission verify members as closely as candidates, but whether anything the verified party wants can be withheld. Membership can be withheld; a fine on France cannot be collected. The asymmetric law reads the Union accordingly: a live single-market verifier enabled the convergence of the accession states without guaranteeing it, and a dead fiscal consequence predicted Greece with near certainty (P7).

**Prediction.** If the Council continues to attach no binding consequence to excessive-deficit findings against France and Italy, expect neither debt ratio to fall over the first seven-year plan horizon, to 2031, and expect the next crisis backstop to exceed the last; if a large member is sanctioned for the first time before then, the case reverts from partial to closing and the soft-constraint reading of the euro (Chapter 11) is falsified for members.

### Chile

**Verdict.** Chile's loop is partial and oscillating: the price and exit verifiers that the 1981 pension design installed fired for four decades on a generator that faced no binding consequence from their verdict, and the 2025 reform is the first attempt to attach one.

**Operated and structured.** The Chilean state since 1990 has operated little and structured much; its one large operating venture, Codelco, sells copper into a world price it cannot lobby, which is why the operator has not embarrassed itself there. The pension system is the structuring instrument that defines the country. Decree Law 3,500 of 1981 replaced pay-as-you-go schemes with individual accounts funded by a 10 percent employee contribution and no employer contribution (KPMG, 2025), managed by licensed private administrators, the AFPs, under a superintendency that regulates portfolios and publishes returns. The design's own logic is strong and should be stated at full strength. A defined-contribution account converts a legislature's promise, which a legislature can inflate away or fund by borrowing from the next generation, into a property right the worker can see, and it puts the verdict on the fund manager in the hands of the person whose money it is; the fiscal soft constraint (Kornai) that ruins pay-as-you-go systems, a benefit voted by one parliament and paid by another, is replaced by an account no parliament can promise beyond what it holds. The state's remaining pension role was meant to be a signal intervention at the bottom, a guaranteed minimum, and since 2022 (Superintendencia de Pensiones, 2022) a universal cash pension, the PGU, which the 2025 law raises to CLP 250,000 from September 2025 for those aged 82 and over (Gob.cl, 2025); cash is the thinnest instrument, and the state chose it. Allende's Cybersyn, a verifier fed entirely by the enterprises it judged (Chapter 2), was destroyed in the coup of 11 September 1973 before it could itself be verified, and is evidence in neither direction.

**Live verifiers.** Price is live for goods and, on paper, for pensions: every AFP publishes its fee, and the fee is a percentage of the worker's wage. Exit is live on paper: a worker may move an account between administrators at will. Benchmark is live: the superintendency publishes each fund's returns by risk class and the press reprints them. Export is live and incorruptible: copper is priced abroad, and the structural balance rule adopted in 2001 (Inter-American Development Bank) budgets on a reference copper price set by an independent committee rather than on the price of the day. Election/press is live; power has alternated between coalitions in every direction since 1990. Referendum has been live and ex post in the decisive sense: the electorate approved a constitutional rewrite in October 2020 and then rejected two completed drafts, the convention's in September 2022 and the expert council's in December 2023 (Americas Quarterly, 2023; Wikipedia), each time voting on a finished text. Bankruptcy is live for firms and for the AFPs as companies, whose managed funds are segregated from their own balance sheets. The constitutional episode from the protests of October 2019 to the plebiscite of 2023 was, underneath the slogans, a contest over ex ante vetoes. The 1980 constitution locked its economic settlement behind supermajority quorums (Sierra, 2012), a veto on future legislation rather than a verifier of it; the 2022 draft would have replaced those with an entrenched catalog of rights enforceable against future legislatures, a veto of the opposite sign; the 2023 draft would have re-entrenched the first settlement in new words. The electorate used the referendum as a verifier, ex post on completed drafts, to refuse both attempts to write new vetoes, which is the distinction of Chapter 4 performed by an electorate. Chile keeps the old vetoes and gained no new ones, and that is the oscillation's resting state.

**Bindingness.** The consequence binds on the worker, not on the manager. The contribution is compulsory, so there is no exit from the system, only between administrators; the fee is charged on wages flowing in rather than on assets or returns, so a manager's revenue rises with employment and indexation and is indifferent to the pension its funds will pay; and switching, for a product whose quality is revealed thirty years after purchase, is rare enough that the exit verifier fires into an empty room. Returns were published, fees were published, and the administrators consolidated into a handful (World Bank, 2023) whose fees stayed high because the price verdict changed no allocation. The honest failure is coverage and adequacy rather than returns: self-employment and informality left long contribution gaps, replacement rates came in far below what the 1981 projections implied (IMF, 2021), and by 2019 pensions were the grievance into which a metro fare protest turned. The state's own constraint proved softer than the accounts: the three pandemic withdrawals of 2020 and 2021 (EBG International, 2021) let workers pull a tenth of their balances each time, a legislature cancelling the savings mandate under pressure, which is the soft constraint reappearing on the side of the design built to abolish it. The repair that matters is a consequence, not a verifier. Chile began auctioning new entrants to the lowest-fee administrator in 2010 (World Bank, 2023), and Law 21,735, approved by Congress on 29 January 2025 and published on 26 March 2025, extends the auction to the stock, moving 10 percent of all affiliates every two years to the lowest bidder from August 2027, alongside an employer contribution raised by 7 points, from 1.5 percent of pay to 8.5, phased over nine years from August 2025, of which 4.5 points go to individual accounts and the rest to a new autonomous fund covering disability, survivorship, a longevity compensation for women, and a temporary benefit for current pensioners (KPMG, 2025; Gob.cl, 2025; Alessandri, 2025; WTW, 2025). The same-shape failure in the other ownership regime is Argentina's nationalization of its own account system in 2008 (UNRISD), which answered the same coverage grievance by removing the verifier and spending the balances; Chilean voters have twice declined to follow it.

**The component.** A verifier installed without a binding consequence for the verified is a decorative market: the AFP system published price and returns for forty years to a captive customer whose exit changed nothing for the manager, the same shape as an insurer paid regardless of the care its network delivers, and the 2025 auction is the first instrument that lets the price verdict take a manager's customers away (P2).

**Prediction.** If the biennial auction of 10 percent of affiliates runs from August 2027 as legislated, expect incumbent fees to converge toward the winning bid within two cycles, by 2031; if the auction is postponed or the incumbents exempted, expect fees to hold at their current level and pension coverage to return to the ballot as a constitutional grievance within the same period.

### The Gulf

**Verdict.** The Gulf loop, with the United Arab Emirates as the anchor, is partial and stable, with the domestic verifiers dead or administered and the performance real. The bar from Chapter 8: the lens survives only if the imported verifier does predictive work beyond the Gulf, in Greece, Korea, and the EU accession states, checks stated here and tallied in Chapter 13.

**Operated and structured.** The ruling families operate the rent and structure the rest. Oil and gas revenue accrues to the state and its sovereign funds, and that is the residual: the lens does not explain why the rent exists, only what it buys. What the state operates directly is the citizen economy: public employment for nationals, subsidized power and water, administered fuel prices until their liberalization in 2015 (UAE Cabinet, 2015), land grants, and a bargain in which nationals receive income without taxation and without representation. What it structures is the non-oil economy, which the Federal Competitiveness and Statistics Centre reports at roughly 79 percent of output in 2025, non-oil GDP having grown 6.8 percent to AED 1.5 trillion inside a total of AED 1.9 trillion (The National, 2026; Khaleej Times, 2025). The structuring runs through state-owned firms sent into world markets (DP World's ports, the Emirates and Etihad airlines, the free zones of Dubai and Abu Dhabi, the Dubai International Financial Centre with its own common-law courts, whose bench is drawn mostly from England, Australia, and other common-law jurisdictions alongside Emirati judges) and through a residency regime that makes roughly nine in ten residents foreigners on revocable visas (DIFC Courts, 2026; Emirates 24|7, 2011). Vetoes are few by design: a ruler who is the residual claimant has no interest in blocking his own generators ex ante, so licenses are issued in days and review is thin, which makes the Gulf veto-poor and verification-poor at once, the reverse of the California pairing in Chapter 4. The Gulf's own logic is coherent and should be stated in full. A dynasty is a residual claimant with a horizon of generations, so it invests the rent in assets the world will price rather than consuming it, and its reputation in the markets for capital, firms, and skilled labor is a verifier it cannot capture and would not trust a domestic institution to run; the citizen bargain buys the domestic quiet in which the imported verifier can operate.

**Live verifiers.** The domestic ledger is nearly empty. Election/press is dead: no election can remove a ruler and no press could try. Referendum is absent. Price is live for most goods and for property, whose buyers are largely foreign, and administered for power, water, and citizen labor. Exit for citizens is limited, since a citizen's income is tied to the state; exit for expatriates is total and fast, a visa ending with a job, which makes the expatriate labor market the most sensitive exit verifier in the region and the emirate the party being verified, since every professional who declines a posting or leaves one delivers a verdict on schools, housing costs, and the law as applied to foreigners. Bankruptcy is live for private firms and decorative for state-linked ones: when Dubai World sought a standstill on its debt in November 2009, Abu Dhabi supplied USD 10 billion (Bloomberg, 2009) and no state-linked creditor took the loss the verdict implied. Benchmark is live in an internal form the federation supplies for free: seven emirates compete for firms and residents, and Dubai, with little oil of its own, 5.1 percent of its output in 2006 (Gulf News, 2007), built the non-oil model that Abu Dhabi then copied with more capital. The verifier that carries the case is export, read broadly. DP World's terminals earn what shippers pay in a market where cargo can be rerouted; Emirates fills seats against every carrier through Istanbul, Doha, and Singapore; the free zones compete for firms that can register in Singapore instead; and the sovereign funds are graded by external scoreboards that no emirate pays. On Global SWF's governance, sustainability, and resilience scoreboard for 2026, the Abu Dhabi Investment Authority scores 52 percent, down from 56 the year before, while Mubadala scores 88 percent in both 2025 and 2026 (Global SWF, 2026). The difference is the verifier. Mubadala borrows on international bond markets and co-invests with outside partners, so its accounts face counterparties who can refuse; ADIA has no creditor and no partner, and on the older ten-point transparency checklist dated secondary sources place it around six against Mubadala's ten (SWFI; Gulf News). The canonical contrast is Norway against 1MDB: a fund that publishes every holding twice a year and scored 100 of 100 on the Global Pension Transparency Benchmark (Top1000funds, 2025), against one from which the United States Department of Justice says more than USD 4.5 billion was taken (DOJ, 2024). The Gulf funds sit between the two, and where each sits is predicted by whether anyone outside can refuse it money.

**Bindingness.** Consequences bind where the verifier is imported and soften wherever the rent reaches. A terminal operator that loses a concession loses it; an airline that loses a route loses it; a fund that cannot refinance pays more. Inside the rent nothing binds: a citizen ministry that produces process rather than output is funded next year regardless, a state-linked developer that overbuilds is refinanced by the sovereign, and the 2009 standstill established that an emirate's word is guaranteed by its neighbor's reserves. The mechanism the case turns on has two parts and they must be kept apart. Rent is slack: it pays the deficit that an open domestic loop produces, so the loop can stay open indefinitely without the shortage, the queue, or the collapse that follow in Venezuela, and this is the mechanism by which a firm with a captive revenue carries dead divisions for decades, Hirschman's lazy monopoly with a better balance sheet. Imported verification is the second part: the non-oil sectors the rent was spent to build are the ones exposed to buyers, creditors, and employees who can leave, and those are the sectors that perform. The lens does not survive on its domestic ledger alone; on the eight verifiers as Chapter 3 lists them, with export read as goods, the UAE is a Venezuela with better weather, and AED 1.5 trillion of non-oil output refutes that reading. It survives with the extension, and the extension is a reading rather than a ninth verifier: export, generalized from goods to capital, talent, firms, and reputation, does for the Gulf's non-oil economy what export in the narrow sense did for Korea's manufacturers. The epicycle risk is real and is named here so that Chapter 13 can weigh it. An extension that lets any performance be attributed after the fact to some external market explains everything and therefore nothing. The guard is that the imported verifier must be named before the outcome, with the party who can refuse and the consequence that follows; it verifies only what it reads, so export in any width grades catch-up and not invention (the China case, Chapter 10), and it stops when the import stops (the European Union case, Chapter 10); and it must predict variation inside the Gulf that oil alone would not: Mubadala against ADIA, Dubai's model against the citizen sector, and the Saudi megaprojects built for a domestic audience with no buyer who can refuse, which were cut back (Bloomberg, 2024), a consistency the reading fits rather than a forecast it made. If the same reading also does work in Greece, where Eurostat was the borrowed statistician, in Korea, where world markets graded the chaebol the banks would not, and in the accession states, where conditionality bound until membership removed it, the extension is a finding; if it works only here, it is an epicycle, and this case stands as the theory's wound.

**The component.** The verifier is outside the country and cannot be lobbied by it: the shipper, the creditor, the expatriate, and the scoreboard sit beyond the reach of a ruler who has silenced every domestic verifier, and the sectors they judge are the ones that work, live verifiers enabling without guaranteeing, since nothing the rent touches is verified at all (P7).

**Prediction.** If ADIA begins to borrow on international markets or to take outside co-investors at scale, expect its governance and transparency scores to converge toward Mubadala's within five years; and if oil revenue falls for three consecutive years, expect the exposed sectors (ports, aviation, the financial center) to hold their output while citizen-sector spending contracts, whereas a joint collapse of both would show the imported verifier doing no work and would settle the Chapter 13 tally against the extension.

### Rwanda

**Verdict.** Rwanda's loop is partial and decaying: a benchmark verifier the state built in 2006 delivered the outputs at the cheap end of the axis and is now set, read, and reported by the generator it judges, so Goodhart decay is the prediction rather than the diagnosis.

**Operated and structured.** The state operates nearly everything and structures little. Thirty districts deliver health, schooling, roads, and agricultural programs under contracts with the presidency; the ruling party's investment arm and the military's holdings (Gokgur, 2012) occupy the commanding heights of what is called the private sector; the national airline is a state project whose losses are absorbed by annual capital injections (KT Press, 2025). The instrument is imihigo. Adopted in 2006 with the first poverty-reduction strategy, it aggregates priorities from village meetings through cells and sectors to districts, has each district mayor sign an annual performance contract with the President, sends national evaluation teams to score districts against their targets, and holds quarterly accountability days at which the scores are read out (AfDB policy brief; NISR imihigo reports; Canadian Journal of African Studies, 2024). The system's own logic deserves its full strength. In a poor state with no press that could report a clinic without drugs and no private sector that could route around a district that fails, a quantified, publicly sworn contract between each mayor and the head of state, scored against neighboring districts and announced in public, is the cheapest verifier available and more than most states at Rwanda's income possess; the pre-colonial imihigo, a warrior's public vow of what he would achieve, gives the ritual a legibility no donor's logframe ever had. At the bottom of the axis it worked as the operator rule says it should: vaccination coverage, insecticide-treated nets, facility births, and kilometers of feeder road are outputs a visiting team can count, and Rwanda's health indicators improved faster than its neighbors' (Exemplars in Global Health, undated) in the years the contracts counted them. The reset of 1994 (Olson) and the country's small scale are residuals the lens does not claim.

**Live verifiers.** Benchmark is the live verifier and it is the case. Its published record shows the average district score rising from 66.3 percent to 81.5 percent between 2009/10 and 2010/11 (AfDB policy brief), the compression toward the top of the scale that any self-reported benchmark shows in its second year, and the evaluation for 2024/25 found planning and budgeting still unrealistic, which a Kigali news site read in April 2026 as a system showing fatigue (NISR, 2026; KT Press, 2026). Export is live but thin: coffee, tea, minerals, and gorilla permits face world buyers, and the sector is too small to discipline the state. Price is live for goods. Exit exists for those who can leave and is punished for those who speak after leaving (Human Rights Watch, 2023).
