Private ownership did not save it and the state did not cause it alone; the sector is the demonstration that price dies as easily under contract as under decree, and that a stack of private generators with no verifier between them and the consumer is Gosplan with a billing department (P2).

**Prediction.** If the price verifier is restored for shoppable procedures, meaning the claims-based actual prices and executive attestation required from April 2026 (CMS, 2025; Dentons, 2026) are enforced to the point that a patient or an employer can compare the negotiated rate for a knee replacement across the hospitals of one metropolitan area, expect within five years a measurable compression of price dispersion for those procedures in the markets where the data is used, on the pattern cash-pay surgery already shows; if the rule is again met with uninterpretable files and a pace of ten penalties a year, expect no change in dispersion and continued growth in the administrative share, because a verifier that is published but not read is decorative.

### The Rating Agencies

**Verdict.** Open loop, stable in its openness: the verifier of the world's largest credit market was paid by the generators it judged, was made mandatory by the state, failed on a scale no verifier exposed to its own verdict could have survived, and stands today with the same three firms, the same payment model, and the same designation it had in 2006.

**Operated and structured.** The state operates nothing here and structures everything that matters. In 1975 the Securities and Exchange Commission amended its net capital rule for broker-dealers, Rule 15c3-1, to weight securities by the ratings of a "nationally recognized statistical rating organization," a designation it then conferred on the existing firms without defining it. Over the following three decades the designation was written into bank capital requirements, money market fund rules, insurance regulation, pension law, and eventually the Basel framework (Congressional Research Service, 2010), so that a rating from a designated firm was no longer an opinion an investor could weigh but a license without which a security could not be held by most of the capital in the world; Frank Partnoy named the product in 1999: the agencies sold regulatory licenses, not information (Partnoy, 1999). The firms themselves had switched in the early 1970s from charging investors for the rating to charging issuers, a change usually attributed to the photocopier and the Penn Central default (Congressional Research Service, 2010). The steel-man is coherent. A rating is a public good once published, so investors free-ride rather than pay; the issuer has the deepest interest in being rated and can fund coverage of the whole market; and the agency's reputation is supposed to be the verifier of the verifier, since a firm whose ratings fail loses the franchise its ratings depend on. Every clause of that argument holds where exit is live and the instrument has a track record, and the rule of 1975 removed the first condition while the structured finance of 2004 to 2007 removed the second.

**Live verifiers.** Price was alive and inherited the capture: the market price of a mortgage-backed tranche in 2006 was freely paid and wrong, because the buyer was reading a rating written for the seller (Chapter 2). Exit was dead by statute. An investor who distrusted the three designated firms could not take its business to a fourth, since a fourth firm's rating did not satisfy the capital rule, while an issuer who disliked one agency's verdict could take the deal to the next; the only exit the model allowed ran in the wrong direction, the generator leaving the verifier while the verifier's readers could not. Under those conditions competition among verifiers is capture at speed, because each agency's structured finance revenue depended on being chosen by a small number of repeat issuers, and the verdict drifted toward the one the issuer wanted at the rate the shopping allowed. Benchmark was decorative: the agencies' historical default statistics were published and accurate for corporate bonds, and were applied to instruments with no history, so the number verified a track record that did not exist. Election/press fired late and loudly. The Financial Crisis Inquiry Commission concluded in 2011 that the crisis could not have happened without the agencies (FCIC, 2011); Standard and Poor's settled with the Department of Justice, nineteen states and the District of Columbia for USD 1.375 billion in February 2015, with USD 125 million more to CalPERS, admitting no violation of law but acknowledging that it had delayed adopting stricter models in 2005 (JURIST, 2015; Burr & Forman, 2015); Moody's settled for nearly USD 864 million in January 2017 with factual admissions that it had deviated from its own published methodologies and had failed to manage the conflicts of the issuer-pays model, and accepted five years of chief executive compliance certification (DOJ, 2017). The press had the story and the electorate had the crisis, and the verifier that fired was the slow, aggregated one, seven and nine years after the crash of 2008, at a combined cost below a single year of the rating revenue the boom had added to the three largest agencies (US Senate Permanent Subcommittee on Investigations, 2010). Bankruptcy never fired: no designated agency failed, none lost its designation, and none lost its share. The scale of American capital markets multiplied the damage and had nothing to do with the design.

**Bindingness.** The soft constraint (Kornai) here was not a bailout; it was the regulation itself. A verifier whose verdict is required by law cannot be punished by its readers for being wrong, because the readers are required to hold its verdict whether it is right or wrong, and the state that mandated it could not withdraw the mandate without unwinding every capital rule that leaned on it. Dodd-Frank in 2010 ordered the removal of statutory references to ratings in section 939A and, in the Franken amendment, ordered the SEC to study assigning structured finance ratings through a board rather than by issuer choice, which would have made the verifier's income independent of its verdict; the SEC's 2012 study declined to adopt it, and the issuer-pays model remains dominant, with S&P, Moody's, and Fitch holding the large majority of outstanding ratings (SEC, 2024; CRS). The settlements fell on the shareholders of two firms as a one-time charge and changed neither the model nor the market structure. The consequence a captured verifier is supposed to face, the loss of its franchise to a rival whose verdicts were right, was cancelled in advance by the designation, and the consequence the state imposed afterwards was a fine on a business that continued. The symmetric state failure is the Greek statistical service before 2010 (the Greece case, Chapter 11): an office housed inside the ministry whose deficit it reported, verifying the generator on the generator's payroll, until an external verifier, Eurostat, fired. The Greek office was rebuilt as ELSTAT under an imported verifier; the American agencies were fined and kept.

**The component.** A verifier whose income depended on the verdict. Nothing else in the case is needed and nothing in the case contradicts it: the fraud suits that followed were a symptom, the correlation mathematics was a pretext, and the housing bubble was the occasion. The agencies were verifiers by design, their whole product a verdict on securities they did not issue, for buyers who could not evaluate ten thousand mortgages themselves, and they were paid by the party that wanted the verdict favorable, in a market where that party could take its business elsewhere and the buyers could not. That is a captured verifier by construction, before any analyst lowered a standard, and the state's contribution was to make the captured verifier mandatory, so that the market's remaining defense, the buyer who declines to trust it, was removed by rule. It is the purest capture on record because no intermediary, no covenant, and no forbearance was required: the loop was drawn open on paper, in the payment line of a contract, and the order in which such a verifier dies is taken up in full in Chapter 15 (P2).

**Prediction.** If the verifier's income is decoupled from the verdict for structured products, by random assignment of the rating agency, by investor-pays, or by any rule under which the issuer cannot choose who rates it, expect within one credit cycle a measurable fall in the share of newly issued structured tranches rated at the top grade and a widening of split ratings between agencies on the same instrument; if issuer-pays and the three-firm designation persist, expect the next new credit instrument, whatever it is called, to be rated at the top grade on the terms the mortgage pools of 2006 received, and to be downgraded in the following downturn at a rate the agencies' published default statistics did not predict, within the same cycle.

### The Canadian Grocery Oligopoly

**Verdict.** Open loop in the cheapest-to-verify sector on the axis, decaying for two decades and closing since 2023, when the state began removing the private contracts that had disabled exit.

**Operated and structured.** Nobody operates. The state runs no store and sets no shelf price, and until 2023 it structured almost nothing either, leaving structure to the five firms that sell most of Canada's groceries (Loblaw, Empire/Sobeys, Metro, Walmart, and Costco), which structured the sector by contract. The instruments were three. Restrictive covenants registered on land titles forbade any successor owner of a vacated site from selling groceries; exclusivity clauses in shopping-center leases forbade the landlord from leasing to a competing grocer; and the efficiencies defense in section 96 of the Competition Act let a merger found to substantially lessen competition proceed anyway if its claimed efficiency gains outweighed the harm, a defense the Tribunal applied to let a propane merger stand over the Commissioner's objection (Superior Propane, 2000 to 2003) and which let the three domestic chains absorb regional rivals for a generation. The steel-man is real. A country of forty million spread across a continent's width cannot support many warehouse networks, grocery retail runs on net margins of a few percent, so scale is the only place a low price can come from, and an anchor tenant that draws the traffic to a shopping center has a claim against a landlord that would let a rival free-ride on it; a competition statute written in 1986 for a small open economy reasonably weighed efficiency against concentration. Geography and scale are genuine residuals; they explain why Canada has few chains, not why no one could open a store on a site a chain had left. The state's structuring began with the Competition Bureau's Retail Grocery Market Study of June 2023, which concluded that Canada needs more competition and recommended a grocery innovation strategy, support for independent and international entrants, harmonized unit pricing, and limits on property controls (Competition Bureau, 2023). Bill C-56, in force from December 2023, repealed the efficiencies defense, gave the Bureau compulsory market-study powers, and extended section 90.1 to agreements between non-competitors whose significant purpose is to lessen competition (McMillan, 2023; Davies, 2023); Bill C-59, in force from June 2024, added administrative monetary penalties, structural remedies, and disgorgement and, from June 2025, broadened private access to the Tribunal (Bennett Jones, 2025). The Bureau's final Property Controls Guidelines of June 2025 treat exclusivity clauses as justifiable only in limited entry-inducing circumstances and restrictive covenants as rarely justifiable outside exceptional circumstances; Sobeys removed its covenants in Crowsnest Pass, Alberta, in January 2025, and Loblaw committed in June 2025 to remove property controls nationally, a commitment the Bureau is monitoring (Bennett Jones, 2025; Competition Bureau, 2026).

**Live verifiers.** Price is live as an observation and dead as a selection. Every shopper reads the shelf daily, which is why the grocery store sits at the zero end of the axis (Chapter 3); but price selects only through exit, and exit was the verifier the covenants killed. Hirschman's exit needs a destination, and a covenant on the abandoned site plus an exclusivity clause on the occupied one removed the destination before it could be built. That is a private veto point in the sense of Chapter 4, an ex ante block on generation written into a land title instead of an environmental statute, and it carries the veto's signature: the cost of a rival's absence with no verdict on the incumbent's shelf. The merger verifier, the Tribunal reviewing an acquisition, was decorative in the exact sense of Chapter 1: it could find that a merger substantially lessened competition and be obliged by the efficiencies defense to approve it, the verdict overridden by a calculation the merging firm supplied. Benchmark fired late and from outside: the cross-border comparison with American shelves and the Bureau's own study were the benchmark that finally ran, and harmonized unit pricing is a benchmark instrument aimed at the shopper. Election/press fired in March 2023, when the House of Commons agriculture committee called the chains' executives to testify (National Observer, 2023) and the market study followed, but election/press is a noisy verifier of merger policy, and it fired twenty years after the covenants were written. Tender, export, and referendum do not apply. Bankruptcy is live for independents, which is why there were fewer of them each year, and has never reached a major.

**Bindingness.** Consequences bound the wrong party. The independent that lost customers closed; the major that raised a price lost customers to nobody, since the rival site was covenanted, so the shopper's verdict changed no allocation. The bread case is the symptom and the measure of how late a consequence arrives when exit is dead. Loblaw and George Weston agreed in July 2024 to a CAD 500 million class settlement (CAD 404 million in cash plus the CAD 96 million gift-card program of 2017) over alleged industry-wide coordination of packaged-bread prices from January 2001 to December 2021, approved by the Ontario Superior Court in May 2025, with payments reaching accounts in 2026 (Global News, 2025; CBC, 2024); Canada Bread pleaded guilty and paid a CAD 50 million fine in June 2023 (Public Prosecution Service of Canada, 2023). Bread is the cheapest good to verify in the cheapest sector to verify, and the coordination is alleged to have run for two decades; coordination on bread of the kind alleged is only possible where exit has already been disabled, since a shopper who could walk to a rival would have broken it in a season, and the arrangement surfaced through Loblaw's own disclosure under the Bureau's immunity program (CBC, 2017), a confession, not a verdict. The state-side failure of the same shape is dairy. Supply management sets a farm-gate milk price through the Canadian Dairy Commission, allocates production by quota, and blocks exit to imports with over-quota tariffs of well over 100 percent (McGill International Review, 2026), an administered price of exactly the kind Chapter 2 names as the captured instance; in June 2025 Parliament passed a statute forbidding the government from conceding on supply management in any trade negotiation (Bill C-202, 2025), a law whose content is that the verifier may not be restored. The remedy side shows the verifier versus veto distinction on the state's own ledger. The Grocery Code of Conduct, voluntary, in force from January 2026 with all five majors signed after Walmart and Costco held out until mid-2024, adjudicated by an Office of the Grocery Sector Code of Conduct, governs retailer-supplier practices, and its own commentators expect its effects to be invisible to consumers (Global News, 2025; USDA, 2026); it is a rule-thicket intervention, conduct regulation by volume with an adjudicator and no channel to the shelf. The covenant guidelines and the efficiencies repeal are the other kind: they remove a private veto point and turn a decorative verifier binding, after which price does the rest and no adjudicator is needed.

**The component.** Exit was killed by private contract, a covenant on a land title, which is the same mechanism as the state grocer's ban on rivals: the Soviet shop (Chapter 11) faced no competitor because the state forbade one, the Canadian shopper's rival store did not exist because a restrictive covenant held the site for the incumbent, and the shelf rotted the same way in the cheapest-to-verify sector in the world (P2).

**Prediction.** If the exit verifier is restored, meaning property controls are actually removed under the Bureau's guidelines and Loblaw's national commitment, expect entry by independent and foreign-owned grocers into previously covered sites and a measurable narrowing of the majors' grocery margins within five years; if the controls survive as lease exclusivities under another name while the Code of Conduct operates, expect no measurable change in shelf prices within the same period, whatever the adjudicator rules.

### Japanese Zombie Banking

**Verdict.** Partial loop, decaying for a decade after 1991 and closing after forced loss recognition in 2002, cross-referenced here from the Japan case (Chapter 10) for one addition only: the mechanics by which a private consequence goes soft with no ministry in the room.

**Operated and structured.** The state operated nothing in this sector. Banks were private, borrowers were private, and the loans that kept insolvent firms alive were private contracts renewed by private loan officers. What the state structured was the bank's own constraint: it insured deposits and guaranteed them in full from 1996, it demonstrated by recapitalizations in 1998 and 1999 that a large bank would not be allowed to fail (Bank for International Settlements, 2001), and it let supervisors forbear, accepting the bank's own classification of its loans as performing. The steel-man is a recession argument. A bank that calls a loan on a solvent but illiquid borrower in a slump destroys value that patience would have preserved, and a supervisor who forces every bank to recognize its losses at once in a falling land market turns a recession into a systemic collapse; forbearance buys time for asset prices to recover and for firms to grow back into their debt. Both halves are true in a recession and false in a decade, and nothing in the arrangement could tell which it was in.

**Live verifiers.** Price, export, and election/press were alive, which is why the case matters. Export kept manufacturing honest: Caballero, Hoshi, and Kashyap find the zombie share in manufacturing rising from 3.11 percent (the 1981 to 1993 average) to 9.58 percent (1996 to 2002) while construction went from 4.47 to 20.35 percent, with real estate, wholesale and retail, and services similarly hit (Caballero, Hoshi, and Kashyap, 2008); the rot concentrated where no world buyer verified. Bankruptcy was decorative for the bank, by expected rescue, and therefore for its borrowers. Exit was disabled on the liability side: an insured depositor has no reason to leave a bad bank, so the run, the cheapest verifier a bank has, never fired. Benchmark, the capital ratio, suffered Goodhart decay, because the classifier of the loans that entered the ratio was the lender whose ratio it was.

**Bindingness.** The mechanics are three moves. First, the roll: when a borrower cannot pay, the bank extends a new loan large enough to cover the interest on the old one, so the old loan stays current, no provision is booked, and the capital ratio holds; Caballero, Hoshi, and Kashyap identify zombies by exactly this subsidy, interest paid below what a borrower of that quality would have faced, and find the share of listed firms so subsidized hovered between 5 and 15 percent until 1993 and stood above 25 percent in every year after 1994 (Caballero, Hoshi, and Kashyap, 2008). Second, the bank's own soft constraint (Kornai): recognizing the loss would breach the ratio, breaching the ratio would force recapitalization or closure, and the bank expected, correctly, that the state would not let closure happen, so the cheapest course was to defer and be rescued if deferral failed. Third, propagation: a generator whose own consequence is soft cannot afford to harden anyone else's, so the softness traveled down the balance sheet from the guaranteed bank to the insolvent borrower and out into the sector, where subsidized incumbents held prices and wages at levels healthy firms could not match and depressed their investment and employment growth, which is the congestion Caballero, Hoshi, and Kashyap measured. The loop closed when the 2002 program made banks recognize losses on the supervisor's classification rather than their own (the Takenaka program of October 2002). The state-side failure of the same shape is the planning ministry that refinances its loss-making enterprise (the USSR case, Chapter 11), and the shape recurs inside a closed loop at the Landesbanken (the Germany case, Chapter 9). Across 14 advanced economies the share of zombie firms rose from about 2 percent in the late 1980s to some 12 percent in 2016 (BIS, 2018), so the mechanism is not Japanese.

**The component.** Deposit insurance and expected rescue softened the bank's own consequence, and a generator with a soft constraint passes softness downstream, so private loans went soft with private ownership at every link in the chain: Kornai's mechanism without a socialist in it (P2).

**Prediction.** The Bank of Japan ended negative rates in March 2024 and reached 1.0 percent in June 2026, the highest since 1995 (CNBC, 2024; CNBC, 2026), the highest cost of rolling a loan in a generation. If the bankruptcy verifier is left live, meaning no return of the forbearance guidance for small-firm lending issued in 2009 under the SME Financing Facilitation Act (IMF, 2012), expect corporate insolvencies to rise and the zombie share on the BIS measure to fall within three years; if the guidance returns, expect the zombie share to hold through the tightening.

### Academic Peer Review

**Verdict.** Open loop, decaying for as long as career consequences have been keyed to the verdict rather than to the finding, with one audit run since 2015 whose result has not yet been attached to anything.

**Operated and structured.** Journals, most of them owned by a handful of commercial publishers, operate the verifier; universities and funding agencies structure the consequence, hiring, tenure, and grants, on the verifier's output; and the generator, the researcher, chooses the venue. The verifier itself is a referee chosen by an editor from the generator's own peers, unpaid, usually anonymous, working after hours, who issues one binary verdict before publication and is never told whether the finding held. The steel-man is a cost argument. Expertise at a frontier cannot be bought cheaply, since only the handful of people working on a problem can judge a claim about it, and the commons of reviewing, each scientist refereeing for others as others referee for her, is the only way to run that verifier at all; the substitutes are either noisier (citation counts) or so expensive (replication) that no funder pays for them at scale. The steel-man is correct about cost and silent about consequence.

**Live verifiers.** Price is absent, and where a price exists it runs the wrong way: an article processing charge is paid by the generator to the verifier's employer, which is the issuer-pays arrangement of the rating agencies (the rating agencies case, Chapter 12), and it produced the same exposure: Hindawi, a publisher paid per accepted paper, became the site of the largest paper-mill clean-up on record, retracting 320 papers per 10,000 published against 9.06 at Springer Nature (Retraction Watch, 2026). Benchmark is live and in Goodhart decay: citation counts, journal impact factors, and the h-index are metrics the generator optimizes directly, by salami slicing, citation cartels, and paper mills, and a metric the ranked unit can manufacture decays by design (Chapter 15). Exit is weak because the reader who acts on a false finding, a clinician, a policy maker, a graduate student, has no other literature to leave to and learns of the error years later if at all. Bankruptcy exists as retraction and is operated by the interested party, the publisher: retractions passed 10,000 in 2023, driven by paper-mill clean-ups at Hindawi (Nature, 2023), the Retraction Watch database held just under 55,000 entries at the end of 2024 (Retraction Watch, 2024) and Springer Nature alone retracted 2,923 papers in that year (Retraction Watch, 2025), figures that measure the audit's reach and not the error rate, since the publisher that retracts least also reinstates most. Export exists in one place, industry, which has to make the finding work and reports that it often does not, as Amgen did in reproducing six of 53 landmark cancer studies (Begley and Ellis, Nature, 2012). Election/press fires when a scandal is large enough to be a story. Tender exists on the state side as the grant panel, where the same peers, unpaid and ex ante, decide what may be generated at all; that is a veto point and not a verifier (Chapter 4), since it blocks generation on a forecast and evaluates no completed output.

**Bindingness.** The verdict binds the generator's career and binds nothing about the finding. A positive verdict is worth a job; a wrong verdict costs the referee nothing, since she is anonymous and unpaid, costs the journal nothing, since its impact factor counts citations to a retracted paper as it counts any other, and costs the author, if at all, a decade later. The audit that finally ran measured the gap. The Open Science Collaboration replicated 100 psychology studies and found 97 percent of the originals reporting significant effects against 36 percent of the replications, with effect sizes roughly halved (Science, 2015); a Nature survey of 1,576 researchers found more than 70 percent had failed to reproduce another scientist's experiment, more than half had failed to reproduce their own, and 52 percent saw a significant crisis (Nature, 2016). The replications were done once, by volunteers, and no rule attaches their result to a tenure or funding decision; an audit whose result attaches to no allocation is itself decorative, and the soft constraint (Kornai) sits in the university, which will not unmake a career on a failed replication. The state-side failure of the same shape is the funding panel, the ex ante twin of the referee, and at the limit Lysenko, a science verifier captured by the state that paid it (the USSR case, Chapter 11); the market-side shape is the issuer-paid rating, already filed in this chapter. This manuscript's cases are verified by the same slow verifier, the reader and the literature it cites, and the ledger at the back of the book is an admission that the verifier has not yet run.

**The component.** The verifier has no consequence attached on either side, nothing for a right verdict and nothing for a wrong one, so the verdict binds the generator's career while verifying nothing about the output, and a verdict that the next paper, the next referee, or the generator's choice of venue can override at no cost to anyone is decorative (P2).

**Prediction.** If a binding consequence is attached to the verdict's accuracy in a field, meaning funded replication whose outcome is recorded against the publishing journal and its referees, or registered reports in which the verdict precedes the result and cannot be revised by it, expect that field's replication rate to rise clearly above the 36 percent baseline of 2015 within a decade; if consequences stay keyed to publication count and retraction stays at the publisher's discretion, expect the retraction total to keep rising with no rise in the replication rate.

## Chapter 13. The Verdict of the Stress Tests

The atlas closed on a replication run once, by volunteers, whose result attached to nothing. This chapter is the replication the book scheduled for itself, and unlike the audit peer review never made binding, the pass marks were written before the cases were run and are checked here as written. Six cases were chosen to kill the theory. None did. None survived cleanly either; the margins run from a case that strengthened the theory to one that survives by a margin the book would refuse from a case chosen to confirm. A tally that reported only the survivals would be a verdict written by the generator.

India survives, on two distinctions of unequal cleanliness. The bar was that the noisy verifier and the veto point had to have been defined before India and not for it. Vetocracy meets the bar without remainder: Chapter 4 built it on CEQA and the Swiss referendum, and the License Raj enters only as an application after the definition. The noisy verifier is less clean, and Chapter 8 said so in advance: Chapter 3 defines the concept eight chapters before the case, but opens by saying it is needed because of India and illustrates it with Indian elections, so it was defined before the case, on the case's material. What rescues it is the stricter test Chapter 8 set for exactly that situation, a concept defined on one case and predicting in several, and the noisy verifier does work where there is no India. It is Madison's reason for "auxiliary precautions" (the United States case, Chapter 10), and it is why a Greek election reset the deficit figure at each changeover and could not verify a quarterly number (the Greece case, Chapter 11). The case kept its side of the bargain, "uses both as defined there and adds no term to either," and its ledger is the law read precisely: one live verifier resolving regimes and not licensing regimes, and seven dead or never reached because "no output could be generated without permission" (the India case, Chapter 11). The component is P6: the licensing office grew as a generator nobody could verify, and it was removed in 1991 by demolition rather than verdict because a veto point has no verifier of its own. Two facts temper the margin: the bankruptcy verifier of 2016 is decaying on its timing leg, closed cases averaging 853 days against a statutory 330 (IBBI via IBC Laws, 2025), and every per-capita statistic in the country rests on a census sixteen years old (Deccan Herald, 2025). The closing loop is not closed.

Japan strengthens the theory, and it is the only one of the six that does. The bar was that bindingness be shown to be a separate leg: a country with live verifiers that refused the consequence and rotted anyway, as against a refusal that was a verifier failure in disguise. The case's finding is one sentence and the tally adopts it: "The consequence leg failed alone, and it failed in the private sector first" (the Japan case, Chapter 10). Every verifier fired, the electorate removed a government in 1993, and the share of listed firms on subsidized credit rose from between 5 and 15 percent until 1993 to above 25 percent in every year after 1994 (Caballero, Hoshi, and Kashyap, 2008). The disguise objection is that bankruptcy, one of the eight, was dead. The answer is in the mechanics: a bankruptcy court rules only if someone files, and the party who declined to file was the party holding the verdict, the bank with the loss in hand. The verdict existed; the allocation did not change. That is a consequence cancelled, not a verdict falsified, and the zombie banking case (Chapter 12) supplies the private mechanics: the roll, the bank's own soft constraint (Kornai's term for a consequence that will predictably be cancelled), and the propagation downstream. A system, the case concludes, "can verify perfectly and refuse to act, and when it does, it rots on the same schedule and with the same symptoms" as one that never verified at all (the Japan case, Chapter 10). The margin is the widest of the six.

The United States survives, and the veto distinction carries the weight the bar demanded, on one condition still running. The bar was that veto density, not verifier absence, predict the sclerosis. The evidence is a within-nation pair under one constitution and one price verifier: a median sale price of USD 357,805 in Houston against USD 1,699,075 in San Francisco (Redfin, 2026), and a 627-day median to a full multifamily permit in San Francisco, up 83 percent in a decade (San Francisco Chronicle, 2023). The verifiers in those sectors are live and binding, and the output they would judge is blocked before it reaches them; the profile is "neither Japan's (verifiers live, consequences cancelled) nor India's (verifiers noisy, the Raj a pure veto)" but verifiers live and a generation increasingly prevented from producing (the United States case, Chapter 10). Two American sectors decayed by verifier death, the rating agencies and health insurance, and the case names them as such and sends them to Chapter 12; the sector-granularity delta of Chapter 3 lets one country carry both mechanisms without the distinction going decorative. The system's own conduct in 2025, substantial deference on NEPA documents (Supreme Court of the United States, 2025) and a CEQA exemption for infill housing (Holland & Knight, 2025), is a court and a legislature removing output from a process they concluded yields cost without verdict. The condition is the case's own: "where veto density falls, output must respond, because if it does not, something other than the veto was the predictor," with five years and a number attached, the San Francisco permit median falling well below 627 days. The tally records survival with the condition.

China survives at the stated bar with the weakest margin of the six. The bar was that benchmark plus export be accepted as sufficient with the press dead, and that the deceleration after 2012 follow their decay. On the first clause the case pays in full: the provincial tournament was a benchmark with a career attached (Li and Zhou, 2005), and the coast was an export platform graded by buyers who could not be promoted. On the second, the National Bureau of Statistics had corrected provincial figures downward by about 5 percent of GDP and after 2008 stopped keeping pace, so that growth for 2010 to 2016 ran about 1.8 points a year below the reported figure (Chen, Chen, Hsieh, and Song, 2019); the youth unemployment series was suspended in August 2023 and resumed redefined (Time, 2023), and hundreds of series have since stopped (China Digital Times, 2025), which is the death order Chapter 15 states, bindingness first, independence second, cost last. Three things thin the margin. The account of the rise rests on two verifiers, the press dead throughout, so the rise has no third reading. One of the two was reported by the generator it ranked, so "part of the rise it explains was a measurement the generator wrote," and it counts as live only for the years the center could and did correct it (the China case, Chapter 10). And the decay was predicted rather than discovered: a verifier the generator can write has a scheduled death, so the theory's most striking prediction here is one it could not have gotten wrong. The forensic figure is contested at the decimal (Federal Reserve, 2025), and the case rests on the 2008 date rather than the number; demography and the exhaustion of catch-up get their one clause as residuals and remain a rival account the case can only date against, not exclude. A China decelerating with its benchmark intact would have refuted the book; what happened is consistent with the book and with at least one other story, which is what a weakest margin looks like.

The Nordic trust objection is met by conceding trust as a multiplier, which Chapter 6 had already done, and by showing that loops decide direction inside the family. The bar was within-Nordic variation on reform timelines. Under one statute and one culture, the share waiting more than 90 days for a first specialist contact runs from about 4 percent in Stockholm to 38 percent in Västerbotten, and no region meets the guarantee (Vårdföretagarna, 2026, an industry association); "trust could raise every region's performance by a constant; it cannot produce a nine-fold spread under one law" (the Nordics case, Chapter 9). Where the consequence is automatic and lands on the failing region's budget, Denmark's 30-day rule (borger.dk, 2024; Aleris, 2025), the deadline binds; where the failing region administers its own remedy, the deadline is a published number with a soft constraint attached. Sweden's contracted job matching left the unemployed no faster than the public service, at higher cost (IFAU, 2024), a tender firing on a choice mechanism with no exit behind it. And the timelines run against the objection: the Swedish crisis of the early 1990s arrived in the highest-trust society then measured, the verifiers came after it, with the budget process overhauled in 1997 and the Riksbank made independent in 1999 (Successful Public Policy in the Nordic Countries, 2022; Riksbank, undated), and three countries with one culture reformed at three dates, and in Sweden, the one with a crisis to date the change by, performance followed the verifiers rather than the culture. The concession costs the theory nothing it claimed; the margin is adequate and rests partly on dates the ledger now sources.

The Gulf requires the imported-verifier extension, and the extension is adopted, with limits, because it does predictive work in four places with no oil in them. The bar was an insight if it explains Greece and Korea, an epicycle if it explains the Gulf alone. On the domestic ledger the United Arab Emirates is "a Venezuela with better weather," and non-oil output at roughly 79 percent of the total in 2025 (The National, 2026) refutes that reading (the Gulf case, Chapter 10). The case predicted variation inside the Gulf that oil alone would not produce: Mubadala, which borrows abroad from creditors who can refuse, scores 88 percent on the Global SWF governance scoreboard, and ADIA, with no creditor, scores 52 (Global SWF, 2026). In Greece every domestic instrument was in place and inert, and the loop closed only when Eurostat read the books and produced 15.4 percent (Eurostat via Dow Jones, 2010); what the domestic system did with an honest verifier, prosecuting its statistician for a procedural breach, is the case's component (the Greece case, Chapter 11). In Korea the developmental state "borrowed the one verifier no domestic actor could reach," and when the domestic loop exempted leverage the verdict arrived in 1997 in the same verifier's capital-account form (the South Korea and Taiwan case, Chapter 10). In the accession states conditionality bound while the reward was ahead and collapsed on membership, with Hungary and Poland reversing afterward (Schimmelfennig and Sedelmeier, 2019), exactly what a verifier that stops being imported should do (the European Union case, Chapter 10). Estonia borrowed Eurostat's surveillance because it was too small to build the equivalent (the Estonia case, Chapter 9). Rwanda supplies the limit: the World Bank could verify the statistics office's arithmetic and not its data, and the Doing Business index was withdrawn after a generator large enough to lean on it did so (World Bank, 2021), so an imported verifier that reads the generator's own numbers, or that a large generator can lean on, was never independent for the small one (the Rwanda case, Chapter 10). The limits adopted are the Gulf case's own: the imported verifier must be named before the outcome, with the party who can refuse and the consequence that follows; it verifies only what it reads, so export grades catch-up and not invention; it stops when the import stops; and it is not a ninth verifier but export read at its full width, from goods to capital, talent, firms, and reputation, so the eight remain eight. Had it worked only in the Gulf it would have been dropped and the Gulf left as a wound. It works in four places and fails in a fifth for a reason it predicts, which is the shape of a finding.

| Stress test | Bar (Chapter 8) | Result | Margin |
|---|---|---|---|
| India | Distinctions defined before the case, not for it | Survives; vetocracy defined on California, noisy verifier defined before the case on Indian material, predicting elsewhere | Adequate |
| Gulf | Imported verifier predicts beyond the Gulf | Extension adopted; works in Greece, Korea, EU accession, Estonia; fails in Rwanda as predicted | Adopted, four limits |
| Japan 1990s | Bindingness a separate leg | Strengthens; consequence failed alone, verifiers live | Widest |
| Nordic trust | Within-Nordic variation on reform timelines | Trust conceded as multiplier; nine-fold spread under one statute; Swedish performance follows its post-crisis verifiers | Adequate, dates sourced |
| China 1980 to 2010 | Benchmark plus export sufficient; deceleration follows decay | Survives; two verifiers, one self-reported, decay dated to 2008 | Weakest |
| United States | Veto density, not verifier absence, predicts sclerosis | Survives; Houston against San Francisco; verifier-death sectors sent to Chapter 12 | Real, conditional on output responding |

The atlas did three things to the theory beyond the six. Every closed loop carried a named soft spot in its fourth field: the Swiss consultation converting an ex post verifier into an ex ante block, Singapore's press decorative by design, Sweden's administered deadlines, the Landesbanken and a railway verified by every clock and bound by nothing, New Zealand's mandate rewritten twice in five years, Estonia's scale, the Dutch nitrogen freeze, Houston's price unable to see a bayou the federal insurance program had taken off the invoice. Success is never overdetermined (Chapter 6), and the closed loops made it exact: each names the verifier whose failure would open it and times its prediction on it. Chapter 12 did the symmetric work, and the symmetry is structural: the loop opened by an intermediary stack is "Gosplan with a billing department"; the verifier paid by the party it judges is the same object as a statistical office inside the ministry whose deficit it reports; exit killed by a covenant on a land title is the Soviet ban on a second shop; the bank rolling an insolvent borrower is Kornai's mechanism "without a socialist in it." And the commitment of Chapter 6 that a state running the zero end of the axis well with exit and price disabled would refute the operator rule met only failures: the Soviet queue, the Venezuelan shelf rationed by identity card, the Cuban bodega, and, from below, North Korea's jangmadang forming a price where the state had abolished one.

The cases strained the vocabulary in four places, recorded here rather than smoothed. China's benchmark had to be treated as live and captured at once, live from 1980 to 2008 with "a captured component from the start" (the China case, Chapter 10); the labels are binary and the tournament was not, and the case resolved it by dating the capture to the year the center lost the ability to correct, and "live" is doing continuous work under a discrete label. Bankruptcy sits on both sides of the loop: Chapter 8 listed it among the eight and said it "turns decorative first, because a consequence is cheaper to cancel than a verifier is to capture," and Japan showed why, since the verdict and the filing are held by the same party; the book keeps it among the eight and reads it as the verifier whose life is a consequence. Referendum had to be split: Switzerland's optional referendum on a passed statute is a verifier, its popular initiative is a generator of constitutional text and an ex ante block once passed, and Chile's electorate used the referendum ex post to refuse two drafts that would have written new vetoes (the Chile case, Chapter 10), so every governance case had to say which half of the label it meant. And "captured" and "decorative" were used as neighbors, Singapore's press labeled both; the distinction held where it mattered, and Argentina drew the sharper line beside it, between captured and dead: "a captured verifier is worse than a dead one" (the Argentina case, Chapter 11). None of the strains touches a proposition; they mark where a vocabulary built for a book met cases not built for it.

System performance is determined by the cost, independence, and bindingness of verification. The sentence stood before the atlas and stands after it, with its margins now known: widest where consequences were tested, thinnest where the theory accepted a self-reported number as a verifier for as long as someone corrected it, and extended once, by a reading of export that had to earn its place in four places before it was allowed one in the Gulf.

What the closed loops shared is where Part III opens. None of them governed with a thick instrument. Switzerland runs its state on a vote taken after the statute exists and a tax rate printed beside its neighbors'. Singapore houses more than three quarters of its resident households through a tender result posted on a procurement portal. Denmark holds its hospitals to a 30-day deadline; Norway holds its oil to a published list of holdings. New Zealand's governor signed a number. Houston lets a price move. A rate, a price, a tender, a deadline, a published number: each is a signal the loop already read, and the state's contribution was to choose the thinnest one and then decline to soften what it said. Part III catalogs them, one instance each, none as a blueprint. The first sits at the end of the Japan case. On 16 June 2026 the Bank of Japan published a number, 1.0 percent, the highest since 1995 (CNBC, 2026), one line long, read that afternoon by every borrower in the country, and for the first time in a generation it turned a loan that could not be serviced into a verdict that someone would have to act on or cancel.

# Part III: The Signal Library

## Chapter 14. The Signal Library

The number the Bank of Japan posted on 16 June 2026 was one line long, and every borrower in the country had read it by evening. That is what a thin instrument looks like, and the closed loops of the atlas share the feature the tally in Chapter 13 recorded and left standing: every one of them intervenes, several of them heavily, and the interventions that held were thin. Germany's ordoliberals wrote the rules of a market and then handed the one price they would not leave to it, the price of money, to an institution the government could not instruct. Singapore charges for road space by the minute and forces saving at a rate it publishes. New Zealand wrote a number into an agreement and made one man answerable for it. Norway publishes every holding of its fund twice a year and lets the world read the list. Where the closed loops operate directly, the Housing and Development Board (HDB), the French nuclear fleet, the Nordic hospital, they do so at the bottom of the axis and through tenders. The instruments were thin because a live loop can read a thin instrument and cannot read a thick one, and the states that keep their loops closed have learned, mostly by accident and occasionally by design, to speak to the loop in its own language.

The signal rule (P5), stated in Chapter 3, is the whole of that lesson: when intervening, prefer the thinnest instrument the loop can read. Three kinds of intervention follow, and the book names them. A signal intervention is state action transmitted through a channel the loop already reads: an interest rate, a carbon price, a cash transfer, an auction. An operational intervention is the state running the thing. A rule-thicket intervention is conduct regulation by volume. A signal intervention rides a live verifier, an operational intervention needs one, and a rule-thicket intervention replaces one, and the difference between the three is not the size of the state or the strength of its preference but the verification cost the intervention inherits. A signal inherits the cost of its channel, which for price, tender, exit, and benchmark is near zero, so the state learns within weeks whether the instrument worked and pays nothing for the lesson. A mandate, an in-kind program, a directed loan, or a license produces an output that must be verified on its own terms, which is the expensive path Chapter 3 described, and the administration that grows around it is the complexity Chapter 5 predicted where output cannot be verified (P6).

Three properties make an instrument thin. It is one number, or one rule that reduces to a number. Every participant reads it without translation, because it arrives inside a price, a bill, a bid, or a bank balance the participant was already reading. And the participant's response is verified by the same verifier that carried the signal, so the instrument's own success is checked by the loop for free. A technology mandate fails all three: it is a schedule, it is read by lawyers, and compliance with it is an output nobody prices. The asymmetric law (P7) applies to instruments as it applies to systems: a thin instrument is a necessary condition for cheap learning, not a guarantee of a good decision. A wrong interest rate is still wrong. What distinguishes it from a wrong credit-allocation plan is that the bond market says so the same afternoon, and the plan's failure surfaces in a bank's books a decade later, by which time the planner has retired.

Thinness has one cost, and the cost is the reason every entry below ends the way it does. A single number can be redefined, exempted, or overridden in a single sentence. A thicket of ten thousand pages defends itself by its bulk; nobody can find the clause to repeal, and nobody can see what it costs. A rate, a target, a transfer, or a price is legible to everyone, including the coalition that wants it gone, and it dies by the same legibility that made it work. The library is therefore also a catalog of deaths, and Chapter 15 will show that they follow a fixed order.

Every instrument here is described through an instance that already happened, and none as a plan; a blueprint is an output whose verification cost sits at the industrial-strategy end of the axis, and the book would not trust its own. Each entry runs the same five fields as flowing prose: the instrument in one sentence, the channel (which existing verifier reads it), what it replaced (the operational or rule-thicket alternative it displaced), what verifies the instrument itself, and the capture mode by which the signal dies.

### Interest Rates and Central Bank Independence

**Instrument.** A single policy rate, set by an institution the treasury cannot instruct, is the thinnest intervention any state has run at scale: one number, changed a few times a year, read by every borrower and lender in the currency within the hour.

**Channel.** Price. The policy rate enters every loan, bond, mortgage, and discount rate in the economy without anyone needing to know why it moved; Hayek's engineer watching a few dials (Hayek, 1945) is watching this one first. The Bundesbank is the archetype because it was built to keep the number out of the hands of the one generator with a motive to misread it. The Bank deutscher Länder of March 1948 was created by the occupying powers before the Federal Republic existed (Hetzel, 2002), and the Bundesbank Act of 26 July 1957 made its successor independent of government instruction (Bundesbank, 2025). The independence was tested early: in 1956 Adenauer denounced a discount-rate increase in a speech at Cologne as a guillotine for the man in the street (Hetzel, 2002), and the bank held the rate. It held again, with less success, when Kohl imposed a one-to-one conversion rate for East German marks in 1990 over the bank's argument for two to one, and Pöhl's resignation in May 1991 (Hetzel, 2002) is the instance in which the statute lost and the bond market noticed. The memory of 1923 and 1948 is a residual the lens does not claim; what the lens claims is that the memory was attached to an instrument that could not be overridden by a minister, and that the instrument, not the memory, is what other countries copied. The general case followed the archetype by four decades: Alesina and Summers found in 1993 that independence correlated with low inflation and nothing else (Alesina and Summers, 1993), the Bank of England was given operational independence in May 1997 (JURIST, 2009), and the European Central Bank was written in 1998 on the Bundesbank's statute (Bundesbank, 2023). Independence is not a verifier. The central bank is a generator whose output, the rate, is verified by the bond market and the inflation print; independence is a design that keeps the generator's owner from setting the number the owner needs to read.

**Replaced.** Credit allocation and directed lending: the state deciding who borrows rather than at what price. France ran quantitative ceilings on bank lending, the encadrement du crédit, until 1987 (Bourse des Crédits, undated), Korea directed credit to chaebol against export targets (Amsden, 1989), India still obliges banks to lend 40 percent of net credit to priority sectors (RBI, undated), and the United States capped deposit rates under Regulation Q until a phase-out that ran from 1980 to 1986 (Federal Reserve History, undated). Every directed loan requires a bureau to verify the borrower's merit ex ante, which is verification at the industrial-strategy end of the axis; a rate requires nobody to verify anything, because the borrower who cannot pay it does not borrow. The failure of a directed loan is hidden inside the lender's balance sheet until the crisis reads it out loud: Korea in 1997, and the German Landesbanken, the domestic survival of directed credit inside the archetype's own country, whose 2007 losses cost taxpayers roughly EUR 50 billion for the Landesbanken and above EUR 70 billion with the private rescues of HRE and IKB, on Hellwig's tally (Hellwig, 2018) and which the Germany case (Chapter 9) names as its soft spot. The market runs the same failure without a ministry. A cartel that sets the price of credit has captured the verifier from the other side: LIBOR was the rate on contracts worth hundreds of trillions of dollars (CFR, 2016), set daily by a panel of the banks whose positions depended on it, and the fines that began with Barclays in June 2012 (CFTC, 2012) recorded that the panel had written the number it was supposed to be judged by; the last panel ceased on 30 June 2023 (FCA, 2023). The Japanese main banks that lent to zombies at subsidized rates through the 1990s (Caballero, Hoshi, and Kashyap, 2008) were allocating credit by relationship rather than by price, a private directed-lending regime with a private soft constraint (Kornai's term) underneath it.

**Verified by.** Inflation outcomes and the bond market, neither of which the bank controls. The print arrives monthly from a statistical office the bank does not run, and the bond market prices the bank's credibility every trading day in the term premium and the breakeven, a verdict no communiqué can revise. The consequence binds through the currency: the mark became the anchor of the European Monetary System from 1979 (Hetzel, 2002) because forty years of prints had verified the generator, and a bank that loses the print loses the exchange rate before it loses an argument. The asymmetric law is visible inside the archetype. The Bundesbank published money-supply targets from 1975 and missed them in roughly half the years, nine of the twenty from 1979 to 1998 (Neumann, undated), and its credibility survived because the outcome verifier, inflation, forgave the intermediate one; the instrument enabled the record and did not produce it.

**Capture mode.** Fiscal dominance, the political appointment, and the terminal case of the central bank financing the treasury. Fiscal dominance is the softening of bindingness: once the treasury's debt is large enough that a rate increase would bankrupt the treasury, the rate is set by the deficit whatever the statute says, which is Sargent and Wallace's arithmetic of 1981 (Sargent and Wallace, 1981) and the mechanism by which independence is lost without anyone repealing it. The appointment is the capture of independence: a statute is a sentence, and a governor who serves at pleasure sets the rate at pleasure. Nixon's pressure on Burns before the 1972 election, recorded on the tapes (Abrams, 2006), is the polite instance; Turkey, where three governors were removed between 2019 and 2021 for raising rates or refusing to cut them and inflation reached 85.5 percent in October 2022 (Daily Sabah, 2019; Arab News, 2021; Turkish Treasury, 2022), is the complete one. Argentina is the terminal case because it ran the sequence to the end. The Banco Central financed the treasury directly, so the rate was a fiction and the price level was the deficit's shadow; inflation reached 211.4 percent in 2023 (INDEC via MercoPress, 2025). The verifier went in the order Chapter 15 gives: captured from January 2007, censured by the IMF in February 2013, suspended in 2016 and relaunched that June (Buenos Aires Times, 2024; IMF, 2013; INDEC, 2017): the generator stopped publishing an honest print nine years before the print itself was suspended. The current program claims zero monetary financing since 2024, and a proposed charter would give the bank a single inflation mandate and a statutory ban on financing the state, unpassed as of July 2026 (Central Banking, 2026); the Argentina case (Chapter 11) holds the verdict open, because a ban that has not been tested by a deficit has not yet verified anything.

### Inflation Targeting

**Instrument.** A published numerical target for inflation, agreed between the government and the central bank and attached to a named person who answers for missing it.

**Channel.** Benchmark, carried by price. The target converts a central bank's output, which nobody could previously verify except by argument, into a number every wage negotiator, bond buyer, and shopkeeper can compare with the print for the cost of a newspaper, and the comparison is what anchors expectations: the generator has pre-registered its own test. New Zealand invented the instrument out of exhaustion. The Reserve Bank of New Zealand Act 1989 made price stability the statutory objective and created the Policy Targets Agreement; the first agreement, in March 1990, targeted inflation of 0 to 2 percent by December 1992 (RBNZ, 2025). Inflation had run in double digits through much of the 1980s, 17.2 percent in 1980 and 15.4 percent in 1985 (Works in Progress, undated), and the country had come out of the Muldoon wage and price freeze of 1982 to 1984 (Te Ara, undated) with nothing verified and everything controlled. The governor, Don Brash, signed the agreement personally, and the Act allowed his dismissal for failing to meet it (Works in Progress, undated); the steel-man of discretion, that a bank must be free to respond to shocks it cannot foresee, was answered by writing the shocks into the agreement as named exceptions rather than leaving the target unstated. Inflation was inside the band by December 1991, a year ahead of the deadline (Works in Progress, undated). The instrument's whole design is that the neck is named and the number is public.

**Replaced.** Discretionary monetary policy, whose only verifier was the next election, and the three thicker instruments that had stood in for it. The first was direct control: a wage and price freeze is an operational intervention in every price at once, verified by a tribunal that cannot read a million prices and captured by every applicant for an exemption, and New Zealand's own freeze is the local instance. The second was monetary targeting, the aggregate whose collapse under control pressure is the occasion of Goodhart's 1975 remark (Goodhart, 1975): the money supply was a benchmark the banking system could game as soon as it was targeted, and the Bundesbank's misses were the same regularity observed politely. An exchange-rate peg was the third alternative, an imported verifier borrowed from the anchor country's central bank, and its bill was presented in September 1992 when sterling left the European exchange-rate mechanism (Economics Observatory, undated). The target replaced a thousand controlled prices, one gameable aggregate, and one borrowed anchor with a single number the bank could be judged by and could not hide.

**Verified by.** The print against the band, at the frequency of the statistical office; the accountability documents the Act required, the twice-yearly Monetary Policy Statement chief among them (Works in Progress, undated); and the governor's tenure. The spread of the instrument was itself the benchmark verifier working across borders. Canada adopted a target in 1991, the United Kingdom in October 1992, Sweden and Australia in 1993 (Federal Reserve, 2025; BIS, 2003), the Federal Reserve announced an explicit 2 percent objective in January 2012 (Federal Reserve, 2012), and by the 2020s some forty central banks formally published one (Warwick Business School, undated); a central bank that refused to state a number was compared with those that did, and the comparison cost its bond market nothing to make. The asymmetric law holds here too. The overshoot of 2021 to 2023 (CEPR, 2024) occurred under published targets in every advanced economy, and the target did not prevent it; what it did was make the miss legible to the month, force a public answer to the word transitory, and leave the bank no story in which the miss had not occurred. The Bank of Japan ended negative rates in March 2024 and reached 1.0 percent in June 2026 (CNBC, 2024; CNBC, 2026) with a 2 percent target it had missed from below for a generation, which is the same instrument reporting the same kind of miss in the other direction.

**Capture mode.** Target redefinition, and the dual mandate as the softening that precedes it. New Zealand's own oscillation is the record: the band became 1 to 3 percent in 2002 with an explicit 2 percent midpoint from 2012; the 2018 amendment added maximum sustainable employment as a co-objective and replaced the agreement with a committee remit from April 2019; the Amendment Act of 2023, in force 20 December 2023, restored the single mandate (RBNZ, 2025; New Zealand Treasury, undated). The New Zealand case (Chapter 9) names that oscillation as its soft spot, and the mechanism is exact. A second objective that cannot be verified (maximum sustainable employment is an estimate the generator itself produces) gives the generator a second reading of every miss on the objective that can: inflation above the band is attributed to the employment mandate, and the verifier gains a clause and loses a verdict. Redefinition finishes the job. The Federal Reserve's 2020 framework redefined its target as an average over an unspecified window, so that no single print could constitute a miss, and withdrew the redefinition in 2025 (ABA Banking Journal, 2025); Argentina adopted a target in September 2016, raised it from 12 to 15 percent on 28 December 2017 when the print would not come down, and abandoned the regime in May 2018 (AIER, undated). The market runs the same capture inside the firm. A conglomerate that measures its divisions by profit and lets them negotiate the internal transfer price has let the generator set the target it is judged by, and its profit centers become decorative verifiers; the international version, transfer pricing that moves book profit to the jurisdiction with the lowest rate and the OECD's action plan against it of July 2013 (OECD, 2013), is the generator writing the number the tax verifier reads. A target set by the party it measures is not a target.

### Cash Transfers and the Negative Income Tax

**Instrument.** Money given to a person, unconditionally or against a test a tax return can verify, in place of goods, services, or a case worker.

**Channel.** Price and exit, at the zero end of the axis. The recipient spends the transfer in the grocery store, where she verifies every purchase, exits every bad one, and pays a public price, and the state verifies nothing except that the money arrived; the instrument rides the cheapest verifier in the world and adds no administration to it. Friedman proposed the form in 1962, in the chapter of Capitalism and Freedom on the alleviation of poverty (Econlib, undated): an income tax that runs negative below a threshold, paid out through the return that collects the positive tax, replacing the welfare apparatus with a schedule. The United States tested it in the only way a signal can be tested cleanly, by randomizing the dose: the New Jersey experiment from 1968, the Gary and rural experiments, and the Seattle and Denver experiments from 1970 (Institute for Research on Poverty, undated). The findings were a modest reduction in hours worked, about 9 percent for husbands and 18 percent for wives (Econlib, undated), and a reported rise in marital dissolution in Seattle and Denver that Cain and others later disputed (Econlib, undated); the point for the library is less the findings than that there were findings, since a cash dose can be measured and a case worker's judgment cannot. The instrument that survived the politics was the earned income tax credit, enacted in 1975 and expanded in 1986 and 1993 (CRS, undated), a negative tax on the working poor administered by the return. Brazil's Bolsa Família consolidated earlier transfers in 2003 and reached nearly 14 million households at a cost of about 0.6 percent of GDP (World Bank, 2013); Mexico's Progresa of 1997 was randomized from its launch, 320 treatment and 186 control communities (World Bank, undated). GiveDirectly's unconditional transfers in Kenya produced the recent evidence: Haushofer and Shapiro found gains in assets, consumption, and psychological well-being (Haushofer and Shapiro, 2016), Egger and colleagues found a local multiplier of 2.5 with minimal inflation (Egger and colleagues, 2022), and the Evans and Popova review found no rise in spending on alcohol and tobacco across the literature (Evans and Popova, 2017). The recipient, it turned out, was a competent verifier of her own consumption, which the grocery store had been demonstrating daily for a century.

**Replaced.** The in-kind bureaucracy in three grades. The case-worker state is the full form: an official assesses a family's need, allocates the good, and monitors the use, and the official's own output, the assessment, sits high on the axis where nobody verifies it, so the office grows by producing process (P6). Public housing allocation by waiting list and points is the operational form: the unit is chosen by the allocator, the recipient's price signal is replaced by a queue, and the queue is a verifier with no consequence attached, the same object Chapter 11 finds outside the Soviet shop. Food stamps are the intermediate case and the instructive one. For most recipients the benefit is smaller than their food spending, so it is spent as cash (Hoynes and Schanzenbach, 2009), and the instrument is nearly a transfer; yet it carries an eligible-item list, a retailer authorization regime, and a perennial fight over soda, and the administrative apparatus scales with the conditions rather than with the money. What an in-kind program destroys is the recipient's own verification: she cannot exit a housing unit she was allocated, cannot price a food basket she did not choose, and cannot tell the state that the good was wrong except through the case worker whose job depends on the good. The market runs the identical program without a ministry. Employer-provided health insurance in the United States, born of the wage controls of the 1940s and preserved by the tax exclusion of 1954 (Institute of Medicine, 1993), is a private in-kind transfer that replaced wages with a good whose price the recipient never sees and whose provider she cannot exit without leaving her job; the US health insurance case (Chapter 12) is its autopsy, and the shape is the same as the case-worker state's: the transferor chooses the good, the recipient loses the price, and administration fills the gap.

**Verified by.** The recipient at the point of purchase; the randomized trial, which the instrument permits because cash is a dose; and the tax return, which verifies earnings through third-party reports at near zero cost. The honest edge sits inside the earned income tax credit. Nearly a third of its payments were estimated improper in fiscal year 2023 (Taxpayer Advocate, 2024), and the error lives exactly in the field the return cannot read: earnings are verified cheaply by the employer's report, and the residency of a qualifying child is verified expensively by nothing. The instrument's error rate is a map of its verification costs, and the cheap half is clean. Bolsa Família is verified by school attendance and clinic records, which are counts, and by evaluations that found extreme poverty falling from 9.7 to 4.3 percent and the Gini coefficient down 15 percent over the program's first decade (World Bank, 2013), with attribution shared by the minimum wage and growth; the residual is conceded in that clause and the lens proceeds.

**Capture mode.** Conditionality creep, until the transfer is an in-kind program by regulation. Each condition (a work requirement, an asset test, a drug test, an approved-purchase list, a reporting interval) is a verification demand placed on the recipient at the cost of the sector it lands in, and each requires an office to check it, so the case worker returns one clause at a time under the cash program's name. Universal Credit in the United Kingdom was designed in 2010 as the consolidation of six benefits into one payment and by 2020 carried sanctions a House of Lords committee called among the most punitive in the world (House of Lords Economic Affairs Committee, 2020); the American food stamp program had its work requirements extended in 2023 and again in 2025, and states began obtaining waivers in May 2025 to bar the purchase of named items (Brookings, 2025; USDA, 2025), the eligible-item list growing into a menu. The conditions that survive the lens are the cheap ones: a school attendance count costs almost nothing to verify, and whether conditions add anything to the cash is contested in the evidence, the Malawi trial finding the unconditional arm did as well or better on several outcomes (Baird, McIntosh, and Özler, 2011). The instrument dies when the state stops trusting the grocery store to verify the recipient and installs an official to verify her instead, and the corollary of P6 states the result: simplification without a verifier fails, and the case worker regrows in the same dark.

### Carbon Price versus Technology Mandates

**Instrument.** A price per ton of carbon dioxide, levied as a tax or formed by auctioning a capped quantity of permits, so that every fuel bill carries the cost of the externality and every emitter decides for itself how to avoid it.

**Channel.** Price. Chapter 2 named the externality as the thing price does not count, and a carbon price is the externality added to the price so that the loop reads it as it reads any other cost, with no emitter needing to know the science. The European Union Emissions Trading System began in January 2005 and caps power and heavy industry, roughly two fifths of the Union's emissions (European Commission, undated). British Columbia introduced a carbon tax on 1 July 2008 at CAD 10 a ton, rising to CAD 30 by 2012, revenue-neutral by statute with every dollar returned through other tax cuts (Carbon Tax Center, 2015); the province's per-capita emissions fell relative to the rest of Canada with no measurable effect on aggregate output (Murray and Rivers, 2015). Sweden has taxed carbon since 1991 and the rate now exceeds SEK 1,400 a ton, above EUR 120 and among the highest in the world (Government Offices of Sweden, 2026; Tax Foundation, 2020), over a period in which emissions fell by a third and output roughly doubled, 33 percent down and 92 percent up between 1990 and 2021 (Swedish Ministry of Finance, 2023). In each instance the state chose no technology, licensed no plant, and subsidized no manufacturer; it changed one number in every fuel bill and let a hundred thousand generators find the abatement it could not have found for them.

**Replaced.** Technology mandates and subsidies by technology: the state choosing the abatement method and verifying compliance with the choice. The rule-thicket instance is the Energiewende's feed-in tariff. The Renewable Energy Sources Act of 2000 (BMWi, 2015) guaranteed each technology a fixed price for twenty years at a rate set by the legislature, 57.4 cents per kilowatt-hour for rooftop solar in 2004; the surcharge that paid for it reached 6.88 cents per kilowatt-hour on household bills in 2017 (Wikipedia, undated), the surcharge ran near EUR 25 billion a year (Clean Energy Wire, undated), one estimate put the cumulative cost through 2025 at EUR 520 billion (DICE, 2016), and the surcharge was abolished on 1 July 2022 (Wikipedia, undated). The mechanism of the cost is the point. A legislated tariff per technology is a forecast of that technology's cost, and the forecast for solar was falsified by the price of panels within a decade, but the state had promised the forecast for twenty years and paid it; a carbon price would have bought the same abatement from whichever technology was cheapest that year and would have paid less as panels fell. The steel-man is the learning curve: the German demand pulled panel costs down for the whole world, and the lens cannot dismiss that. It observes that the cost was verified by nobody at the time, that the benefit accrued in large part to Chinese manufacturers, whose modules felled Q-Cells, Solon, Conergy, and finally SolarWorld (Clean Energy Wire, 2017), and that a price would have reported the bill monthly instead of leaving it to a court of auditors two decades later. Fuel-economy standards in place of a fuel tax (Anderson and Sallee, 2016) are the same substitution in a smaller frame: a mandate inherits the verification cost of the technology sector, which is high, where the price inherits the cost of a fuel bill, which is zero.

**Verified by.** The price itself and the emissions inventory. A permit price is a daily verdict on the cap: a price near zero says the cap does not bind, and the ETS said so in public. Over-allocation in the first phase drove the price to zero by 2007; the crisis and free allocation kept it below EUR 10 through most of 2012 to 2017, at EUR 2.75 in 2013; the Market Stability Reserve from 2019 and tighter caps pushed it above EUR 80 in 2022 and to EUR 100 in early 2023 (Clean Energy Wire, undated), and emissions under the system stood roughly 47 percent below 2005 by 2023 (European Commission, undated). The point is not that the first two phases failed but that the failure was read by everyone, including the legislature, every trading day; a mandate's failure is discovered by audit, years later, by an office the mandate's authors fund. British Columbia's tax was verified by benchmark, the comparison with provinces that had none, and Sweden's by the inventory against the growth series. In a carbon price the verifier and the signal are the same object.

**Capture mode.** Free allocation, exemptions, and price corridors set by negotiation. Free allocation is the capture of bindingness: permits grandfathered to incumbents on the basis of past emissions are paid for by consumers and pocketed by generators, which is how utilities took windfall profits in the first phase, and the carbon-leakage list that placed most heavy industry on free allocation (Clean Energy Wire, undated) converted a price into a rent. Exemptions are the same move by sector: Swedish industry outside the trading system paid a fraction of the household rate for decades until the reduction was phased out by 2018 (Government Offices of Sweden, 2026), and the Energiewende's exemptions for energy-intensive firms, which paid 0.05 cents per kilowatt-hour, shifted the surcharge onto households (Wikipedia, undated), so that the instrument's cost fell on the one group that could not lobby it. A price corridor is the capture of the price itself: Germany's national fuel trading system from 2021 set a fixed price path by statute through 2025 (ICAP, undated), and a price written by a legislature is an administered price, the captured instance Chapter 2 named, capturable by the next coalition with a single vote. British Columbia shows the death that thinness invites. The revenue-neutrality was abandoned in the 2017 budget (Globe and Mail, 2017), and the consumer tax was repealed on 1 April 2025 the day the federal consumer carbon price was scrapped (Government of British Columbia, 2025): a signal instrument dies with one vote because it is one number that every voter can see on a receipt, and a mandate of the same cost survives because nobody can see what it costs. That exposure is the honest edge of the whole library. The market side runs the same capture with trees. Voluntary carbon offsets attach a price to carbon through a verifier, the registry, that is paid by the project developer it certifies, and a 2023 investigation found that more than 90 percent of the rainforest offsets approved under the leading standard were likely phantom credits (Guardian via Carbon Brief, 2023); a carbon price read through a captured verifier is the rating agency of Chapter 12 with a forest attached. The internal carbon prices firms declare, with no consequence attached to any decision, are the decorative version of the same number.

### Spectrum Auctions

**Instrument.** A spectrum auction is a tender built to make a price form for a public asset that never had one: the state sells the right to transmit on a band of frequencies to the bidder who values it most, under rules engineered so that the bid reveals the valuation rather than the bidder's guess about the regulator. The Federal Communications Commission ran the first in July 1994, selling ten nationwide narrowband PCS licenses for USD 617 million with the simultaneous ascending design of Milgrom, Wilson, and McAfee (Cramton, 1997); the first six FCC auctions raised about USD 20 billion (Cramton, 1995). The design's substance is that every license opens at once, bidding proceeds in rounds, an activity rule forces bidders to stay active or lose eligibility, and nothing closes until nothing moves, so a firm can assemble complementary licenses across regions and the price of each ends up carrying the value of the package. Mechanism design, the discipline of Hurwicz, Maskin, and Myerson (Chapter 7), had spent thirty years as a theory of engineered verifiers; the FCC put one into production, and the production run, not the theorem, is the instrument.

**Channel.** Tender, with price forming inside it. The ministry does not know what a license is worth to a firm that must build a network on it; the firm knows, and the auction extracts the knowledge by making a lie expensive, which is Hayek's point about the man on the spot (Chapter 2) with the spot moved into a bidding room.
