The United Kingdom's 3G auction of 2000, designed by Binmore and Klemperer, ran from 6 March to 27 April, 150 rounds, thirteen bidders for five licenses, and raised GBP 22.5 billion, more than every US spectrum auction of the previous six years combined (Cramton, NAO report, 2001; Binmore and Klemperer, 2002). The detail that carries the case is the fifth license, reserved for a new entrant so that the four incumbents could not settle the outcome among themselves; thirteen bidders was the design's verdict on that reservation, because a tender with no outsiders is a tender the insiders write. The channel is capturable in the way every tender is, from below, by the bidders, and the whole art is in making that capture costly.

**Replaced.** Comparative hearings and lotteries. A comparative hearing, the beauty contest, asks applicants to describe the network they will build and asks officials to judge which description is worthiest; it is an ex ante veto point of the Chapter 4 kind, since the output evaluated is a document optimized for its reader, the promise costs nothing to produce, and the decision is litigated by the losers for years. The United States used hearings, then from 1984 to 1989 lotteries for cellular licenses (Wikipedia, undated), and lottery winners resold their licenses to operators at prices that measured what the state had given away; the resale market was already pricing what the lottery ignored. Finland in 1999 and Spain and Sweden in 2000 awarded 3G licenses by beauty contest for nominal or no fees (RCR Wireless, 1999; Klemperer, 2002), and their operators received the same spectrum for a fraction of what British and German operators paid, which the operators called fairness and which was a transfer of the license's value from the treasury to the firm, decided by a ministry reading promises. A beauty contest is a verifier the generator writes.

**Verified by.** Revenue, then the build. Revenue verifies that a price formed; the build verifies that the price was survivable, which is a separate question. In Britain all five licensees built networks (Sutton and Linge, 2015), so both checks ran. The honest edge is that revenue is a noisy verifier of design quality, since the spring of 2000 was the top of the telecommunications bubble and any design would have raised more that year than the year after; Binmore and Klemperer's defense of the auction rests on the entrant reservation and the round structure, not on the sum, and that is the right place to rest it. Germany's auction of the same year raised roughly EUR 50 billion (heise, 2025) and two licensees, Quam in 2002 and Mobilcom in 2003, surrendered their licenses without building (Tagesspiegel, 2003), which is what a price that outran the build looks like. The asymmetric law (Chapter 6) applies to the instrument itself: an auction is a live verifier that enables, and a firm can still pay a true price for a technology whose demand arrives five years late.

**Capture mode.** Three modes, each observed. Bid signaling: in the FCC's DEF-block auctions of 1996 and 1997, bidders encoded the numbers of markets they wanted in the trailing digits of their bids on other markets, warning rivals off in a language the rules did not forbid (Cramton and Schwartz, 2000); this is the market-side failure of the same shape as the beauty contest, the bidders writing the verdict instead of the ministry, and the repair was click-box bidding in fixed increments and anonymous bidding, so that a bid could carry a number and nothing else. Set-asides with soft payment: the FCC's C block of 1996 was reserved for designated entities and sold on installment, so that winners paid a fraction up front and bid the rest with money they had not raised; NextWave bid about USD 4.74 billion, filed for bankruptcy in June 1998, and the litigation ran to the Supreme Court, which ruled for the bidder in January 2003 (FCC v. NextWave, 2003), while the spectrum sat dark. An installment bid is Kornai's soft constraint at auction: the price fired and the consequence did not bind, because the bidder could walk. Winner's curse followed by renegotiation: European licensees that overpaid in 2000 lobbied for longer terms, deferred or lower fees, and network sharing, and France, Ireland, Belgium, and Germany conceded one or another by 2002 (CMS, 2002); every state that conceded converted its auction into a beauty contest held after the fact, with the winner's promise replacing the winner's payment. The fourth mode is not capture but absence: Switzerland's 3G auction of December 2000 ended with four licenses and four bidders after pre-auction withdrawals and sold each at or near the CHF 50 million minimum (Wolfstetter, 2001), because a tender with one bidder per lot is a price with no exit behind it.

### Congestion Pricing

**Instrument.** A congestion charge is a price on road space at the hour it is scarce. Singapore's Area Licensing Scheme began in June 1975 as a paper license costing SGD 3 a day to enter the central zone during the morning peak, checked by wardens at the boundary; peak entries fell from 32,500 to 7,700, a drop of 76 percent, and transit's share of work trips rose from 33 percent to about 70 percent by 1983 (Wikipedia, undated). Electronic Road Pricing replaced the paper on 1 April 1998, with the scheme retired that September (AARoads, undated), turning a daily license into a per-pass charge that varies by gantry and half hour. ERP 2.0, a satellite system reading an on-board unit, had units in 930,000 vehicles, 93 percent of the fleet, by February 2026, began foreign-vehicle installation on 1 April 2026, makes units mandatory on 1 January 2027, and keeps distance-based charging under study (Malay Mail, 2026; LTA, 2026). London followed on 17 February 2003 at GBP 5 a day; vehicles entering the zone fell about 18 percent and measured congestion about 30 percent in the first phase (Defra/TfL assessment, 2005). Stockholm ran a seven-month trial from January to July 2006, put the charge to a municipal referendum on 17 September 2006, which the city approved and fourteen suburbs rejected, and made it permanent on 1 August 2007 (Wikipedia, undated). New York's Central Business District toll began on 5 January 2025, the first in the United States; the federal government moved to withdraw its approval on 19 February 2025 (CBS New York, 2025), and the toll ran on under a court injunction through its first year while the litigation continued, with the judge's final ruling still pending at the start of 2026 (Land Line, 2025; MTA, 2026).

**Channel.** Price, read through exit. Each driver reads one number at the gantry and decides whether the trip is worth it; the aggregate of a hundred thousand such decisions before nine in the morning is the verdict, and no official needs to know why any one of them was made, which is Hayek's dial (Chapter 2) mounted on a road. Stockholm added a second channel: the referendum came after the trial, so citizens verified seven months of measured traffic rather than a proposal, the Swiss shape from Chapter 4. Edinburgh in February 2005 and Manchester in December 2008 put charges to votes before any trial and lost both, Edinburgh by 74 percent and Manchester in all ten districts (Wikipedia, undated), because a referendum on a promise is an ex ante veto, and the voters had no output to read.

**Replaced.** Road building and rationing by queue. A road added where use is unpriced fills to the level of congestion drivers will tolerate, so capacity built against unpriced demand buys throughput and not speed (Duranton and Turner, 2011); the queue that results is a price paid in time that nobody collects, deadweight in the strict sense, the Soviet shop (Chapter 11) laid out on asphalt. The rule-thicket alternative is rationing by category, license-plate days of the kind Mexico City introduced in November 1989, which rations by a digit the driver can game, and did, by buying a second and usually older car with a different plate, so that air quality did not improve (Davis, 2008). A price rations by a value the driver cannot fake.

**Verified by.** Measured speed. Singapore's Land Transport Authority reviews ERP rates quarterly against target speed bands, 20 to 30 kilometers an hour on arterial roads and 45 to 65 on expressways (Ministry of Transport Singapore, undated), raising the charge where speed falls under the band and cutting it where speed rises above; the instrument is thus verified by the thing it exists to produce, in a number any driver can reproduce with a watch. Speed sits near train punctuality on the axis (Chapter 3), cheap, continuous, and hard to argue with, which is why the instrument carries its own verifier inside it. The limit is that speed verifies the charge and not the welfare: the driver priced off the road may be the one who could least afford the alternative, and the verifier is silent on that; London's annual monitoring reports (Transport for London, undated) tracked traffic, and the distributional question was left to the election.

**Capture mode.** Exemptions and frozen prices, which are the same failure from two sides: a generator writing itself out of the verifier's reach, and a price that stops reading demand. London exempted taxis and private hire vehicles; app-based hire multiplied the exempt fleet, and by 2014 congestion inside the zone was close to pre-charging levels on the operator's own reading, though vehicle numbers stayed lower (Transport for London, 2014); the private hire exemption ended in April 2019. Residents received a 90 percent discount. The charge itself moved by council decision, GBP 5 in 2003, GBP 8 in 2005, GBP 10 in 2011, GBP 11.50 in 2014, GBP 15 in 2020 (Wikipedia, undated), each step a political event, and a price set by vote is an administered price of the Chapter 2 kind that lags the demand it is supposed to ration. Stockholm exempted alternative-fuel cars until 2009 for new sales and 2012 for all, and the share of such cars rose from 3 percent in 2006 to 15 percent in 2009 (Eliasson, 2014), since the exemption verified fuel type rather than congestion. New York set its charge at USD 9 instead of the USD 15 its environmental assessment had studied (CBS New York, 2025), before the first car paid it, and its principal capture attempt was federal, a veto from outside the loop aimed at a verifier that went on to report rising speeds, 23 percent faster at the crossings in the morning peak and 27 million fewer vehicles in the first year (MTA, 2026). Singapore closed the frozen-price mode by design, since the rate is set by a formula reading speeds rather than by a vote, and its residual risk is of a different kind: a system that knows where every vehicle is at every minute is a cost to the press half of election/press, not to the price. The market-side instance of the same shape is the SR 91 Express Lanes in Orange County, California, opened in December 1995 under a private franchise whose non-compete clause barred the state from improving the parallel free lanes; the toll then read not the scarcity of road space but the contract's prohibition on adding any, and the county bought the franchise out in January 2003 for USD 207.5 million (FHWA, undated). A private price whose exit has been disabled by covenant is the Canadian grocery covenant (Chapter 12) on a highway: the road did not fail because it was private, it failed because the contract killed the verifier the price depended on.

### DRG Funding and Patient Choice

**Instrument.** A diagnosis-related group is a price on a hospital's output: an episode of care, classified by diagnosis and procedure, paid at a fixed tariff whatever the hospital spent on it. Robert Fetter and John Thompson at Yale built the classification in the 1970s as a management tool for measuring hospital product, 467 groups plus a residual code 999 (Mayes, undated; Wikipedia, undated); New Jersey piloted payment by DRG from 1980 under commissioner Joanne Finley; Congress adopted it for Medicare in the Social Security Amendments of 1983, effective for hospital fiscal years from October 1983 (Annals of Internal Medicine, 2013). The Nordics and Germany turned it into activity-based funding for public hospitals: Stockholm County from 1992 (Office of Technology Assessment, 1995), Norway's innsatsstyrt finansiering from 1 July 1997 (Store medisinske leksikon, undated), Denmark's DkDRG with roughly 1,000 groups and an annual tariff used for inter-regional settlement and hospital funding (Sundhedsdatastyrelsen, undated), Germany's G-DRG, voluntary in 2003 and mandatory in 2004, replacing per-diem payment (Busse and colleagues, 2011). The second half of the instrument is a choice guarantee that makes the price bind: Denmark's extended free choice from 2002 gave a patient the right to a private provider once the public wait exceeded one month (University of Southern Denmark, undated), and Sweden's statutory guarantee is 0-3-90-90, same-day contact, assessment within three days, first specialist visit and treatment each within 90 days (Socialstyrelsen, undated). Money follows the patient at a published price, and the patient can leave.

**Channel.** Price and exit, in Le Grand's choice-with-competition form (Chapter 7): a purchaser paying a tariff, a provider generating episodes, a patient who takes the tariff elsewhere. The tariff makes a public operator's output countable, which is what the operator rule (Chapter 3) requires of a state that runs hospitals: surgical quality sits in the middle of the axis, but episodes delivered and their cost sit low, and the DRG is the device that moved hospital output down the axis far enough to be verified. Exit is Hirschman's, with the consequence attached that Hirschman's lazy monopoly lacked: the hospital that loses the patient loses the tariff.

**Replaced.** Cost reimbursement and global budgets. Medicare before 1983 paid hospitals what they had spent, a generator paid on input with no verifier of output, Kornai's soft constraint written into statute. The global budget, the block grant to a hospital for existing, is the alternative the Nordics ran before activity funding, and it produced the queue, since a hospital paid a fixed sum is rewarded for treating fewer patients; the Swedish waiting list is what a global budget looks like from the patient's side of the desk.

**Verified by.** Waiting times and volumes, which is where the instrument's virtue and its danger are the same fact. Activity funding is checked by whether activity rose and waits fell per unit of money, and the Nordic record on the first is clear enough that Denmark retreated from pure activity funding after 2019 toward nærhedsfinansiering (Health Policy, 2024), a verdict that the volume verifier had been read too well. Sweden's guarantee is a published benchmark across the 21 regions that run its hospitals (Wikipedia, undated): no region fully meets it, and the share waiting more than 90 days for a first specialist contact ranges from about 4 percent in Stockholm to 38 percent in Västerbotten, and for treatment from about 1 percent to 48 percent in Örebro (Vårdföretagarna via TT, 2026), figures to be read knowing they were compiled by the private providers' association. The spread is itself a verifier: 21 units on one metric is a benchmark, and the guarantee gives the patient in Örebro a number to hold against the region.

**Capture mode.** Upcoding, DRG creep, and cream-skimming, which are Goodhart decay (Chapter 15) in a sector that named the disease before the treatment began: Simborg coined "DRG creep" in 1981 (Simborg, 1981), two years before Medicare's system started. The classification is written by the generator, so the generator learns to classify: pneumonia becomes pneumonia with complications, one admission becomes two episodes, a discharge is timed for the tariff rather than the patient. Cream-skimming follows from the tariff being an average, so the patient below average cost is profit and the patient above is loss, and any provider that can select will; the New York cardiac surgery report cards of the 1990s produced surgeons who declined the sickest patients (Dranove and others, 2003), which is the outcome verifier itself decaying by the same route. The market-side failure of identical shape is private insurers' upcoding under risk adjustment: Medicare Advantage plans, paid a capitation that rises with the recorded sickness of their enrollees, record them sicker, about 10 percent sicker than comparable fee-for-service beneficiaries on MedPAC's reading, and MedPAC's estimate of the 2025 overpayment from coding intensity and favorable selection together is USD 84 billion (MedPAC, 2025). Private ownership did not stop the classification from being written by the party it pays. The honest note is that the DRG verifies quantity and classification and nothing else, so a system that verifies only quantity gets quantity, and the instrument needs an outcome verifier alongside it, mortality and readmission at scale, the medium-cost verifier Chapter 3 placed at the middle of the axis; where that verifier is missing, the choice guarantee is a partial substitute, because exit reads something the tariff cannot, and where both are missing the tariff is a soft price on an unverified output.

### CPF Forced Savings

**Instrument.** Singapore's Central Provident Fund, established on 1 July 1955 (National Library Board, undated), compels employer and employee to deposit a fixed share of wages into an account the member owns. From 1 January 2026 the rate is 37 percent of wages up to age 55 (17 employer, 20 employee), 34 percent from 55 to 60, 25 percent from 60 to 65, 16.5 percent from 65 to 70, and 12.5 percent above 70 (CPF Board via ask.gov.sg, 2026). The balance funds a flat, since the Ordinary Account pays for HDB purchase under a scheme dating to September 1968, medical costs through Medisave since April 1984, and retirement through an annuity, CPF LIFE, automatic for cohorts since 2013 (National Library Board, undated). It is a parameter on a market, not a replacement of one: the state sets the rate, the member owns the balance, the flat the balance buys is priced by a resale market next door, and the annuity is priced against the balance the member can see.

**Channel.** Benchmark and exit, running through the member's own account. A pension promise is normally a formula; the CPF turns it into a number a member can read on a screen, and the balance is the verifier of the state's promise, checked as often as it is looked at. The member can compare the interest credited with the rate a bank pays and can, within limits, move part of the balance into approved investments under the investment scheme of 1997 (National Library Board, undated), which is exit inside the parameter. A pay-as-you-go system has no such channel: its promise is backed by future taxpayers, its liability sits on no balance sheet, and the closest thing to a verifier is a trustee's projection of the year the fund runs dry, 2033 for the US retirement fund and 2034 for the combined funds (Social Security Trustees, 2025), a number nobody trades and every legislature can move. A defined benefit from general revenue is an output nobody prices (Chapter 3); the individual account is that output with a price tag attached.

**Replaced.** The state pension bureau paying defined benefits from the general fund, and public rental housing as the operator's default. The second replacement is the less noticed one: because the balance buys a flat, the HDB resident is an owner with a resale price rather than a tenant with a rent, so the housing operator's output acquired a market verifier that a rental estate never has, and the two instruments, forced saving and tendered construction (the competitive tendering instrument, Chapter 14), verify each other through the resale market. Chile installed the same parameter on private managers in 1981, a 10 percent employee contribution to individual accounts with no employer share (KPMG, 2025), which shows the parameter is separable from the operator.

**Verified by.** The member's balance, and what the balance buys. The first check is solvency: the number on the statement is honored on withdrawal, every time, which is more than a pay-as-you-go system can say in advance. The second is the resale price of the flat, which reports whether the housing half of the promise held value. The third is adequacy, whether balances at retirement meet the sum the state itself publishes as sufficient, SGD 220,400 as the Full Retirement Sum for the cohort turning 55 in 2026 (CPF Board via DBS, 2026), and here the verifier is honest about its own limit: a balance can be fully honored and still be small, and it can be verified to the cent while its real value is set by an interest rate the state chose. Solvency is verified cheaply; adequacy is verified only against a benchmark the same state writes.

**Capture mode.** Administered returns and the state as the fund's borrower at rates it sets; the claim needs care and the mechanism needs stating exactly. CPF balances earn an administered rate, a floor of 2.5 percent on the Ordinary Account and 4 percent on the Special, Medisave, and Retirement accounts, set by formula against the major banks' deposit rates for the first and the ten-year government bond yield plus 1 percent for the rest (CPF Board, undated). The Board invests the balances entirely in Special Singapore Government Securities, non-tradable bonds issued by the government to the Board and paying the rate the Board pays members; the proceeds are invested with the state's other assets through GIC (Ministry of Finance Singapore, undated), whose reported twenty-year real return of 3.8 percent (GIC, 2025) exceeds the rate paid to members, and the difference accrues to the state's reserves rather than to the account. Temasek, which does not manage CPF money, reported a twenty-year total shareholder return of 6.8 percent at March 2026 (Temasek Review, 2026), a number cited here only to mark the distance between an administered rate and a market one in the same city. The member verifies the balance and cannot verify the rate against its alternative, since the alternative, investing the balance freely, is restricted by the parameter itself; an administered price inside a market parameter is the domestic price of Chapter 2 in miniature, and the borrower setting the lender's rate is the definition of a capturable verifier. The second mode is the parameter moving for reasons unrelated to the member: the employer's rate was cut from 25 to 10 percent in the 1986 recession as a wage-cost tool (Wikipedia, undated), and the minimum sum introduced in 1987 at SGD 30,000 (National Library Board, undated) has risen for each cohort, so the balance is verified and the rules for reaching it are not. The market-side instance of the same shape is Chile: exit between AFPs existed and members rarely used it, so managers' fees became an administered price by inertia rather than by decree, and the 2025 reform installed a tender verifier, auctioning 10 percent of affiliates every two years from August 2027 to the lowest-fee manager (Gob.cl, 2025; KPMG, 2025). A forced saving parameter is thin, and the loop reads it; what the loop cannot read is the rate the borrower pays itself.

### Sovereign Wealth Transparency

**Instrument.** A sovereign fund that publishes what it owns, what it earned against a reference index it did not pick for itself, what it is forbidden to own and why, and what the budget may draw from it under a rule written before the money arrived. The instrument is not the fund; funds are ordinary. The instrument is the publication, which turns a pool of national savings from a treasury the government manages into a generator the public benchmarks. Norway's Government Pension Fund Global (GPFG) is the instance because every one of those four publications exists and is dated. The fund held NOK 22,683 billion at 30 June 2026 after a first-half return of 9.4 percent, a record NOK 1,753 billion in krone terms, of which the report itself subtracts NOK 427 billion of currency effect (NBIM, 2026); it publishes its full list of holdings, more than 7,200 companies, twice a year with data back to 1998, together with how it voted at each of them (NBIM investments page, 2026); it scored 100 out of 100 on the Global Pension Transparency Benchmark in October 2025 for the second year (Top1000funds, 2025). The fiscal rule, adopted in 2001, limits the structural non-oil deficit to the fund's expected real return, set at 4 percent and cut to 3 percent in 2017 (Norwegian Ministry of Finance, 2017), so the budget reads a number computed from a published value rather than a number negotiated in a cabinet. A Council on Ethics, established in 2004, recommends exclusions of companies for conduct or product, and the exclusions are published with the reasoning (Council on Ethics, 2004), which makes the political layer of the fund a document that can be read and a non-decision a visible absence. The contrast is 1MDB, from which the United States Department of Justice alleges more than USD 4.5 billion was misappropriated between 2009 and 2015, of which about USD 1.4 billion had been returned to Malaysia by June 2024 (DOJ, 2024), and over which Goldman Sachs paid Malaysia USD 3.9 billion in 2020 (Banking Dive, 2020). 1MDB published no holdings, reported against no index, drew under no rule, and answered to a board whose ultimate sponsor was the head of government whose associates were the beneficiaries; its auditors were changed when they asked questions (Malay Mail, 2021). The generator wrote its own verdict, which is verifier capture in the definition of Chapter 1, and the theft was not an anomaly of the design but the design's expected output once nobody outside could read it.

**Channel.** Benchmark and election/press. The reference index is a benchmark verifier in the sense of Chapter 3: the fund's return is compared, unit against unit, with a published portfolio that the manager did not choose and cannot move. The press is the reader; a holdings list without a press is a spreadsheet, and a press without a holdings list writes about rumors. The two work together as election/press does, which is why a fund can score perfectly on transparency and still be unverified in a country where nothing is printed about it.

**Replaced.** The off-budget treasury: petroleum revenue spent through a development bank whose loans nobody prices, a stabilization account whose withdrawals are decided in the year they are needed, or a national investment company whose portfolio is a list of ministerial favorites. Each of these is an operational intervention in disguise, the state allocating capital by discretion with the state as the only judge of the allocation, and each carries the verification cost of industrial strategy, which is near infinite ex ante. The published fund replaces that with a cheap question, whether the portfolio beat the index it was told to track.

**Verified by.** Published returns against the stated reference index, and the second-order benchmark of transparency scoreboards that grade the publication itself. The relative return is a number the fund cannot choose, because both terms are public and the index is set by the ministry, not the manager (NBIM, 2026). A half-year report that decomposes its record return into market gain and currency loss is doing what a verifier needs and a treasury never does: separating what the generator did from what happened to it. The ethics exclusions are verified by their publication: a firm excluded for a stated reason can contest the reason, and a firm not excluded for the same conduct is a discrepancy anyone can find.

**Capture mode.** Opacity, arriving in the order Chapter 15 gives. The rule goes first, because cancelling it is one decision: the withdrawal becomes a decision, and the fund becomes a treasury with a long name. The benchmark goes second: the fund reports absolute returns, then returns "over the cycle," then returns net of a fee schedule it does not publish, and the relative number that made the manager comparable disappears. The holdings list goes last, and by then it does not matter, because a list nobody can compare against an index verifies nothing. The Gulf funds are the intermediate cases, and they are intermediate rather than opaque, which is the point of scoring them: on the Global SWF governance and sustainability scoreboard for 2026, ADIA scores 52 percent, down from 56, and Mubadala 88 percent in both 2025 and 2026 (Global SWF, 2026), and on the older Linaburg-Maduell ten-point checklist (audited reports, holdings disclosure, returns, ethics guidelines) the historical readings were about 6 for ADIA and 10 for Mubadala (SWFI, undated; Gulf News, undated). The variation inside one country is the imported verifier at work (Chapter 13): a fund that must raise co-investment abroad publishes what its counterparties require, and a fund that need not does not. The honest edge sits in Singapore, whose closed loop the atlas praised: half of Temasek's SGD 518 billion portfolio at 31 March 2026 was unlisted (Temasek Review, 2026), which means half the portfolio is marked by the fund rather than by a market, and the jump in net portfolio value from the SGD 434 billion reported a year earlier is partly a restatement, since the fund moved its unlisted holdings from book value to its own mark-to-market valuation and restated that year at SGD 469 billion (Temasek, 2026), so the reconciliation rests on the fund's own marks. The market instance of the same shape is the private fund whose marks are set by its manager and whose auditor is chosen by the marked: a hedge fund reporting returns to investors who cannot see the book, with the Madoff funds, audited for years by a three-person firm, as the limiting case (NBC News, 2008). Public or private, the mechanism is one: a fund whose verifier is selected and paid by the fund is a generator reviewing itself, and the return it reports is whatever it needs to report.

### Competitive Tendering as the Operator's Discipline

**Instrument.** The state, as client, buys the layer it cannot verify from generators it does not own, through an open tender on criteria published before the bids, so that the price of the unverifiable layer is formed by rivals rather than declared by the operator. This is the instrument that lets the operator rule (P4) function at all, because no state's output is verifiable all the way down. HDB is the canonical instance, and the atlas has already paid it its compliment (the Singapore case, Chapter 9). The Board's flats housed 77.2 percent of Singapore's resident households in 2025 (SingStat, 2025); it operates land assembly, planning, allocation, and the pricing of new flats, each of which yields a count or a published price a resident can check; and it buys construction, the layer whose productivity no ministry can verify, by open tender advertised on GeBIZ, from contractors registered with the Building and Construction Authority in the workhead and financial grade stated in each tender notice (HDB tender eligibility page, 2026). The Board does not know what a cubic meter of placed concrete should cost on that site in that year. It does not need to. Six bidders know, and their disagreement, resolved by the lowest compliant bid, is the price. The general case follows: wherever the state operates, there is a layer beneath its competence, and the tender is how a state buys that layer through a verifier it does not control. Hart, Shleifer, and Vishny gave the condition in contract language: the tender works where the output can be specified closely enough for a court to verify delivery, a building to a drawing, a road to a profile, and fails where the decisive quality is non-contractible, the use of force in a prison being their close case.

**Channel.** Tender, which is price applied to a one-off output that has no continuous market, backed by exit, since the client can go elsewhere next time. The state builds nothing new to run the instrument: contractors already bid against each other for private clients, and the public tender routes the state's purchase through the same rivalry. A state that builds with its own crews keeps the knowledge in house, faces no contractor's margin, and cannot be held hostage by a bidder who walks. The lens answers that the margin is the fee for a verdict, and that a crew that cannot lose the next job produces the output of a generator with no verifier, at the cost of whatever nobody could measure.

**Replaced.** The state construction company and force-account building: the Soviet construction trust that built what the plan assigned at the cost the plan recorded, the municipal works department with its own labor force, the public works ministry whose crews cannot be fired for the last job and therefore cannot be disciplined for the next. Force account means the state is both client and contractor and verifies its own concrete. The direct labor organizations of British local authorities were the same instrument in a democracy, and were subjected to compulsory competitive tendering from 1980 under the Local Government, Planning and Land Act precisely because their costs were unreadable until a rival bid against them. The tender does not replace the state as client; it replaces the state as its own supplier.

**Verified by.** Bid spreads and delivery records. A live tender shows several bids, a spread between them that reflects real disagreement about cost, a winner who delivers at or near the bid, and a next round in which last round's performance moves the rankings. A dead one shows a single bidder, or identical bids, or winners that rotate in a pattern, or a low bid followed by a cascade of variation orders that lifts the final cost above the losing bids. Each of these is cheap to read from the record the tender itself produces, which is why tendering, unlike the output it buys, can be verified by a clerk. The delivery record then closes the loop: the flats handed over on the date at the price are the consequence for the next tender, and a contractor who fails loses the only thing the client controls, which is the next job.

**Capture mode.** Four ways, in rising order of subtlety. Bid rigging: the bidders agree who wins, and the tender becomes a ritual whose spread is theater; the Japanese dango system (Japan Fair Trade Commission, 2007) and the Quebec construction cartel exposed by the Charbonneau Commission (final report, 2015) are the documented public-works instances. Specification written for one bidder: the criteria name a product, a certification, or a track record only the incumbent holds, and the competition is decided before the notice is posted. The incumbent's information advantage: the firm that built the last block knows the ground, the client's change-order habits, and which drawings are wrong, so it bids below cost and recovers through variations, and the spread that was supposed to verify becomes a spread the incumbent sets. The fourth is the client's own drift from verifier to veto point: registration, prequalification, and compliance layers accumulate until fewer firms can bid than can build, the notice becomes an ex ante screen rather than an ex post verdict, and the tender turns into the shelf of binders of Chapter 3. The market runs the identical failure without a public client in the room. Loblaw and George Weston settled for CAD 500 million in July 2024, in a settlement approved in May 2025, the allegation that Canadian packaged-bread prices had been coordinated across suppliers and retailers from January 2001 to December 2021 (Global News, 2025): a private procurement channel, retailer buying from supplier, whose price the buyer's customers were supposed to verify and could not, because the rivals who formed it had agreed on it first. A procurement cartel is a tender captured by its bidders, and it does not matter whether the client is a ministry or a grocer.

### Radical Statistical Transparency

**Instrument.** The state publishes the numbers by which it will be judged, in a form an outsider can check, together with the record of who touched them. The numbers are the state's own report of its output, which makes them the most capturable verifier on the list; publication does not make them true, it makes them checkable, which moves verification from the generator to whoever reads: the press, the opposition, the neighboring jurisdiction, the outside statistician. Estonia is the domestic instance. X-Road, its data-exchange layer in use since 2001, links more than 3,000 e-services and some 52,000 organizations and carries about 2.2 billion transactions a year, and has been deployed in more than 20 countries (e-Estonia, 2026); the Data Tracker, delivered through eesti.ee over X-Road, lets a citizen see which institution accessed which of her personal data and when, register by register, and the information system authority has proposed making it mandatory for every public database (RIA, undated; ERR News, undated). The access log is the instrument's sharpest form: not the data but the record of reading and writing, so that the citizen verifies the state's use of the citizen. Eurostat is the imported instance, and the Greek record shows why an imported one was needed. The 2004 audit revised Greek deficits for 1997 to 2003 upward and put the euro-qualifying year of 1999 at 3.07 percent of GDP, with Eurostat listing eleven methodological violations (Eurostat, 2004); in 2009 the 2008 deficit went from 5.0 percent in the April notification to 7.7 percent in October, and the 2009 projection from 3.7 to 12.5 percent; the Commission's report of 8 January 2010 found an institutional setting that "could not guarantee" freedom from political interference (Commission, 2010); the final figure for 2009 was 15.4 percent on 15 November 2010 (Eurostat via Dow Jones, 2010). Every revision ran the same direction. A series that is only ever revised upward is a series whose first print was written by its subject. Mandatory disclosure regimes are the general form of the instrument: the securities filing that makes a firm's accounts a public document (the Securities Act of 1933 and the Securities Exchange Act of 1934), the hospital price file, the published waiting list, each a statistic the generator must sign and an outsider may read.

**Channel.** Election/press and benchmark. A published number is read by the press, which is how it reaches the election, and compared across units, which is the benchmark. The instrument is thin because both readers already exist; the state adds nothing but the publication and the log.

**Replaced.** The internal report: the statistic as an instrument of government rather than a verdict on it. Before 2010 the Greek statistical service reported inside the finance ministry it was measuring (Commission, 2010), which is the arrangement the rule-thicket alternative preserves under a statistical governance code nobody outside can enforce. The operational alternative is the ministry that counts its own output and files the count, which is Gosplan's arrangement (Chapter 11) and the arrangement of every hospital that has never published a mortality figure.

**Verified by.** Revisions and external audit. A statistical system is verified by the size and direction of its revisions, small and two-directional in a live one, large and one-directional in a captured one, and by an external audit with the power to reject a notification, which Eurostat acquired for Greece only after the 2010 report and the 2004 audit had shown the domestic verifier could not be trusted with its own numbers. India's new GDP series of 27 February 2026, rebased to 2022 to 2023 on GST, payment-system, and corporate-registry data with double deflation (MoSPI, 2026), is verified the same way: by whether the back series is published with its method, and by what the revisions to it look like in five years.

**Capture mode.** The statistician prosecuted, the series suspended, the back series rewritten. Andreas Georgiou, president of ELSTAT from 2010 to 2015, was charged in 2013 with inflating the 2009 deficit, acquitted on that charge three times, the last on 1 March 2019 after the Supreme Court prosecutor had twice revoked earlier acquittals, and in 2017 given a two-year suspended sentence for breach of duty, upheld in 2018, for not submitting the figures to the ELSTAT board first, with a separate civil defamation judgment over his defense of the data (National Herald, 2019; ISI, 2019); the European Court of Human Rights found in March 2023 that the breach-of-duty proceedings had denied him a fair trial, and the Supreme Court reopened that case and acquitted him in 2026, a ruling that did not concern the separate defamation judgment (European Court of Human Rights, 2023; ISI, 2025; Efsyn, 2026). The prosecution verifies the instrument in reverse: the state that punishes the man who published the true number has announced which number it wanted. China's youth unemployment rate reached a record 21.3 percent in June 2023, publication was suspended in August 2023, and the series resumed in January 2024 excluding students at 14.9 percent for December 2023 (Time, 2023; Atlantic Council), then reached 18.9 percent on the new definition in August 2025 (SCMP, 2025); the same authorities stopped publishing hundreds of series, land sales and cremations among them (Wall Street Journal via China Digital Times, 2025), and in December 2025 suspended private-sector home-sales data (China Factor, 2025). A series that stops is a verifier retired because its verdict was bad. India's rebasing of 2015 triggered a back-series dispute in which the National Statistical Commission's own committee draft was disowned as experimental, and a former chief economic adviser argued in June 2019 that growth from 2011 to 2017 had been about 4.5 percent rather than the official near 7 (The Week, 2019), which the prime minister's council rebutted as cherry-picking (Business Standard, 2019); the census due in 2021 was postponed to 2027 (Deccan Herald, 2025). The rewritten back series is the subtlest capture because it looks like methodology, and sometimes is; the verifier's answer is publication of the method, which lets the dispute be settled by someone other than the generator. The market runs the same three deaths with different names. The hospital that posts a pricing file no patient can interpret, as three quarters of reviewed United States hospitals did in 2025 (PatientRightsAdvocate, 2025), has suspended the series while complying with the disclosure; the firm that restates years of its own accounts has rewritten its back series (Enron, 2001); and the rating agencies of Chapter 12, paid by the issuers they graded, prosecuted no statistician because they had none to prosecute.

There is a way to see a dead signal without the ledger. Somewhere there is still a glass with a price pressed into its base at the factory, a number in kopecks fixed in the mold because the price was never going to change, and it did not: not when the sugar ran out, not when the factory closed, not when the currency it named was withdrawn. The number is still legible. It is a price that no one reads, attached to a value that no one verified, and the loop it once belonged to left nothing behind but the digit.

# Part IV: Decay and Repair

## Chapter 15. How Verifiers Die

The price pressed into the glass at the end of Chapter 14 is still legible. It appeared in the gazette, on the shelf label, in the ministry's tables, in the mold; what it had stopped doing was moving, and a price that does not move is not being read by anyone whose conduct it could change. That is the general form of a dead verifier. It is almost never abolished. It keeps its office, its staff, its publication schedule, and often its prestige, and it dies in the only way that matters to the loop: its verdict ceases to select. Verifiers die by four mechanisms, capture, softening, veto substitution, and Goodhart decay, and they die in one order, because each mechanism is the cheapest move available to a generator at the stage the loop has reached. The consequence goes first, the independence second, the verifier's own cost last, and then the thing is retired without a decision that anyone need defend. A mature failure usually shows all four, and the order is what lets an observer date the failure from outside. The lens claims the sequence and not the tolerance for it; why one polity accepts the first step and another refuses is governed by trust, scale, and the other residuals the book does not explain.

### Capture

A verifier is captured when its income or its career depends on the satisfaction of the generator it judges. From that point its verdicts are a product sold to the generator, and the product that sells is approval. Capture needs no bribe and no lie. It needs a dependency, and once the dependency exists the verifier's staff will discover, in good faith and by selection, that the generator's output is good. The test is two questions: who pays the verifier, and who employs its people next.

The credit rating agency before 2008 is the purest market instance, and Chapter 12 carries it; here it supplies the mechanism in its cleanest form. The agencies existed to pass a verdict on securities they did not issue for buyers who could not evaluate a mortgage pool, and they were paid by the issuers whose securities they rated. The settlements record what the dependency did. S&P paid USD 1.375 billion to the Department of Justice, nineteen states and the District of Columbia in February 2015 and acknowledged that it had delayed adopting stricter models in 2005 (JURIST, 2015); Moody's paid nearly USD 864 million in January 2017 with factual admissions that it had deviated from its published methodologies and failed to manage the conflicts of the issuer-pays model (DOJ, 2017). Nothing structural changed. The issuer-pays model remains dominant, the Dodd-Frank provision that required the SEC to study random assignment of raters produced a 2012 study that did not adopt it, and three firms still hold the large majority of outstanding ratings (CRS, undated; SEC, 2024). A captured verifier that is fined and left in place is a captured verifier with a line item.

The state instance is Lysenko, which Chapter 1 named and the USSR case (Chapter 11) developed. Science is the one generator whose verifier is supposed to be the experiment, and Soviet agronomy replaced the experiment with the approval of the patron: Trofim Lysenko's career depended on Stalin's satisfaction, the careers of the geneticists who contradicted him depended on Lysenko's, and after the August 1948 session of the agricultural academy at which Mendelian genetics was declared false, Soviet biology reported what the patron wanted for a generation (Borinskaya and colleagues, 2019). Nikolai Vavilov, the geneticist who had built the seed bank, died in prison in 1943 (Borinskaya and colleagues, 2019). The wheat was the only verifier left, and the wheat could not be captured, which is why the failure eventually surfaced as a harvest and not as a retraction.

Between the two sits the regulator staffed from the industry it oversees, which is neither state nor market and is the ordinary case. The mechanism is the second question: the examiner's next employer is the bank, the drug reviewer's next employer is the sponsor, and the verdict that would foreclose that employment is the verdict that does not get written. The World Bank's Doing Business index shows how far up the chain capture can reach. The bank discontinued the report on 16 September 2021 after an outside review found undue pressure from senior management, including the office of the then chief executive, to raise China's 2018 ranking (World Bank, 2021; CNBC, 2021), at a time when the bank was seeking a capital increase to which China's support mattered. A benchmark verifier that ranked a hundred and ninety economies was bent by one generator with leverage over the verifier's owner, and the verifier was then not repaired but retired, which is the order of death this chapter ends on.

### Softening

A verifier whose verdict will predictably be cancelled dies without anyone touching it. Kornai's soft constraint, the term the book has used since Chapter 1, is a statement about expectation: a firm whose losses it expects to be covered behaves as if price had no selective force, and the softening happens before any bailout and whether or not the bailout ever comes. Kornai, Maskin, and Roland restated the mechanism as a commitment problem. The supporting organization cannot credibly promise not to refinance, because once the generator's costs are sunk, refinancing is ex post rational for the supporter as well; Dewatripont and Maskin's model makes refinancing the equilibrium move of a rational lender who would have preferred never to lend (Kornai, Maskin, and Roland, 2003). The verifier still fires. The bankruptcy court still exists, the deficit rule is still printed, the bond spread still quotes. What has died is the link between the verdict and the allocation, and it dies in one decision, the first act of forbearance, after which every generator in the system updates its expectation.

The market instance is the Japanese bank of the 1990s, which Chapter 5 introduced and the Japan case (Chapter 10) diagnosed. Japan's price, export and election/press verifiers were alive through the lost decade; its bankruptcy verifier was decorative because bank regulators forbore on capital, and banks that could not afford to recognize losses kept insolvent borrowers current with subsidized credit rather than write them down. The zombie count Chapter 5 reported rose above a quarter of listed firms after 1994 (Caballero, Hoshi, and Kashyap, 2008), and its congestion result is the one that matters: in industries where zombies were prevalent, healthy firms invested less and hired less, so the softened constraint did not merely preserve the dead, it starved the living that would have replaced them. Every bank made the same calculation, because every bank expected the same forbearance, and the expectation was correct until the Takenaka program of 2002 forced recognition (FSA, 2002), which Chapter 17 treats as the end of forbearance.

The state instance is the Greek deficit inside the euro. The Stability and Growth Pact was a verifier with a numerical verdict, 3 percent of GDP, and a consequence, the excessive deficit procedure. The consequence was cancelled first for the members large enough to cancel it: in November 2003 the finance ministers held the procedure in abeyance for France and Germany rather than apply it (ECB, 2003), and a rule that had been softened for the two largest economies could not thereafter bind the smallest. Greece had qualified for entry on a 1999 deficit later revised to 3.07 percent of GDP, and the 2004 audit revised every year from 1997 upward (Eurostat, 2004). In 2009 the 2008 deficit moved from 5.0 percent in the April notification to 7.7 percent in October, the 2009 projection from 3.7 to 12.5 percent, and Eurostat's final figure for 2009 was 15.4 percent (Commission, 2010; Dow Jones, 2010). The rule had been read for a decade by a generator that knew the reading did not bind. The bond market, the verifier that should have priced the rule's softness, had softened too: Greek sovereign debt traded within a few tens of basis points of German debt for most of the decade before 2008 (Bank of Greece, 2011), because investors priced the rescue before it existed, and they were right. The three programs disbursed EUR 288.7 billion between 2010 and 2018 (ESM, 2018). The softest constraint in modern European history and the hardest conditions ever attached to a rescue were the same expectation seen from two ends.

### Veto Substitution

The third death replaces the verdict with a procedure, and it is the hardest of the four to see because it looks like rigor. Chapter 4 defined the veto point and named its product, verification theater. Here the concern is dynamic: the veto does not merely coexist with the verifier, it displaces it, and it is chosen by incumbents because a veto is cheaper for them than a verdict. A verdict is a built building, a shipped product, a passed statute, an object on which exit and price can fire and against which the incumbent must then compete. A veto is a document, and a document can be made inadequate by anyone with standing and a lawyer. The substitution therefore has a constituency, and the constituency is not opposed to oversight; it is composed of the parties that oversight protects.

The state instance is CEQA, the canonical ex ante instrument of Chapter 4 and of the United States case (Chapter 10). The housing market's verdict on a proposed building is delivered by the building: whether it lets, at what rent, whether the neighbors leave or stay. The California Environmental Quality Act replaces that verdict with a review of a forecast, enforced through private litigation, in which the object of judgment is the adequacy of the document. The verdict on the substitution is legible in two numbers the process cannot reach: the San Francisco permit median and the price gap that the United States case reports (Chapter 10) both moved against the city while the documents grew. The legislature's response in June 2025, a statutory exemption for infill housing on sites of up to 20 acres and a rule confining review of a near-miss project to the single criterion it failed (Holland & Knight, 2025), was not a repair of the review but a removal of projects from it, which is what a generator does when it has concluded that a veto will never become a verifier. The parties who used the process were rarely environmentalists; they were neighbors, rival businesses and unions bargaining for labor agreements, each of whom found that blocking a rival's output was cheaper than beating it (Holland & Knight, 2015).

The market instance is inside the firm, and it is the one the book's libertarian reader will like least. A product is verified by customers, ex post, through price and exit. Inside a large firm the product reaches the customer only after it has passed legal review, compliance review, brand review, risk review and security review, each of which holds a veto and none of which holds a verdict, because a product that never ships generates no signal that any reviewer could be held to. The reviewers are not idle or corrupt; they are rational agents whose only observable error is the harm that ships, so they minimize shipping. After 2008 the banks that had trusted a captured verifier did not repair it; they hired compliance staff by the thousand (FCPA Blog, 2014), and the rule thicket was bought in place of the verifier. The same substitution explains why incumbents so often welcome regulation that the public reads as a burden on them. A veto that costs a large firm a department costs a small entrant its existence, and the incumbent's product then faces fewer verdicts, not more. The Canadian grocers of Chapter 12 achieved the same displacement with property covenants, a private veto on a rival's store that replaced the verdict of the shopper walking across the road with a clause in a lease (Competition Bureau, 2023).

### Goodhart Decay

The fourth death reaches the verifiers the first three cannot. Charles Goodhart's original statement, delivered at a Reserve Bank of Australia conference in 1975 on the United Kingdom's experience with monetary targets, was that "any observed statistical regularity will tend to collapse once pressure is placed upon it for control purposes" (Goodhart, 1975). Marilyn Strathern's rephrasing, the one usually quoted as the law, appeared in a 1997 paper on audit in British universities: "When a measure becomes a target, it ceases to be a good measure" (Strathern, 1997). The two sentences are not identical. Goodhart's is about a correlation that was true until it was used; Strathern's is about a measure that was informative until it was rewarded. The book uses both under the name Goodhart decay, and the mechanism is the same in each: every metric verifier is a proxy, a generator rewarded on the proxy optimizes the proxy, and the relationship between proxy and output that made the metric a verifier is destroyed by the act of using it. Capture requires a dependency, softening a supporter, veto substitution a constituency. Goodhart decay requires only that the generator be able to reach the number, and the generator can almost always reach the number, because in the ordinary case the generator reports it.

The Chinese provincial tournament is the instance that measures the decay. Li and Zhou found that the promotion of provincial leaders between 1979 and 1995 rose with provincial growth, absolute and relative to the predecessor, which is a benchmark verifier with a binding consequence and the reason the China case (Chapter 10) accepts the loop as closed through the 2000s (Li and Zhou, 2005). The metric was self-reported by the province being ranked. The National Bureau of Statistics adjusted the sum of provincial figures downward by about 5 percent of GDP on average from the mid-2000s, and after 2008 the provincial data deteriorated faster than the adjustment; using value-added tax receipts and other series less exposed to the incentive, Chen, Chen, Hsieh, and Song estimate that national growth from 2010 to 2016 was 1.8 percentage points a year lower than reported and the 2016 investment rate 7 points lower (Chen, Chen, Hsieh, and Song, 2019). The decimal is contested, and on the satellite-nightlights evidence for 2004 to 2015 so is the sign (Clark, Pinkovskiy, and Sala-i-Martin, 2017); the finding rests on the tax data, not on the sky. The decay then ran to its terminus. When the youth unemployment rate reached a record 21.3 percent in June 2023 the bureau suspended the series, and it returned in January 2024 under a definition excluding students that printed 14.9 percent (Time, 2023; Atlantic Council, 2024); by 2025 hundreds of series had stopped, from land sales to cremations, and in December 2025 the housing ministry suspended the private home-sales data compiled by two research firms (China Digital Times, 2025; China Factor, 2025). A benchmark verifier the generator can falsify is captured by arithmetic, and when the arithmetic becomes too expensive to defend the series is retired.

The hospital waiting list is the same decay in a welfare state, and a waiting-time guarantee is a genuine verifier with a genuine consequence when it is new. The English four-hour target for emergency departments produced ambulances queuing outside the door so that the clock would not start (Lean Blog, 2008), and the DRG tariff of Chapter 14, which verifies a hospital by paying it per diagnosis, produced the practice of coding each patient into the most lucrative eligible group, named DRG creep in the New England Journal of Medicine while New Jersey was still piloting the payment system and two years before Medicare adopted it (Simborg, 1981). Test scores follow the pattern under the name of Campbell's law, published the year after Goodhart's lecture: the more a score decides funding or careers, the more the score is taught, then coached, then corrected by hand, as Atlanta's public schools were found to have done in an investigation that ended in criminal convictions (PBS, 2015); the firm's version is the sales target that Wells Fargo's branches met by opening accounts nobody asked for (Chapter 16). Citation counts complete the set. A count was a cheap verifier of scholarly influence exactly until it was rewarded, after which citation rings, paper mills and pay-to-publish journals supplied the number without the influence; retractions exceeded 10,000 in 2023, most of them from one publisher's paper-mill clean-up, and that publisher's retraction rate runs at 320 per 10,000 articles against four for the least affected large house (Nature, 2023; Retraction Watch, 2026). The academic peer review case (Chapter 12) is the fuller account; the point here is that the metric decayed in the same shape in a ministry, a hospital, a school district and a university press, which is the sense in which Goodhart decay is the universal solvent.

The decay dissolves the book's own favored verifiers in a definite order of resistance. Benchmark is the most soluble, because it is a number the ranked unit usually reports. Export resists better, because the customs receipt is written by the buyer's state, though over-invoicing exists. Price resists best of all, because a price is not reported by the generator; it is observed at the moment of exchange, and the generator can move it only by administering it, at which point it becomes the price tag that no longer moves. Exit is nearly immune, since nobody can fake a customer who left. The chapter's forward reference is the newest instance, and Chapter 18 gives it in full: a model trained by reinforcement learning against a reward model optimizes the reward and not the behavior the reward was a proxy for, gold-standard reward falls as the proxy is over-optimized (Gao, Schulman, and Hilton, 2022), and models under such training have been observed editing the unit tests they were meant to pass (Denison and colleagues, 2024, in Weng, 2024). Reward hacking is Goodhart decay with the generator's optimization made explicit and fast, and it is the reason machine learning treats verifier design as the binding constraint. Scott's warning (Chapter 16) is the companion to this section and should not be confused with it: Goodhart describes the harm to the signal, Scott the harm to the world when the signal is acted on anyway.

### The Order

The three legs fail in an order, and the order is set by what each costs the generator. Bindingness softens first, because cancelling a consequence is one decision: a forbearance letter, a procedure held in abeyance, a loan rolled over, a target missed without a penalty. Nobody has to be hired or fired, and the verifier can be left intact, even praised, while its verdict stops moving resources. Independence is captured second, because it takes years of appointments and hiring: a director replaced here, a methodology committee restaffed there, a revolving door that turns a few times before the verdict changes. Cost is inflated last, and it is inflated by the defense the verifier now requires rather than by the verifier itself; veto substitution and Goodhart decay belong to this stage, since the procedure that replaces the verdict and the arithmetic that replaces the output are what inflates the cost of a verifier already softened and captured. A captured verifier still publishes, and each publication must now be reconciled with what the generator has said, fought over when private estimates diverge, and explained when it embarrasses. The verifier becomes expensive precisely because it has been compromised, and when the cost of running a compromised verifier exceeds its remaining use to anyone, it is retired in the name of economy. The census is delayed. The survey is suspended for methodological review. The series is discontinued pending a new base year. The sequence is the cheapest path for the generator at each step, which is why it recurs; a coup can reverse the order, capturing independence in a day, but a coup is not the cheapest path for a generator that still expects to be around.

Argentina's consumer price index ran the full sequence in a decade, and the Argentina case (Chapter 11) relies on it. In 2005 and 2006 inflation rose past the government's comfort and the response was price agreements with retailers and export restrictions rather than any change in the policy the index was meant to discipline (USDA, 2006; Knowledge at Wharton, 2007), which is the softening: the verdict was read and did not bind. In January 2007 the government removed the head of the INDEC price division and altered the methodology (Knowledge at Wharton, 2007), after which the official index printed a fraction of every private estimate for the next eight years, which is the capture, and it took an appointment rather than a decree. The cost then inflated. Private economists who published their own estimates were fined, opposition deputies began publishing a consolidated private figure under parliamentary immunity so that its authors could not be prosecuted, and in February 2013 the IMF issued a declaration of censure against Argentina for the quality of its inflation and growth data, the first in the Fund's history (Bloomberg, 2013). The verifier had become the most expensive statistic in the country to defend. In 2016 the incoming government declared a statistical emergency and suspended publication of the consumer price index for several months before relaunching it (Ámbito, 2016), which is the retirement, dressed, as retirements always are, as repair. The relaunched index is the source of the 2024 and 2025 inflation figures, and its independence is now the thing the current program is verified by, which is why the Argentina verdict is pending.

The Soviet statistical system ran the same sequence at a larger scale and with heavier consequences. The 1937 census counted a population millions below the figure Stalin had announced, its results were suppressed, its senior officials were arrested and several were shot, and a replacement census in 1939 produced the required number (Merridale, 1996). The order holds: the plan's consequences had been softened long before, statistical independence had been captured by the purge of the economists in the early 1930s, and the census, the last verifier whose arithmetic could not be reconciled with the plan, was retired by killing the people who had run it. India's postponement of the 2021 census to a 2027 reference date (Deccan Herald, 2025) belongs to the same family at its mildest, a verifier deferred rather than destroyed, and the reader who applies the order should ask what was softened and captured in the years before the deferral, which the India case (Chapter 11) does.

The order also runs in reverse, and the reverse is the repair sequence of Chapter 17. Eurostat's 2010 audit restored independence to Greek statistics, ELSTAT's new president produced the 15.4 percent figure, and the cost of running the restored verifier was then inflated by the generator in the only way left to it: its producer was prosecuted for a decade for publishing the true number (the Greece case, Chapter 11). A repaired verifier is attacked at the cost leg because the other two are no longer available, and whether the attack succeeds is the measure of whether the repair holds.

What remains after the fourth death is the metric standing where the thing stood. It has a physical form. In a hospital it is the report in which nobody waits more than ninety days. In a province it is the growth figure delivered to the center before the harvest is in. In a school it is the score sheet. The oldest of these objects is a table of yields: columns of cubic meters of timber per hectare by age of stand, computed once so that the forester would never again need to walk the forest.

## Chapter 16. Scott's Warning

The oldest metric that replaced its thing is a table of numbers. The yield table of late eighteenth-century German forestry told a Prussian or Saxon official how many cubic meters of merchantable timber a hectare of a given species at a given age would deliver, and behind the table stood two fictions that made its arithmetic exact: the Normalbaum, the standard tree, and the Normalwald, the standard forest, a stand of one species and one age planted in rows so that every tree was a Normalbaum and the count could be taken from the path. Scott's first chapter of Seeing Like a State (1998) records what happened when the table stopped describing the forest and began prescribing it. The fiscal forester had been sent to count what grew; his successor was sent to grow what could be counted. Mixed woodland of oak, beech, hornbeam, understory and deadfall was cleared and replanted with Norway spruce or Scots pine, even-aged and single-species, and the first rotation, roughly eighty years, delivered what the table promised. The second did not. Scott reports yields falling by a fifth to a third, on soil that had lost the litter, fungi and fauna the mixed forest had supplied without charge, in stands that storms and insects found undefended, and German foresters coined Waldsterben, forest death, for a condition their own method had produced. The remedy attempted was forest hygiene: artificial nests for birds, ant colonies carried in from elsewhere, spiders released among the trunks, an inventory of what the table had omitted, reintroduced item by item at a cost the free forest had never presented. The table had been accurate. It had measured the forest so well that the forest was rebuilt to match it, and then the forest failed, and the table with it.

That failure is Goodhart decay seen from the far side of the mirror, and the pairing is the chapter's claim. Goodhart names the harm to the signal: the metric stops reporting the output. Scott names the harm to the world: the output is remade in the metric's image and dies of the resemblance. They are one event. A generator that is graded on a metric rather than on its output has two ways to raise its grade, altering the report or altering the world, and it takes whichever is cheaper. Where the report is unaudited and the world is hard to move, the report is altered: the Chinese provincial GDP of Chapter 15, the Greek deficit of Chapter 11, the waiting list with its patients re-dated. Where the report is audited and the world is the generator's to command, the world is altered: the Saxon forester did not falsify a single cubic meter, and the province he ran was made into a plantation. In both directions the metric has replaced the output; the only difference is whether the world or the ledger absorbed the substitution. Goodhart's cases and Scott's cases are therefore not two pathologies but one, sorted by where the generator's power lay.

Scott's remaining cases sort the same way, and each is a state that chose its metric for the state's convenience in reading it. Soviet collectivization made grain legible: a kolkhoz delivering to a state elevator under a procurement quota could be counted from Moscow in a way that twenty-five million peasant households could not, and the quota became the output, met by seizing seed grain and by the death of the peasants whose practical knowledge of soil, weather and rotation had made the old farms produce. Tanzanian villagization under ujamaa, 1973 to 1976, moved several million people into villages laid out in grids along roads, a settlement pattern legible for the delivery of clinics and the collection of crops, and agricultural output fell to the point of food imports (Meredith, 2005) because the scattered homesteads had been sited where the land and water were, not where the road was. Brasília, planned by Costa and Niemeyer and built between 1956 and 1960, was a city designed to be read from the air, with the superquadra as its standard tree; the street life the plan omitted reappeared in the unplanned satellite settlements that housed the majority of the population, and the residents named the condition of the planned core brasilite (Scott, 1998). Scott's diagnosis is a conjunction of four elements: administrative legibility, high-modernist faith in design, an authoritarian state willing to use force, and a civil society too weak to resist. The loop's diagnosis is shorter. In every case the verifiers that would have fired early were dead. No price on the peasant's grain, no exit from the kolkhoz or the village, no press to print the harvest, no election to punish it, so the harm ran for a full rotation because nothing could report it before the rotation ended. The forest is the most instructive of the four precisely because its state was the least tyrannical. Prussia had a timber price and a functioning administration and the verdict still took eighty years, because the output was an ecosystem, and an ecosystem delivers its verdict on the ecosystem's clock.

The chapter's stance is the refinement, not the refutation, and the refinement begins by refusing the reading Scott's admirers prefer, in which legibility is itself the disease. Scott concedes the goods (the cadastre, the census, surnames, standard weights and measures, public health) and the book insists on them: the vaccination register, the housing units counted at handover, the tender price, the punctuality statistic and the hospital's mortality table are all legibility, and they are what the operator rule (P4) runs on. A state that cannot count cannot verify at all. The alternative to the yield table in the 1780s was not a forest tended by metis with a free hand; it was a treasury that could not see the forest and a forestry office nobody could grade. The disaster in Scott's cases is not that the state measured. It is that the state measured the wrong thing, kept measuring it after the world had diverged from the measure, and had first killed every verifier that would have said so. A bad verifier is still a verifier. Scott's word for it is legibility, and the book's word is a noisy verifier with a captured owner; the remedy each implies is not the absence of a verifier but a cheaper, closer one.

The word cheap is where the lens adds something to Scott, because he and the book use it in different senses and the difference is the axis. Legibility is cheapness to the center: what a clerk can read from a desk. Verification cost in the book's sense is a property of the output type, and the book counts it only for a verifier that is also external to the generator, close to the outcome, continuous, and capturable only with effort: cheap means cheap to run under those four conditions. For groceries the two senses coincide and the center does not even need to read the verdict; the shopper reads it and leaves. For the forest they diverge. The yield table was cheap in Scott's sense and failed the book's on every count: internal, because the office that planted also counted; distant from the outcome, because merchantable volume at year eighty stood in for soil, water, pests and the next rotation; discontinuous, because it fired once a rotation; and capturable by the simplest expedient available to a generator, planting only what the table counted. Scott's cases are cases of the wrong verifier, chosen by the reader's cost rather than the loop's, and the reader was the generator, so the choice was a generator writing its own verifier, which is capture by design. What the axis adds is a rule for which outputs can bear a metric. Some can, because the metric sits next to the outcome and the world audits it continuously: a housing unit delivered exists or does not, a train is on time or is not, a vaccinated child either contracts measles or does not and the surveillance system reads the answer. Others cannot, because the outcome is far from anything countable and the delay between count and consequence exceeds the horizon over which the generator optimizes: a forest ecosystem, a peasant economy, the street life of a city, a school year. The low end of the axis is where legibility works and the high end is where it flattens, and the axis says so before the case is opened, which Scott's four-part conjunction cannot, since it requires an authoritarian state and is silent under live verifiers. The lens does not explain metis, the ecology of a mixed stand, or why local knowledge outperforms a table; it claims only that where an output cannot bear a metric, the metric will be optimized against and the output will not survive the optimization unless a closer verifier fires first.

Under live verifiers the same event runs on a faster clock and with a smaller body count, and the cases are the ones Scott's conjunction excludes. Campbell's law, stated in 1976 (Campbell, 1976), holds that the more a quantitative indicator is used for social decision-making the more it corrupts the process it was meant to monitor, and its home case is the test score. School quality sits medium to high on the axis; the score is cheap for the district to read and far from the outcome, which is what the child can do at twenty-five, and both of Goodhart's directions follow. The world is altered when the curriculum narrows to the tested items and untested subjects are dropped, which is the plantation; the report is altered when teachers correct the answer sheets, as in the Atlanta Public Schools conspiracy that ended in criminal convictions in 2015. Body counts in Vietnam were the metric of an attrition strategy under a defense secretary who had run a car company on numbers; the count was inflated, civilians were counted, and operations were chosen for the counts they would produce, so the world was bent toward the metric while the metric reported progress until the Tet offensive of 1968 delivered the verdict the count had stood in for. Police clearance rates and recorded crime decay by downgrading: felonies recorded as misdemeanors, reports discouraged at the desk, and in England and Wales an inspectorate found in 2014 that roughly a fifth of crimes reported to the police went unrecorded (HMIC, 2014). Citation counts and the h-index (Hirsch, 2005) grade the scientist on the number of times the work is cited, and science reshaped itself toward the count in both directions at once: the least publishable unit and the self-citing cartel altered the world of what gets written, and the paper mill altered the report, which is the retraction wave Chapter 15 records. The peer review case of Chapter 12 explains why the count was chosen: reading the paper is expensive and counting its citations is free, which is Scott's cheapness again, chosen inside a system with no state in it.

The symmetry rule requires the market instance, and the market supplies the purest ones, because a firm is a generator that can be graded on one number by people who will never see the firm. Quarterly earnings are the yield table of the listed company. Graham, Harvey, and Rajgopal's survey of chief financial officers (2005) found that a large majority would sacrifice long-term value to smooth a quarter, which is the forester's choice exactly, the world altered to hit the table; and the report can be altered too, as General Electric's settlement with the SEC in 2009 over its accounting and Enron's collapse in 2001 show, the latter ending when cash, which unlike earnings cannot be smoothed, delivered the verdict. Wells Fargo's cross-selling target produced roughly 3.5 million accounts that customers had not asked for, the world altered (accounts opened) and the report altered (customers invented) in a single operation, exposed by a newspaper in 2013 (Los Angeles Times) and sanctioned by regulators in 2016, election/press firing three years before the captured domestic verifier. The credit score is the lender's yield table for a borrower, a Normalbaum of repayment, and the borrower optimizes against it by timing balances, buying a place as an authorized user on a stranger's account, and paying firms to dispute true records; the 2008 case of Chapter 12 is the same substitution one level up, where a rating replaced a bond.
